THE EGG STANDARD.

The consumer unconsciously plays an import- ant part in fixing the standard for the first- grade egg. Popular prejudice may not always be supported bS,- scientific fact, but, as the con- sumer is ultimately responsible for the demand, the producer will find it to his advantage to study public requirements carefully and en- deavour to meet them as far as lies in his power.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
A BUSY HATING PERIOD.

All the available eg ;3 should be hatched dur- ing the present moi,Lh, whether the chickens therefrom are wanted for laying next winter, for selling for stock purposes, or for marketing as spring chickens. The one exception to this rule is in the case of some of the laying or non- sitting varieties, which are very rapid growers, and which consequently, if hatched now, would commence laying during the summer months, when eggs are plentiful and cheap. Probably, however, some eggs will be required during the present month for marketing, in order to main- tain the regular supply, and those from the non- sitting varieties are the ones that should be chosen. Table fowls should be brought out during February, so that they may have plenty of time in which to develop in order to partici- pate in the high prices ranging during the spring. The general-purpose varieties do not commence to lay till they are about seven or eight months old, and thus, in-order that they may begin laying in'the autumn,. the eggs must be set some time during the present month.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
FEATHER EATING.

In many poultry yards the vice of feather eating is very prevalent indeed, and very often does this occur about the-present time, when on so many days the weather is bad and the birds merely stand idly about. Some authorities on parasitic life have put feather-picking don to a parasite, and many different remedies have been suggested. I have very minutely examined fowls affected in this way—both those that pick their own feathers and also those that pick the feathers of other birds. I think that lam quite justified in saying that for every bird that picks its own feathers there must be a hundred that pick feathers from other birds. This, to my way of thinking, seems to do away with the parasite theory. No doubt in some cases it is caused by a parasite, but there are hundreds of such birds upon yrhich no trace of insects can be found. Very little appears to be known about this vicious habit—probably this is a more cor- rect term than disease. It is very often claimed as the reason for this habit that the birds are not getting animal food; the cause is also given that they are getting too much animal food, thus causing the body to become too heated; lack of grit, and no green food, are all given as the raison d'etre for this ditigusting habit, which absolutely spoils the appearance of the whole pen. lam inclined to•think that it is generally caused by sheer idleness, and this idea is strengthened by the fact that the majority of such cases are found among fowls that are kept in close confinement. I have tried the various remedies for rubbing into the feathers, but, I am bound to admit, with a very small amount of success.—E. T. Brown in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

MILK SOLD AT A LOSS.

The increased cost of milk production was referred to at the meeting of the Council of the Central and Associated Chambers of Agricul- ture, at the Surveyors' Institute, Westminster, when a rePort by the Dairy Products Com- mittee was adopted expressing the opinion that, with the present and increasing price of feed- ing stuffs, milk sold under contract was in many cases being produced at a loss. Mr.' YoUng (Nantwich Farmers' Club) declared that in Cheshire the dairy farmers were now producing milk, under a twelve months' contract, at a loss They recognised that at the present time, owing to the increased cost of foodstuffs, they must bear the loss, but they looked forward to increased prices, when they made the fresh con- tracts.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TRAINING OF THE LABOURER.

Among the many problems now engaging the attention of persons interested in the prosperity of rural districts, perhaps there is none of more importance at the present time than the pro- vision of some form of education- and training suited to the requirements of the labourer, in particular the farm labourer. From all parts of the country we hear domplaints from farmers of the great scarcity 4f skilled farm labour, and the statement is often made that the ris- ing generation do not possess the skill and handiness of the labourers of former years. This is due, to a certain extent, to the bookish form of teaching which is now adopted in our elementary schools. It may be said with con- fidence that a young labourer who has gained the latest information, and who takes a pride in his work, whether it be horses, cattle, sheep, pigs, or in any branch of farm work, would never need to seek employment, as farmers would only be too pleased to obtain the services of a good man they could rely on. Such a young man, too, would very naturally be full of

ambition, hoping in time to rise to the post of farm foreman or bailiff. This is as it should be, and would show his best skill, ar d so give satis- faction to his employer. -4 Farmer's Wife in "Farm and Home:"

===== 0002601_19150213_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
R.A.S.E. SHOW AT NOTTINGHAM.

The Royal Agricultural Society of England has now issued the prize sheet for live stock, poultry, produce, implements, &c., at the seventy-sixth annual show of the society, to be held at Nottingham from Tuesday, June 29th, to Saturday, July 3rd. The total 'value of the prizes offered (inclusive of champion prizes, special prizes and medals) is £9,000, of which £2,454 are contributions from various breed societie4 and £283 froni other sources.

Where in any class of horses, cattle, sheep and pigs (unless otherwise stated) only one prize is offered, and three different exhibitors make entries, a second prize will be given; where six different exhibitors, a third prize; where ten different exhibitors a fourth prize, and where fifteen different exhibitors make entries, a fifth prize.

In view of the exceptiOnal circumstances pre- trailing it has been found impossible to include classes and prizes for driving horses and jump- ing competitions, but should favourable condi- tions prevail later the Council will consider the advisability of offering prizes for these sections, in which case particulars will be issued in a separate schedule.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
GARTONS' ADVICE.

In a tastefully' atranged and capitally printed catalogue just issued by Messrs. Gartons, Ltd., the well-known seed experts, of Warrington, a plea is made for agriculturists to sow additional acreages of cereals' this spring, Messrs. Gartons adding that a larger cultivation of those will add to the wealth of our country and make us less dependent upon other nations for our food supplies. This publication contains much that is of interest to all those engaged in agricul- tural pursuits, the illustrations and descriptions being of a very original character. The catalogue deals with new and regenerated breeds of farm seeds for 1915, and speaks con- cisely of the abundance of crops produced as the result of the methods for which this firm have a world-wide fame. Reports upon experiments, testimonials as to excellence, and illustrations of specimens marvellous for purity and perfec- tion, make up a most attractive book for the farming community.

Copies, we understand, can be obtained from the head offices of Messrs. Gartons, Ltd., at Warrington.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

SOIL CULTIVATION.

It is an unfortunate fact that to many ama- teur gardeners soil cultivation spells working in the autumn, winter or spring, as the case may be, and nothing more. They appear to think that as long as they then dig deeply and thoroughly and manure the ground intelligently according to the crop that is to be grown upon it, they have done all that is necessary for the benefit of the plants. This, however, is very far from being the real truth of the case. As a matter of fact, the probabilities are that the man who is not quite so thorough in his initial operations, but persists in soil cultivation throughout the entire year, will achieve more gratifying results than he who works well .in the preliminary stages and slacks off badV; later on. It can be asserted without the small- est fear of contradiction that there is not one successful gardener in this country who does not persist in surface loosening from the time that the crops are first put out until they are harvested. It is true that they may differ as to methods; all, however, are convinced not merely of the desirability, but of the necessity or the practice.—" One and All Gardening."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
WINTER GREENS.

If the recent very heavy rains, literally flood- ing the soil with moisture and causing all the cabbage tribe to be gross and sappy, should, as is not at all unlikely, be followed by severe frost, then great injury will result. Although it is constantly advised that the ground for such things as borecoles, savoys, coleworts, and such like should be firm, how often do we see such planted in richly manured, loose soil that mgenders a sappy growth, which, in the event of severe frost, is sure to perish.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
BROAD BEANS.

If seed was not sown in the autumn, then when the weather has improved and the soil has become dry enough to deal with it, a sowing of these may be made. At this season one of the early long-pod varieties is the best. If, however, a small, delicately flavoured bean is desired. Beck's Dwarf Green Gem should be selected. Broad beans always do best in a fairly heavy soil. Growers who have a light soil to deal with should make the same firm before sowing. The rows, unless in the case of the very dwarf kinds, should be at least 2ift. apart, and a warm, sheltered position is neces- sary at this date.

about well-chosen, well-made mourning. Neat- ness and suitable materials are all important. The fabric for a widow, or near relative, should have a dull surface, and the dress should be trimmed liberally with crape. No other trim- ming is permissible, beside this ,emblem of deep Mourning, lace and embroideries being quite out of place. Relief is obtained only by means of the snow-white muslin collar and the cuffs, but the latter are not now obligatory. Black furs are de regueur, but exception is sometimes made in favour of sables. A tiny white 'waistcoat is sometimes introduced in place of the white col- lar when the widow is quite young.

Half Mourning. Diamonds and pearls may be worn with dis- cretion, but black bordered handkerchiefs have long been banished to the limbo of things for- biddeti. Needless to say, footwear, hosiery, handbags, umbrellas, and all accessories of the toilette must be of the same sombre hue as the raiment. As in dress, so in millinery, no material having a shiny surface should be com- bined With crape. The silks used for headgear must therefore be dull, but rich. Elderly,women wear plain bonnets of crape and silk, but the young generally choose round hats of the toque type. Veils may be short or long. In the former case they are worn over the face, and are edged with Crape, in the latter they form the trimming of the hat, or partly so, and fall in rich folds down the back. For half-mourning the fashionable striped shirts and blouses in black and white silk are very chic, especially when donned with simple white muslin collars and a black tie. Fashionable Footwear.

Hosiery and footwear will be of special im- portance this spring, on account of the short- ness of skirts. The latter will permit of perfect ease of movement, but at the same time a. cer- tain amount of stoCking. will inevitably be visible above the shoes. Dainty hosiery, of which there is an unlimited supply, will be in great demand. Shoes and boots have of late as- sumed a different form, and the new models are fashioned on Nature's lines: Pegtop heels and slender toes have vanished, and the broad, short shoe has returned to favour. This shape make§ for comfort, although at first the feet look per- haps broad and thick. ,Walking shoes have bars and straps which keep them secure. Manufac- turers are dyeing leather in all the modish colours, therefore shoes may be bought that match exactly all prettily tinted afternoon, evening or rest gowns.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Duchess of Brunswick.

• _ Not very happy is the present position ,of the once gay and light-hearted (laughter of the Kaiser. Throughout her childhood and girl- hood she is said never to have known pain or sorrow, but: those who have seen her recently say she is only the shadow of her former self. Married to a prince whom she sincerely loved, she began her wedded life with every prospect of happiness, but the war has brought 'her both anxiety and grief. Her husband, the Duke of Brunswick, has been ill for months, and is now only slowly recovering from a serious operation. Her youngest brother and chief playfellow dur- ing her childhoo(T, is seriously ill; her child's health also gives her great anxiety, and she felt keenly the death of her favourite cousin, Prince Max oi Hesse. Beyond these, there are sorrows in the Kaiser's family that so far -have been concealed from the world, but which this Prin- cess naturally shares.

The Order of Leopold for Lady Dorothy

The honour bestowed by the King of the Bel- gians on Lady Rerothy Fielding has been well merited. Ever since August of last year Lady Dorothy and her assistants have been with the soldiers, rendering the aid they so sorely need. Neither the bombarding of towns, nor the ter- rible wintry weather daunted these brave women, whose work has been so thoroughly ap- preciated by officers and men. The Order of Leopold, which King Albert personally bestowed upon Lady Dorothy, is the highest Belgian mili- tary honour. •

Japanese Doctors and Nurses for the Front. A number of Japanese doctors and nurses have arrived from the Far East, on their way to the Front, where they intend to offer their services to the Allied Armies. The nurses, who are all members of the Red Cross Society, and twenty- five in number, were received by Queen Alexan- dra, who, as all the world knows, has worked with tireless energy for our soldiers and sailors ever since the war broke out. HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Renovating Cloth.

Sage stewed for half an hour in water, and then strained into a bath, is splendid for renovating shabby black cloth and helps revive the colour.

Tea Stains.

Tea stains are removed from white dresses, tablecloths, &c., by covering with powdered salt somewhat thickly. The salt absorbs the stain and dampness from the article so stained.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
A Viennese Heroine.

In all time's, ancient and modern, women have joined the ranks of armies in the field, and fought side by side with men for longer or shorter periods, cleverly concealing their sex. The war that is now devastating Europe has had its heroines also. One of these, a well-known society favourite of Vienna, donned masculine attire and volunteered for the Front. On many occasions she distinguished herself for excep- tional bravery, courage.and resource, and after a signal instance of heroism, when fighting the Russians, her merit obtained for her a commis- sion in a crack regiment. But her career was soon brought to a disappointing termination, when, on presenting herself at headquarters, she was recognised, and sent back to• Vienna. The young lady was Fraulein Marie Weissman.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
Advance Fashions.

It is not easy in normal times to obtain trust- worthy :information regarding spring fashions as early as February. The war: increases- the difficulty tenfold. It is, however, safe to say that the narrow skirt will be no more seen in the 'world of fashion, but that it will quite dis- appear this spring is -unlikely. Theie will first be variations of it, then the skirt three yards in circumference at the hem will be generally worn. This width is considerably less than that of many tunics introduced last year. The materials chosen for the new full skirts are particularly soft and yielding, and there are no foundations to give additional weight. Under- skirts, too, are no wider, and are made of the thinnest materials. In Paris and America the wide skirt has been almost universally adopted for evening wear, and to judge from its width, the crinoline may be an accomplished fact in the near, if not immediate, future.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
Minced Veal.

To make this white do not use any dark pieces of meat or any browned pieces; cut into squares and flour well. Mix a teacupful of cream, the juice of a lemon, a little finely-chopped peel, with salt and pepper to taste; put in the meat, and shake it well over the fire. Do not let it boil for more than a minute, or it will be hard.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
Grease Spots.

Grease spots on woollen or silk materials can be easily removed by being rubbed with a clean rag dipped into benzine, benzoline, or petrol, all of which, however, should be used during the day time, as they are highly inflammable. French chalk used to cover an oil spot in silk will very frequently remove it, but it has to be left on for some hours, so that the former method is preferable if in a hurry.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
Sweetbread Cutlets.

Parboil a pair of sweetbreads, after soaking in warm water. When cooked, press between two plates; cut them into the form or cutlets; have some aspic jelly, half melted; drop each cutlet into it; chop some aspic in small pieces; form a circle or it; arrange the cutlets on this; fill the centre with small lettuce leaves; pour mayonnaise round, and garnish with fancy shapes of jelly. Lentil Savoury.

Soak a pint of lentils overnight, boil until soft in just enough water to cover, grate ilb. cooking cheese, and mix with Ob. of brown breadcrumbs (crust may be used soaked in cold water, then squeezed out and beaten soft with a fork), add salt and pepper to taste, slice a pound of cooking tomatoes. Grease a pie dish and put in the bottom a layer of sliced tomatoes, then of the cheese and bread, then one of len- tils, repeat until the dish is full, having the cheese and bread at the top. Bake in a moder- ate oven until browned. Rice may be used in place of lentils.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Baked Fish.

A fish weighing four or five pounds is a good size to bake, and should not fie cut more than is necessary to clean nicely. Make a dressing of breadcrumbs, butter, salt, and a little salt pork, with chopped parsley- and onions if desired. Mix the dressing with one beaten egg. Fill the body, sew it up, and place in a dripper, with some strips of salt pork for flavouring. Put one .cup of water in the pan. Bake an hour and a half, basting frequently. Take up the fish and

===== 0002601_19150213_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE WOMAN IN BUSINESS.

She Learns Much Which Does Not Lessen Her Natural Charm.

Women in business learn many things which women who do not work should know, but which they have absolutely no chance of learn- ing. Hence, when it comes to really knowing men the girl who has never worked with them is a mere tyro hn.! novice compared with the womai!h. has been in In Einess with them. The girl who na never been in business labours under a severe handicap when it comes to deal- ing wit n uken

One of the great and grand and beautiful things that a woman in business learns is to abandon the tricks that, from time immemorial, women have used to eimquer men. She learns that she can cut out whims and weaknesses and baby play without lessening the charm or power of her sex one whit. She rejects the doctrine of the mid-Victorian era that physical delicacy, headaches, womanly deficiencies, smell- ing salts and sofas are the things that men most . admire in women She finds out that health and ability ,o p.:vfo:'nf her daily task what counts. So she keeps herself in much better physical condition than does her sister of the social life. 'II

The business woman cannot moan a day away nursing a toothache. So she keeps her teeth in good condition. Her efficiency in her work keeps her face calm and young and fair. She does not need to worry. Body and brain are kept in perfect order by the necessity of work. How often does the girl who has a good home and who does not have to work realise that em- ployment and exercise of brain and body is the best preservative good looks, youth and com- plexion can have? The business woman very rarely worries about business. She does not take her office cares home with her. She knows that men are younger after 30 than women, and she learns that women can keep as young as long as men can. She learns to know everything a man knows. Everything she learns is of use to her employer, but doubly so to herself. And with all this knowledge the business girl loses not one bit of that charm and sweetness which are the characteristics of the womanly woman the worlc! over.

is to use ATORA Beef Suet for frying fish, pota- toes, pancakes, &c. Makes them deliciously crisp and digestible. ATORA can be used over and over again. Ask your grocer for ATORA (in blocks) and refuse substitutes.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information in Popular

Language.

Pimples.

You should take a good tonic to purify the blood, as bad blood is usually the cause of pim- ples. Then wet the places affected with the fol- lowing: Rose water, three ounces; sulphate of zinc, one dram; mix and after allowing it to remain on a few seconds dry it gently with a soft linen towel and then touch the 'Zees over. with cold cream which also gently dry off.

No Lazy Children. Not long ago itowas the practice summarily to divide children into good and bad, stupid and clever. Now science, having looked into the matter, has come to the conclusion that children should be divided into the sick or the well, the hungry or the fed, the weak or the strong. This may sound queer to those who still advocate the spare-the-rod-and-spoil-the- child idea along with other superstitions be- loved of our grandparents. But it's a fact that there is a wonderful relation between mind and body, stomach and brain, whether you take any stock in all this modern nonsense about children" or not. Both in this country and in America certain men and women who think have been making investigations with results which ought to revolutionise child education.

Bright, If Normal. One fact they discovered—and proved—is that there is no such thing as a " lazy " child. The normal child is interested, alert, bright-minded, full of waking curiosities about the world into which he has come, and endowed with an al- most unquenchable physical vivacity. What then is the matter with the little inert, stupid, dull-eyed creatures for ever at the bottom of the class, who never put a question or seem to pass a given point?

The matter is that they are ill. Some of them are hungry, and it is as hopeless to expect a child to study well when he needs food as it would be to expect a fire that needs fuel to burn well. The hunger may be due to an actual lack of food or it may be that the teeth are in such poor condition that the child cannot chew and therefore does not digest. Or it may be that the food offered to it at home is entirely un- suited to its needs.

Hindrances to Remove. Some of the children cannot hear well and some cannot see. They do not complain because they do not know what is the matter. They accept constant rebuke and defeat with the dumb mystification of ignorance. Many of them have never drawn one natural breath since their birth owing to the presence of enlarged tonsils and adenoids.

These children can never compete with their fellows until all these hindrances have been re- moved. Some recent investigations made by a French physician show that among well-nour- ished children only 19 per cent. have physical defects, while among the poorly nourished over 60 per cent. suffer from these defects. We are trying to cope with these established facts by medical inspection and feeding in necessitous cases of our schoel children.

COMPETITION: TOWN OR COUNTRY? My DEAR CHILDREN,-

Another competition! That was the idea which struck me at the beginning of this week. I was looking round for a subject upon which you would be able to pen me another nice set of essays, when along came a very interest- ing letter from my little niece, Dorothy E. Bennett.

For it I thank Dorothy heartily, because it solved my problem for me. I will tell you how. Dorothy lives at Stockton Heath, and has grown very fond of the country. And so she told me, "It is not nice in the town, but it is nice at Stockton Heath.", So for .the competi- tion I want you. each to write an essay on, `'Which is better to live in, town or country? And why?' The closing date will be -Monday, the first day of next month. Mind we have a good entry I:

Your loving UNCLE NED

" WAR " NAMES FOR BABIES.

A few weeks ago, there was amongst the wounded soldiers in Warrington Private John James Williams, of the South Lancashire Regi- ment. lie fought and was wounded in the Battle of Mons, was invalided home, and recov- ered in this town, from which he again went to the Front. '

His baby girl was born on November Btl3, at his home, 1, Pleasant View, Cymmer, Port Talbot, Glamorgan, and as her father had fought where he did, she was called "Ypres Louvain Williams." Don't you think." Ypres" a -pretty name? ' Such " war" names Eire at present quite fashionable. In Leeds, for instance, the records of a single week include: William Aisne, Vera Louvain, Mary Argonne, Thomas Liege, William Mons, Arthur Marne, Herbert Jellicoe, William Thomas 3-offre, .John French, and half a dozen Kitcheners

===== 0002601_19150213_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
MOST POPULAR NAMES.

Whilst we are talking about names, it is in- teresting to note that someone has been making out a list of 6,650 males and 6,250 females, whose names appeared in the "Glasgow Herald" mar- riage and death column for last year. Amongst all those males, there were only 257 Christian names (of which 134 came only once each); and the ladies had 261 Christian names (157 occur- ring only once). Every year, too, the use of surnames as Christian names is becoming more common.

As usual, John (1,158) outran all the other names. Then James (947) was followed by Wil- liam, Robert, Alexander, Thomas, George David, Andrew, Archibald, Charles, Hugh, Henry, and Peter (each 103). Of the rarer Bible names there were Abel, Enoch, Gideon, Samson, Amos, and 10 Ebenezers. Of the women, Margaret retarns pride of place with 727, though Mary, 677, for some time led the field. Then come Elizabeth, Jean and Jane, Annie, Agnes, Janet, Isabel, Catherine, Helen, Jessie, Christina (135), Marion (110). Of more or less vain efforts to please male relatives one notes Archina, An- drewina, Hectorina, Davidina, Neilina, Peterina, Jacobina, and Isaacina.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITE MAN AND SNAKE.

This is a Hottentot fable. A white man met a snake, upon whom a large stone had fallen and covered her, so that she could not rise. The white man lifted the stone off the snake, but when he had done so she wanted to bite him.

"Stop !" said the man. "Let us first go to some wise peqple, and see if it is right that you should bite me."

They went to the hyena, and the white man asked: "18 it right that the snake should want to bite me, though I helped her when she lay under a stone, and could not rise?" The hyena (thinking he would get a Share of the white man's body) replied: "If you were bitten, what would it matter?" Then the snake wanted to bite the white man, but he said: "Wait a little. Let us go now to other wise people, that I may be certain whether this is right." They went and met the jackal, to whom the white man put his question. The jackal an- swered: "I do not lielieve that the snake could be covered with a stone and be unable to rise. Unless I see it myself, I would not believe it. Therefore let us go back to the stone and see whether it can be true."

They returned then to the place where it had happened. The jackal said: "Snake, lie down, and let thyself be covered with the stone." The snake then let the white man lay the stone upon her, and although she tried ler hardest she could not rise. Said the white man: "She cannot rise. Let me now release her again." But the jackal stopped him, exclaiming: "Do not lift the stone! She wanted to bite you, therefore, let her raise herself."

And they both went their way, leaving the snake beneath the stone as a reward for her ingratitude.

SONG OF THE SOLDIER. (BY JESSIE Sndm). Oh! to be a soldier, now the war is here. Fighting for my freedom, never knowing fear. Wife? I have none. Sweetheart? She is gone before . . . Oh! to be a soldier. Who could wish for more?

Rifle to my shoulder, marching with the men, Long I for the conflict—though we're one to

ten. Should I lose my birthright? Dare Ibe

afraid? No I'll up and at them. Where's my trusty blade?

Do not go and tell me some have stayed at home, Letting us fight for them o'er the briny foam! Well, I s'pose they can do; we mustThear the

more, But in point of honour—that is where we score.

Oh! to be a soldier, fighting for the Crown. When the war is over, time to settle down . . . Rushing o'er the trenches, fighting with the

men— All for dear old England—Life's worth living then

PATRIOTIC PUPILS.

Secondary Schools Raise More Money

For War Funds.

The pupils of Forms 111. and IV. of the Secondary School arranged a successful enter- tainment last Saturday afternoon in aid of the War Funds; many parents and friends assem- bling in the school gymnasium to enjoy the varied items on the programme. The first part of the entertainment included recitation "The Rat and the Mouse," by Ethel Leigh; a song, "Down Vauxhall," by Gertrude Johnson; a monologue, "Defective Memory," splendidly rendered by Willing; a con- certed number, "Smiling Geisha," by L. Hough- ton, G. Johnson, M. Newall, 0. Boothroyd, B. Ashton and S. Cross; a dance by 0. Boothroyd (which was generously 'applauded), and a song, "England,' by Sophie Cross. A.. Dickens' sketch, "The Micawbers Dining with David CoppeOfield," showed a taste for acting on the part of the young pupils, the •ha...acters ITing taken by R. Tole (Copperfield), A. Spence (Micawber), H. Lloyd (Traddles), F. Davies. (Lattimer), A. Broadhurst (Mrs. Micawber), D. Broom (Landlady), and M. Newall (Maid). After the refreshment interval, the pupils gave a charming exhibition of living whist, the " cards" being designed and executed in colours under the superintendence of Miss Clark and Miss Morris. • The humorous chorus "Sister Susie" concluded the programme. The proceeds, amounting to £9 3s„ will be divided between Queen Mary's Work-for-Women Fund and the Belgian Fund for Distressed Pro- fessional Men and Women.

The staff and pupils o+ . the Secondary .3chool have now -!ollected the sr.rn of .RBB towards he various War Funds, and this total will be fur- ther increased by a bazaar to be held at Easter. Contributions from old pupils Will be grate- fully acknowledged by the senior mistress (Miss Clark).

Mr. Roger Parr's Gift.

The Council meeting of the above society was held on Monday, the President (Sir Gilbert Greenall) in the chair. It was decided to hold the next annual show on Thursday, August sth. Mr. Joihua Ball was unanimously re-elected chairman of the Council and Mr. William Weir vice-chairman. Mr. Alfred Broome was re 6 elected honorarfy treasurer and Messrs. Bennett and Bennett, auditors.

The final consideration of the prize schedule and selection of judges was left over until -a later meeting. Considerable discussion took Place as'to the best means of securing new mem- bers, and it was decided that each member of the Council should endeavour to get a list of new namis and put them forward for member- ship at the next meeting.

The Chairma*announced that the Vice-Presi- dent, Mr. Rtbger C. Parr, had kindly intimated his intention to give £5O towards the prize fund for this year. The members of the Council in- structed the Secretary to write Mr. Roger Parr It letter expressing their sincere thanks for his munificent donation.

PANCAKE DAY, 1915.

In view of the near approach of Shrove 'Tues- day, the following recipe for Pancakes should be found useful :

Take half a pound of flour, one teaspoonful of Borwick's Baking Powder, and a little salt, MiX well in a dry state, add one egg and three-quar- ters of a pint of milk. Fry quickly over a brisk fire and serve immediately. Pancakes made according to these directions will turn out beautifully light and appetising. Borwick's Baking Powder, besides being ad- mittedly the best, is also most economical to use, one teaspoonful to every half-pound of flour 'being quite sufficient to ensure the most deli- cious Pancakes.

Its contents are varied and instructive, and it is profusely illus- trated.

Cordially Welcome a New Member.

ELECTIONS "AS USUAL."

The business for transaction at the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians, held in the Workhouse on Wednesday morning, was principally of a routine character. Mr. W, C. Brooks was voted to the chair, the Chair- man, Mr. A. J. Jolley, being away in London attending a Poor Law Conference.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW MEMBER WELCOMED.

Mr. Brooks, on behalf of the Board, then gave a hearty welcome to the newly-elected member of the Board, Mr. W. J. Shaw, who topped the poll in the recent by-'llecti3n in the Bewsey Ward. Mr. Brooks wished Mr. Shaw continued health, so that he might be enabled to discharge his 'duties as a Guardian ably and efficiently. He hoped that the new member's association with the Board would be a pleasant one. (Hear, bear.)

Miss Broadbent, joining in the welcome, ob- served that they had too many who were nominally members of the Board, but who t-mk little or no interest in their work.

Other members having spoken, Mr. Shaw re- -turned thanks for the cordial welcome accorded him, and declared that, with the co-operation of the other members, be would endeavour to do his work to the satisfaction of the Board and the ratepayers generally.

CONCERNING ROMAN CATHOLIC

CHILDREN.

The Padgate Cottage Homes Committee re- ported that at a meeting on February 9th they considered the letters forwarded to the Board by the Rev. Father Pennington, of the Roman Catholic Certified Schools, Liverpool. and the Rev. Father Whittle, of Warrington, relative to the proposal of the Guardians to erect new buildings at the Homes to accommodate 'certain classes of children, under the provisions of the Poor Law Institutions Order, 1913. The com- munications urged the Guardians to take into consideration, as an alternative to the building proposals, the question of sending the Roman Catholic children to the certified schools under the control of the Archbishop of. Liverpool. A full discussion on the points at issue took place and it was finally resolved to adjourn the fur- ther consideration of the point involved until the next meeting of the committee. The report was, adopted.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====

It was recommended' by the Padgate Homes Committee that the Rev. H. I. Wadeson, rector of Croft, be asked to continue to act as chaplain temporarily to the end of the quarter, and that, in the interval, the Guardians would be pleased if the Bishop could nominate Mr. Wadeson for the office permanently.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
CHATEAU TOURLAVILLE.!

The Anglo-French Hospital at Cherbourg. _,_

MISS TRUDA CROSFIELD'S SPHERE.

Mrs. Eva McLaren writes an interesting article in the current number of "The Liberal Women's Review" regarding the work at the Anglo-French Hospital, Cherbourg, at which Miss Truda Crosfield is giving her valuable ser- vices as nurse and interpreter—and, as a recent letter in "The Examiner " went to prove, as chauffeur. We extract the following from Mrs. McLaren's communication The chateau of Tourlaville is a beautiful old sixteenth century building, lying on rising ground which slopes towards the bay and com- mands extensive views over the town and har- bour of Cherbourg. It was not exactly built for a hospital; but it is wonderful what can be done in the way of adaptation where the good will and energies of some thirty-five or forty people are concentrated on the task. INDESCRIBABLE QUAYSIDE SCENES.

The Warning after our arrival was bright and still. We were up at seven, and off at eight to the Arsenal Quay to give any help we could to the wounded coming off the hospital ships, and to get our own men into the motor ambulances and cars (we have three of each) for the chateau. The vessel, a huge British India boat, was already alongside, and another just coming into harbour. ,We stood by the gang- way and watched the men being brought down on stretchers, or helped between two soldiers to the landing stage. • Our .cars were alongside, aril Dr. Mabel Ramsay took charge of four very serious cases and ten others, which Were motored off at once to our hospital. • The scene on the quay was indescribable, but for all time the picture of the gigantic troopship with her freight of 780 wounded men, will live in one's memory. Down the gangway they were borne in an endless stream, the worst cases to be placed side by side on stretchers on the landing stage, waiting to be taken off to the base hos- pitals, whether our own or those in the town itself; others, rather less severely wounded, carried one by one to the long trains which were in waiting to convey them on a journey of four, sight, or even ten hours, to some hospital in a distant town. I talked to many of them and soon found they were suffering terribly from thirst. The Femmes de France had organised a tea supply in the sailors' kitchen on the quay, and we got this going while one of the nurses and I procured a great can of water and took it round to the men. The voyage from Dunkirk had lasted eighteen hours, and some of the men had been wounded as long as a week previously. All had of course been bandaged, or put into temporary splints, but their dressings were in a bad way, and many of the men were exhausted. •

===== 0002601_19150213_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
WORLD GONE MAD?

When we had done all we could for the men here we went to the further quay where the wounded—not such serious cases on the whole-- were coming off the Second hospital ship-960 in aIY. I was giving them water as they were helped along when a young fellow thanked me in broken English, and said he had been so fortunate as to be looked after by an English doctor and two English nurses at Dunkirk, and they had been so wonderfully good to .him. Then he added: "1 have a bad head wound, but they tell me I can be at the front again in a month." Just .then a Frenchman tame up to me and said that a man who was badly wounded needed to have his dressing attended to, as it had been displaced and he was suffer- ing very much. My Frenchman . introduced himself as the head of the Cherbourg branch of the 'Red Cross, and Dr. Ramsay promptly volunteered to see to the dressing. We went, down a trainload Of 500 men but could not find him, and then our guide took us into the great shed, where the wounded soldiers who can sit up are sent to wait and rest until they can be dispatched to the hospitals. A third ship had come in meantime, and some 1,800 men were _sitting and lying about in that shed till accom- modation could be found for them.

What can one say about it all? Here in his tiny corner, remote from the fighting area, one sees a glimpse of what is going on in every port and town in France, Belgium, Germany, and Russia, not to mention gallant little Serbia. That shed, and those 1,800 brave, patient, enduring soldiers make one wonder if anybody is sane, and if all the world has not gone mad. Well, we found our wounded man, and tidied him up as best we could. All he could say as the poor shattered hand was dressed was a few touching words of apology: "I am too dirty for you to touch, but it isn't easy to keep clean in the trenches."

When our stores were exhausted we found our way back to the chateau, where our first job was to see to the soldiers' clothes as they were stripped off and th,e patient got into bed. This is work which brings the battlefield very near—such a tale is told by the tattered, mud- stained, blood-drenched clothes, and the con- tents of those pockets, filled as they are with a medly of fragments of shell, stray bullets, cartridges, buttons from an enemy's coat, let- ters frolu home, a purse, a rosary, pipes, and tobacco. Our men, nbw seventy-five in number, are, with the exception of four or five Belgians, Frenchmen from every part of the country, and they come to us for the most part from the trenches in the neighbourhood of Ypres, Dix- made, and La Bassee. The wounds are ter- rible—comminuted fractures of arms, legs, and jaws, deep yawning rents caused by fragments of shell, or by the so-called "humane" bullet which has ploughed up the soft tissues lying between the points of entry and exit, carrying in with it shreds of clothing or—most to be dreaded of all—particles of soil which may mean, sooner or later, an attack of tetanus.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
NO "CLEAN" WOUNDS.

With us, as in all the other hospitals over this fair land of France, there ,is practically no such thing as a clean wound to be met with—everywhere sepsis reigns supreme until hot fomentations and free drainage come to the aid of struggling nature and bring order out of chaos. Surgically the cases are often as bad as they can be, but life in the trenches, hard though it is, seems wonderfully healthy, • and once they have recovered from the fatigue of the journey the men are able to eat and sleep well for the most part, and are always ready to sing or whistle or enjoy a joke. The daily paper is looked for with great eagerness, and every step in the campaign, whether on the eastern or western front, is keenly discussed. There is no questioning the fact that the patients are really happy, and they do not deny us the satisfaction of knowing it. When the time conies for passing on else- where and vacating the much-needed bed ex- pressions of the most • genuine gratitude are never wanting, and "good-bye" is the bardest word in the world to say, even though France has done her best to cancel out the sadness of it by her cheery "au revoir" ! That the French military authorities are im- pressed with the good work already accom- plitihed is evident from the fact that they have offered us tlke Gare Maritime, a building situ- ated on the quay at Cherbourg, which is well adapted to the work of a hospital. Here we hope to have an additional sixty beds. Our committee at 39, St. James's-street is arranging for the necessary increase of staff, and we are endeavouring to raise the .t 4.50 which will be needed to equip the operating theatre, and provide an X-ray apparatus. Towards this sum A:100 has already been promised, and we are confident that our friends will rally round us and enable us to make the most of our new opportunity. So much has already been done by the members of the Liberal Women's Suffrage Union that I find it impossible to express my gratitude both for the liberal con- tributions in money and the welcome gifts of clothes. I am sure every giver would feel amply rewarded if she could see the pleasure I per- sonally experience in receiving and dispensing what is sent, and the intense appreciation dis- played by the recipients.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 13, 1915. THE MAYOR'S REGRET.

His Worship Confides in Wesley

Guild Members.

INSPIRING ANNUAL FESTIVAL

Inspiring addresses and beautiful music marked the annual festival of the Bewsey-road Wesley Guild, which took place on Sunday and Monday and was attended with conspicuous success. The ,Rev. Jas. W. Dinsdale (of Shef- field) occupied the' pisulpit at the morning and evening services, whilst in the afternoon the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) presided over a well-attended musical service, rendered by the choir. The speaker was the Rev. Wm. Reid. THE MAYOR'S "LOSS."

The Mayor said it was to him a very great pleasure to do what little service- he could to help forward the Wesley Guild .belonging to that church. He had very pleasant recollec- tions of belonging to that church many years ago. He regretted he had never joined the guild at that time; it had been his loss. He knew the good work which the guild was doing amongst the young people; it fostered a spirit among them which must have a lasting benefit. And, notwithstanding the fact that so many of their members were now taking part in the great war, he was glad to know that their num- bers had still been maintained.

Those guilds brought forth young men and women who could obtain• spiritual comfort, joy, and blessing in such times as they were now passing through. He trusted that the guild would continue to prosper, and that the work it was doing would increase manifold.

"WORKERS' INADEQUATE RETURNS!'

In the course of his address, the Rev. Wm. Reid Said he-would like to give them a word of encouragement. One could not help being struck by the fact that, notwithstanding one's efforts, there was nothing like an adequate return for the energy and enthusiasm put forth from time to time. Really one could not help feeling that, for, he was bound to confess it, oite did not see anything like a reward. They asked themselves: "Why should there .not be it better return?" He felt almost convinced that one of the rea- sons was that compromise was far too much in evidence—that was to say, the desire to stand well in anything that might be in the fashion, Aven when it was not good or lofty, was apt to be paramount. He thought that if they were wise they would leave alone those. things which did not appear righteous. If they got returns here, they might depend upon it that they were of that order which, would not stand. Of course when Jesus used the word " world " one could not conceive that He had Meant any region which Could be defined by geographical limits. They looked about the world of nature in which they lived, and it was a beautiful world. God was anxiously concerned for the benefit of every man—" He so loved the world —! " And even when that world steeped itself in tears there were compensations. That was true to life itself. Then if there was a thing in their lives which did not savour of God, let them cut it out, no matter what it cost, for otherwise it cost life!

===== 0002601_19150213_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL CONTRIBUTIONS.

The ladies and gentlemen who during the afternoon contributed solos and duets were Mrs. C. H. Winstanley, , Madame Sara Silcock, the Misses M. and V. Jones, and Messrs. A. Baker, W. Dutton, S. Owen; Q. Sinclair, and J. Turton.

The choir rendered two anthems in excellent. style.

The Rev. H. Williamson also took part in the service.

GLIMPSES OF BAHAMIAN LIFE.

On Monday ,evening the Rev. J. W. Dinsdale lectured to a numerous and interested audience on "Glimpses of Bahamian Life and scenery." in the main his lecture dealt with life on the island of Andros, which forms one of the Bahamas. Mr. J. G. Withinshaw was in the chair. Having recounted many amusing inci- -dents which befel him in the Bahamas, and particularly on the island of Andros, the lec- turer dwelt upon the lonely life that had to be experienced by the missionaries. He pictured the difficulties: confronting the messengers of the Gospel on the, outposts of civilisation, and said that the black natives of the Bahama group not only desired to be instructed, but also to be helped, guided, and controlled just in the way that children were. Their anxieties and fears were intensified by reason of the fact, that circumstances had made them superstitious. They devoutly wished that they had been born white, and women of the islands had been known to powder their faces, possibly in the belief that the pigment would nd in making them as their over-seas neighbours. The great problem in the Bahamas—as in many another mission field—was the colour problem, and he ventured to think that the fact that the black was patient and could take a kick without kicking back again would ultimately result in his winning a place alongside the white. The black possessed a great sense of humour, and that, combined with his mother wit, led to the belief that the backward would not always be .baCkward, and that ultimately the colour ques- tion would be settled along the lines of affinity. Ultimately the isles of the .Southern Sea would .come together and take their place in the civilised world, and, as a prelude along the right lines, it was a matter for thankfulness that so many of the islanders had already learned to subscribe to the belief that "the kingdoms of this world should become the Kingdoms of Christ." During the evening the Misses M. and V. Jones sang the duet "0 Divine Redeemer."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
GHOST IN THE BEDROOM.

Warringtonian's Weird Nocturnal Experience.

At a meeting held in the Masonic Hall on Tuesday night a young Warringtoniah re- counted an eerie experience which befel him at the "witching hour of midnight" on Sunday. He related the experience in the form of a ques- tion to a Swedenborgian lecturer who had been dilating upon the mystery of death. Said the young man: "I should like to recount an ex- perience that I passed through on Sunday night. Just before twelve o'clock I lay awake in bed; I had not been to sleep. All at once there was a rapping, and a form passed my bed. It was the form of a human—a man— though I could not see his face. To me it seemed a form as human as it possibly could be. I shivered, in fact I have hardly finished shivering yet; and then I coughed, and the form at once vanished. Before I coughed, how- ever, the form had passed right round my bed. I am not saying this because I am merely thinking that I had this experience. I really had it. I got up and rubbed my eyes—l was fully awake—and it is impossible to get the thing out of my mind. My trousers were hang- ing on a peg behind the bedroom door, and they distinctly flapped against the door as the form entered. This is not mere imagination; the thing is planted right there in my mind." "I should advise our friend,", said the lec- turer, "to strike a match and light the bed- room candle next time, and also to read a chapter of the Bible." "To tell you the truth," vouchsafed the inquirer, "I slept last night with the gas alight."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
fARLY CLOSING OF PUBLIC- HOUSES.

Licensees Appeal to the Bench at

Stockton Heath.

JUSTICES ADHERE TO ORDER.

The licensed victuallers in the Daresbury Petty Sessional Division of the County of Ches- ter=in which, among other townships, Stockton Heath is included—made an effort at the annual licensing sessions, held at Stockton Heath on Tuesday, to get the magistrates to withdraw their order under the Intoxicating Liquor (Tem- porary Restriction) Act, 1914, notice of the com- ing into force of which on the 9th inst. had been duly given. The effort was unavailing, and con- sequently the opening and closing hours for licensed houses .in the division for at least the next three months will be as follows: Week days, open at 9 a.m., close at 9 p.m.; Sundays, open at 12.30 p.m., close at 2.30 pm.; open again at 6.30 p.m., close at 9 p.m. Hitherto the hours of opening on week days have been from 6 a.m. to 10 p.m., and on Sundays from 12.30 to 2.30, and 6 to 10.

Those present on the Bench on Tuesday were: Messrs. F. A. Frost (chairman), R. C. Parr, Jas. Hepherd, C. Jackson Holmes, J. A. Ransome and T. Baxter, but it was intinutted that Mr. Parr refrained from adjudicating in the matter, though he remained an interested listener to the proceedings. .

BENCH ASKED TO RE-CONSIDER THEIR DECISION.

The application of the licensees was put for- ward IV Mr. E. A. White, solicitor, of War- rington, who began his address to the\Bench by explaining that he represented all the licensees in the Daresbury Division. He had been in- structed, he said, to ask their Worships to re- consider the matter of their recent Order, and to put before them a few grounds upon which, it was submitted, they should see their way to do so. He ,quite appreciated the fact that, by means of his application, lie was asking the Bench to revoke an Order which had only been recently made, and which actually only came into force that day; a course which, quite con- ceivably, they might regard as a bold one on his part. The new Intoxicating Liquor (Tempo- rary Restriction Act), 1914, provided that Orders under it might be made at the annual Licensing Sessions, and that was the first opportunity his clients had had of bringiig bcNre the justices their point of view •rith regard to the matter.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Publicans Say the Order is a Hardship.

He ventured to submit with confidence that flit; Bench would realise ihat the Order which the justices had issued inflicted a very great hardship on the licence-holders of the division, inasmuch as it deprived them of the right to sell after nine o'clock at night, instead of up to ten o'clock, as heretofore. By ordering the clos- ing of the licensed houses an hour earlier the Bench would be taking from the licensees prac- tically the greater part of their trade. He would further submit that the Order was wrong on the ground that circumstances (lid not justify its being made. There was a popular impres- sion that the new Act was passed withyreference to the question of treating of soldiers. But that was not so. It was not a military Act. The military authorities had full power to deal with the military side of the question under the pro- visions of the Defence of the Realm Act. If it were merely a question of the treating of sol- diers, or the serving of soldiers, the military authorities could have got over the difficulty by prohibiting the sale of intoxicants to soldiers during certain hours. •

===== 0002601_19150213_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
"The Suppression of Drunkenness."

But the fact of the matter was that the Tem- porary Restriction Act was not intended to deal with the military, but with the civil population. If their Worships would consider the provisions of the Act they would see that it said that "on the recommendation of the Chief Constable, if the magistrates think it desirable for the main- tenance of order, or the suppression of drunken- ness in any area, they may make an Order." He understood that in Cheshire the Chief Con- stable had made recommendations all over the county. If he, (the speaker)-wer4 right in that statement, he submitted that these recommenda- tions were not entitled tp be very seriously con- sidered. The Act intended licensees to be reasonably protected, and to that end they fixed upon the Chief Constable as being the person who would naturally know the state of order or disorder and the state of drunkenness or sobriety in the districts under him, and he (Mr. White) suggested that the Chief Constable of Cheshire ought to have discriminated on the matter of putting into force this Act, unless he went so far as to suggest that the whole of the county of Chester was drunken and disorderly.

" No Justification."

When they considered the Superintendent's report for that particular • division, he thought he would be right in venturing to suggest that there was no justification whatever to be found for putting the. Order into force in that divi- sion, at any rate. The Chief . Constable had made his recommendation, but it was for the magistrates to coMe to a. judicial determination, and he ventured to think, that, in view or the report on drunkenness in the division, the,mag- istrat/es could not judiciously determine that in their particular area it was necessary to have such an Order to secure the maintenance of order or the suppression of drunkenness. He suggested that the magistrates had not had before them, facts and material such as would justify them in putting the Order into force. in the division, and, on that ground and others, he asked them to re-consider their decision and revoke. • His.elients had Always been, and would always be, prepared, to do anything they could to assist either the Bench 'tor the police in the matter of the prevention of drunkenness and the maintenance of order. '

===== 0002601_19150213_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
Patriotism of "The Trade."

His clients, he was quite sure, were as patriotic as the members of any 'other body in the country, and if they thought that the mili- tary question came in in their case, or that it was for the good of the district and the country, that their selling hours should be curtailed, they would submit without question. But, he ven- tured to suggest, that, in the matter of this Order, neither of the two reasons he had re- ferred to operated. He knew that it was use- less that day to ask them to vary the Order, and so he did not do so. The only option they had was to allow the Order to stand or to revoke it, and his appeal to them was to revoke it, at the same time pointing out that it was particularly with reference to the doing away with that last hour at night that objection was taken. If it would help them, he might point out that the practice with regard to these Orders was not uniform throughout Cheshire. In considering his application he did suggest that they should ask themselves whether there was anything in that particular divislon to justify them in decid- ing that in order to prevent disoler and drunkenness, it was necessary to close the pub- lic-houses at nine o'clock at night.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
A Recent Incident.

The only thing he had heard suggested with regard to that point was that recently there was a case in which .two _men on military duty on one of the bridges in the pear neighbouillood got into trouble. As to that case, he would re- mind the magistrates that, first of all, the two men were not civilians. Secondly, it was not suggested that they got drunk in any licensed house in the district. Thirdly, he ventured to argue that the closing of the public-houses at nine o'clock, or at any other hour, would not have -prevented that regrettable case, or any other case of a similar character, so long as there might be people wicked and foolish enough to take drink to sentries on duty, as was dime in the case to which he referred. Because Benches did not, as a rule, countenance such things, he had, not that day come before them armed with a big, long petition, but if the mag- istrates would like to have any evidence as to the feeling of the neighbourhood on the ques- tion, he was quite prepared to have his appli- cation adjourned, so that he might have the opportunity of calling before them a large num- ber -of witnesses from the district, who would tell them that the proposed hours of closing were a great inconvenience to them.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE JUSTICES' REFUSAL.

After the magistrates had deliberated in their retiring-room,

The -Chairman said: "We have given this matter further consideration, and have come to the conclusion that the Order, as made, must stand. At the end of three months it may come up for re-cousitieraticui."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
DANTE AND SIENA

Miss B. L. Broadbent's Scholarly "Lit. and Phil." Paper.

PORT AND POLITICIAN.

It is difficult at this time, when overwhelm- ing events dominate our minds, to turn our thoughts back many hundred years to other happenings and other ideals. Yet Dante, like the Bible, has an appeal for all circumstances and all time. He was a scholar, a theologian, a philosopher and a poet; but he was also pas- sionately a politician—or, since that word is rather in disrepute, let us say a statesman.

With these words Miss E. L. BROADBENT opened the engrossing lecture upon "Dante and Siena," which she delivered to the Literary and Phil- osophical Society on Monday evening, in the Cairo-street Hall. The President (the Rev. H. V. Pigot) occupied the chair, and there was a fair attendance.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
A PRACTICAL IDEALIST.

Though, said the lecturer, it was•possible for Dante to lift his thoughts upward to behold the mysteries of Paradise, he was also keenly alive to all that was happening in his own city and country; and had his own views for the solu- tion of the problems of his day. Italy was then divided into city states, which were often at war with each other, and besides that there were rival parties within each city—to which strife Dante owed his banishment. Over all was the great question of the claims of Pope and Emperor. Dante looked to the Emperor— yes, t* must admit it—the Emperor of that confederacy of states known as Germany—to give peace and order to Italy. put his ;was a conception of an outside authority and a tri- bunal of justice to check the strife of states and to protect the weak', such as they looked for to-day, when the war was over and a council of the nations should lay down righteous laws "(with power to enforce them) for the good gov- ernment and peace of Europe. Let them, there- fore, in the midst of their present distress, try to realise the peace that Dante won—not easily, but through strife and sorrow.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
INFLUENCE OF SCULPTURE.

Having first recalled the chief features of the 13th century—" that wonderful period of intel- lectual and spiritual life "—the speaker went on to say that it was easier now to picture Dante in Siena than in Florence, for .the former city had changed far less and still retained its mediaeval aspect. The steep, narrow streets of Siena, the old walls climbing the hill-sides, the slopes of fruit trees, with the black and white marble dominating it all, presented to the eyes of Dante a very similar picture to that which one saw to-day. His eyes rested upon the 'glory of mediaeval sculpture, and what effect the renaissance of that sculpture had on Dante's mind one could gather from the Purgatorio ; for the proud, besides having beneath their feet the sculptured story of the vice they were to overcome, were stimulated by the representation of the virtue of humility. In one instance, the portraiture of the choirs was so vivid that the poet thought he heard the singing and smelt the frankincense. That was Dante's tribute to the sculpture of Micolo, Pisano and his school. Next to the Cathedral, the Palazzo della Sig- noria was the most striking feature. It was of curious shape,. curved like a bow round the Campo.—that open space which was the heart of Old Siena, and. which was referred to several times in the Divina Commedia. Here were held the palio, the horse race, and many other fes- tivities of the gay city. The only record of Dante's actual presence in Siena, though they knew by inference that he must have been quite familiar with the city, was in Boccaccio's life, and that referred to this very Campo. He also had certabl;amount of, intimacy with one of Siena's poets (Cecco), and an early sonnet of Cecco's was written to him "as his lord " but he remonstrated with him on his love for a shoemaker's daughter, and Cecco's tone changed into a bitter one igainst Dante's pride. Rossetti had suggested that Cecco must have outlived Dante, otherwise he would Certainly be found in one of the meaner circles of the Inferno.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
FLORENCE v. SIENA.

Meanwhile the rivalry between Florence and Siena was accentuated by strife and the people of Siena prayed in the Duomo, whilst a watch- man was stationed on the tower of (what was now called) the Palazzo Saraceni, to see, three miles away, the varying fortunes of that cruel battle on one of the volcanic elevations in the plain outside Siena, and to announce these hap- penings to the people by beating a drum. De- ceived by treachery, the Florentines turned and fled, and great Was the slaughter. But the tri- umph was not enough for Siena. She sought vengeance, and solemnly proposed that Flor- ence should be destroyed. Through Farinata, leader of the- Florentine exiles, however, a League of Friendship was made between the two cities. It was drawn up by Brunetto Latini, Dante's tutor.

In the Inferno, Dante "met" two of the chief actors in the drama of Montaperti. Again, in Circle VI. (the city of Dis) the heretics were written of as 'being punished, more especially those who denied belief in a future life. This was the reason why,they were enclosed in tombs, for they believed the grave lo be the end of all things. Consequently their punishment was measured by Vaeir own thought, but increased in torment by the flames which made the tombs red hot. There was also another reference to Montaperti when Dante " met " the traitor Bocca degli Abati.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
DANTE DOES HONOUR INHERE THE CHURCH REFUSED.

But though the Sienese had triumphed at Montaperti, the Ghibelline cause was doomed to defeat, and its leader—the gallant Manfred— was killed by Charles of Anjou at Benerento. He died excommunicate, but Dante boldly re- versed that judgment of the Church and ad- mitted to the confines of Purgatory this gracious figure. It was a cruel age and one was some- times surprised, after contemplating the history of the time, not that Dante wrote the "Inferno," but that he wrote the " Paradiso." Capocchio himself was known to Dante as a young man. According to Buti, they had stu- died philosophy together. Capocchio was an, artist, and one Good Friday Dante came upon him in the cloisters of a certain convent and found that he had been painting, on each of his nails, with the most marvellous art and minute- ness, the different scenes of the Passion. Sur- prised by Dante, he foolishly licked off with his tongue what had taken him so long to execute. It was for this waste of his talents that Dante contemptuously termed him a "good ape of nature." . DANTE'S PORTRAITURE.

Dante made no reference to Siena in the "Paradise," but on the whole One could not say that he had been particularly severe on the Sienese. As a Florentine he naturally repeated the usual gibe as to their vanity, just as the English always felt it their duty to say that the French were frivolous, or that the Scotch had no sense of humour. Provenzano Salvani (who had taken the lead in Siena) he ad- mired sincerely, and placed him on the cornice of pride, the sin which he felt to be the least .degrading. - He gave Sapia the advantage of a full explanation of her conduct, and La Pia— as they had seen—furnished one of the most touching passages in the " Commedia." It was true that he had placed three Sienese in Hell; but though he lived only a generation after Montaperti—a defeat most bitter to the Floren- tines—he blamed no Sienese for that, as they had fought for their own country. It was his own fellow citizens Farinata and Bocca, whom he 'punished for Montaperti. It was. to the Virgin Mother specially that Siena directed its prayers. But to Dante she was precious -because-through her aid and inter- cession he reached upward to the vision of the Godhead—the threefold Light and Love which was revealed to his transcended mind, "the Love that moves the seen and the other stars." (Applause.) Miss Broadbent was heartily thanked on the motion of Messrs. E. Sykes and H. Grosvenor, the Chairman supporting.

FATAL "SKYLARKING."

Youth Drowned in a Warrington

Mill Reservoir.

WORKMATES GALLANTRY COM- MENDED.

With becoming modesty a witness related to the Deputy Coroner (Mr. F. A. Jones) at an inquest, held at the Warrington Coroner's Court on Tuesday, the story of his gallant but unavailing attempt to rescue a young workmate from drowning. The witness was Arthur Gil- bert, a sixteen-year-old lad, of 39, Marsh- House-lane, and the body_ upon which the in- quest was held was that of Andrew Sauce, also aged sixteen, the son of Andrew Sauce, a general labourer, of 3, Glasshouse-yard. Sauce, so the evidence showed, foolishly ventured on Saturday afternoon upon a roughly constructed raft on the reservoir at Messrs, Armitage and Rigby's factory, where he was employed, and as a result, despite Gilbert's plucky efforts at rescue, was drowned. SUGGESTED A SAIL ON A RAFT.

The first witness called was Edward Percival, aged sixteen, of 13, Aitken-street, a factory hand employed at Messrs. Armitage and Rigby's Cockhedge factory, who stated that at 3.30 on Saturday afternoon he was employed along with the deceased and another lad, Horace Ashton, clearing up the yard at the works, which were some .forty yards away from the reservoir. Sauce suggested that they should go to the reservoir and try a sail on a wooden raft, which they had roughly constructed earlier in the day. The raft was afloat in the middle of the reservoir, and to reach it Sauce had to climb over a con- denser, which hid him from view of witness and others who had gathered round. Suddenly wit- ness and the rest heard a splash, and concluded that the deceased had fallen into the water. Witness shouted, "Are you all right, Andy?" and getting no answer went to look for Sauce, being just in time to see him disappear under the water, one of his hands only being visible.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
FACTORY RULES RELAXED OWING TO WAR.

Answering Mr. J. D. Healey (of the firm of Messrs. John Taylor and Co., solicitors, Man- chester, who represented Messrs. Armitage and Rigby), the witness said that no part of the work that he and the deceased were engaged upon would take them to the reservoir; they were supposed to be movink bricks in the yard. A juror asked whether boys were allowed to work after one o'clock on Saturday afternoons, and the Deputy Coroner replied that he under- stood that there had been some relaxation of factory rules owing to the war, whilst subse- quently Mr.. John Ainsworth, of Manchester- road, manager of the works, explained that, generally speaking, his firm understood that there was a relaxation, though not definite and official, allowable consequent upon the great shortage of labour arising from the war. They understood that in view of that shortage they nould to some extent do what they otherwise would not do in the matter of employing boys, who wore somewhere near the age of 18, in simple out-door work on Saturday afternoons. A PLUCKY ATTEMPT AT RESCUE.

The 'lad Arthur Gilbert was next called, and he told the Court that he was engaged in help- ing to brush out the yard at the Cockhedge Mills at about 3.35 on Saturday afternoon, when two of the other lads employed on the premises came to him and told him that Sauce had fallen into the reservoir. Witness at once ran thither, and, fully dressed as he was, dived into the water, but by that time Sauce had disap- peared, and he failed to find any trace of him. Just before he reached the reservoir he had heard a splash. Earlier on in the afternoon witness had heard Sauce suggest to the previous witness and a lad named Ashton that they should go to the reservoir and get on the raft. Witness did all he could to try and find Sauce, but he failed, although he dived twice into the water. He had to leave the water owing to exhaustion, and when he did so he summoned further assistance. Altogether he was in the water between five and ten minutes.

The Deputy Coroner: It was very good and brave of you to have tried to save this lad's life, especially-as you had never tried to do anything of this sort before. RECOVERING THE BODY.

William Moorby, a joiner in the employ of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, and living at 39, Fothergill-street, said that at about four o'clock on Saturday afternoon he beard that there was a lad in the reservoir, and he at once obtained assistance and got to work with the grappling irons. At about 5.25 he recovered the body from the centre of the reservoir, where the water was about eight feet deep, Mr. Ainsworth then gave evidence. Sauce, he said, had no right to go near the reservoir, nor had the other youths. THE JURY'S COMMENDATION.

In summing up, the Deputy Coroner made special reference to the bravery of the lad Gil- bert, who, he said, acted most commendably. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental Death," and requested the Deputy Coroner to bring Gilbert's plucky conduct to the notice of the Royal Humane Society, also expressing their sympathy with the relatives of the deceased.

The Deputy Coroner said that he quite agreed with the jury's recommendation as to the lad Gilbert, and would see that their views were expressed in the proper_ quarter. -

Mr. Healey, on behalf of the firm, joined in the expression of sympathy with the family, and added that the conduct of Gilbert would be most favourably brought before the manage- ment of the mills with a view to his prospects being improved. The Deputy Coroner: I am very glad to hear you say that, and I am sure that the jury are also glad.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
"SOCIAL SERVICE."

Mr. W. J. Rowland and the Essence of Christianity.

An inspiring address on " Churchmanship and Social Service" was given by Mr. Wilfrid J. Rowland, sec. of the Warrington branch of the Guild of lielp on Saturday night of last week to the Church of England Men's Society. A large number of parishes in and around Warrington were represented at the gathering, which assembled at St. Mary's School, Sankey, under the presidency of the Vicar (the Rev. J. R. Jones).

Mr. Rowland said that men belonged to societies not only for what they could get, but for what they could give. Christianity was essentially a social religion. Its natural unit was not an individual but a group. The model prayer was one for bread and forgiveness and deliverance for all and not merely for one. One of the tests of this religion was that of social service—service to the hungry, thirsty, lonely, naked, sick, and imprisoned.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
Medical Officer and Apostles.

The speaker, quoting that saying of the Flemish mystic, ltuysbroeck, "I fain would be to the Eternal Goodness what a man's hands are to a man," went on to say that social con- ditions were a continual challenge, • and the challenge of the world was the call of God. The present war was a sign of the failure of Christendom. But precisely those things which issued in international war were also at the back of our industrial life. A strike or a lock- out was a trial of force when reason had failed. Scraps of paper were as sacred in the industrial world as in international relationships. There were, plenty of challenges in the life of War- rington—such as drunkenness and impurity, bad housing and insanitary conditions. Chris- tianity must touch life at every point. It had a gospel of healing, and the Medical Officer might be walking in the footsteps of the., Apostles.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
Practical Measures.

Mr. Rowland urged the society to form a social questions committee to map out a definite course of action, including study circles. There was a pressing need for men who would render reliable service through public bodies—boy scouts, trade unions, the Guild of Help—but it was not necessary that men should wait for such an organisation, as there were opportuni- ties of social service to be found by everyone.

Ethics of Gambling.

An animated discussion followed on a wide range of social subjects, including Sunday labour and gambling. In reply to a question on the "Ethics of Gambling," Mr. Rowland aid he thought it wrong of a man to be willing to take something of value from others and yet at the same time to be unwilling to give an equivalent in goods or service. A resolution was afterwards passed pledging the members to do all in their power to dis- countenance gambling. At the conclusion of the meeting a hearty vote of thanks was ac- corded to Mr. Rowland for his address on the motion of the Rev. G. C. Briggs.

A fortnight ago we said that there was no truth in the statement that there was, or had been, a Victoria Cross hero in the Warrington Infle- mary, and recalled a claim by our con- temporary that it was "pleasing to reflect that the story of the noble ex- ploit for which" a patient in the mili- tary ward "is to receive the Victoria Cross first appeared in the columns of the ' Guardian.' " There has been no answer to that. _We also asked, in the same issue of "The Examiner," that Warrington should emulate Stockton Heath by closing its public-houses earlier during the period of the war.
