would they not have followed the line of least resistance, and have kept away from the ter- rible ordeal of fighting under present-day con- ditions? Again, he denied that the pursuit of legitimate amusement had made the people of to-day less religious than the people of yesterday and the day before.

What Lack of Amusements Has Done' for

Germany.

Let them, in considering that phase of the question, not hastily decide that because a number of people, in reality a very small num- ber, played golf or motored on Sundays, that the desire for amusement was the cause of de- creased attendance at church, or less true re- ligion. The opinion was often expressed that the people of this country did not take life seri- ously enough. Better that way than that they should become like the Germans, who, as the result of their intense application to the serious side. of life, without the compensating amuse- Ftents, had become—as mere cogs in one great commercial machine—a nation utterly soulless and with all its ideas on the really great things of life quite out of focus. Twelve months ago, maybe, the nation were not so sure of them- selves, but recent events had shown that Britain was not a weak-kneed, pleasure-sated nation, but that she was, as always, a nation ready and able to bear her responsibilities and to sacrifice everything to maintain the principles of honour and real progress amongst the nations of the earth. Let them not be too anxious to curtail their amusements, particularly their sports, but let them continue to work with a will and play with a will, so that they might keep both their minds and their bodies healthy. •

JUNIOR LIBERALS AND A REFRESHMENT

BAR. TO THE EDITOR.

Sir,—At the risk of giving an advertisement to your. contemporary, I write to inform you that my attention was drawn on Saturday to a very fiercely-worded diatribe against the licens- ing trade, but on turning to another part of the paper I read : •At the annual meeting of the club (the. Warrington junior Liberal Club) last week it was decided by a big majority to ada a bar to the club for the sale of refreshments of all kinds, &c.

The paragraph is headed "Further Popular- ising the Club." Junior Liberals, mark you, and a bar fot refreshments of all kinds! What cant !—Yours, &c.,

ONLOOKER.

Warrington, Yebruary L 1915.

As it happens that we own the "The Examiner," but do not own the Junior Liberal Club, it is difficult to see the connection between our leading article, for which we were responsible, and the decision of the Juniors to have refrshments for which we were not responsible. Judging by this cleverly- conceived chop logic we should say that " Onlooker " is one of those correspondents who badly lack something to do now that the Tariff Reform campaign is in abeyance. The trailing of red herrings, whether in the guise of figures or facts, needs prac- tice, and " Onlooker " is evidently making good use of his leisure.

OUR EDUCATION BUDGET. - Mr. A. J. JOLLEY, as the head of the financial department cf the Education Committee, will place his estimates of income and expenditure for the year 1915-16 before his colleagues on Mon- day evening. • The 'cost of public education in Warrington next year will be £61,922, but receipts from grants, fees, and other sources will be £32,187, leaving a deficit of £29,735. The committee are asking for £29,0001 from the rates, leaving the odd £7OO to be met in a manner of which we shall. learn .on Monday night This £29,000 is. £3,000 more than the amount charged against the borough rate for the same purpnses last -year, when The deficit provided for was £2,6,000, an odd £9OO being otherwise met upon that occasion. The cost of education steadily advances, but it is a form of expenditure from which the greatest good and the most lasting benefits are derived. So long as we get proper services it is 'difficult to imagine waste in this department whatever the sum employed. There are, in , fact, increasing returns at an increasing rate with every application of this kind of capital. But we must see ,that it is applied properly, and in

Warrington there is no ground for complaint on that score. 'Teachers' salaries in elementary schools have advanced by £1,500 on the year, and rates, rents, interest on loans, and other charges that , show • how our educdtiOnal system is expanding, have likewise advanced. NOT TOO MUCH, BUT THE WRONG KIND. Mr. Burgess said that if he were asked for an answec to the question under debate, he should say that amusement had not taken too great a hold on the community, but that the wrong kind of amusement had. He was no Puritan, but he said without hesitation that if the music halls, the theatres and the picture palaces were swept off the face of the earth to-morrow, the people would be better off.

Mr. Taylor opined that where the British people had made a mistake was that they had not sufficiently encouraged the right sort of pleasure. Outside sport was certainly the pleasure and amusement that the people of these islands required most. Mr. Chadwick said he diA not think that the people of this country were too much given to amusement, so much as that some of the amuse- ments might be improved, as, for instance, the music balls. Mr. Rathbone wanted to know what the opener would suggest to replace the present-day amusements, pointing out that the majority of the people were not in a position to appreciate art, music or literature, except in their lowest forms.

MODERN AMUSEMENTS MORE REFINED. Mr. Walsh warned the members against tak-

jug too hypercritical a view. A study of. his- tory would show that the people had always insisted on a great deal of amusement, and the amusements of to-day were less gross, less bois- terous, and altogether more civilised than were the amusements of bygone generations. The stage, he contended, was taking a right step, and was having a great influence on the public, the increase in .the number of good repertory theatres being a factor that one could note with pleasure. As to the cinema, many chief con- stables had testified that it was doing good in the way of lessening the amount of drinking and of keeping the young people from aimless wan- dering about the streets, and, in consequence, had a reft social value.

Atter further disussion the question was put, and the affirmative was defeated.

GERMAN AND AUSTRIAN COTTON GOODS EXHIBITION.

As our readers will be aware, a very interest- ing exhibition of German and Austrian textiles which have successfully won their way in com- petition with English goods was held in London at the end of last month, and excited a good deal of interest. An almost universal sugges- tion made by those who saw the samples was that they should be brought to Lancashire. Acting upon this view, which was fully shared by the Manchester Chamber of Commerce, arrangements were made for the exhibition to be held in Manchester under the auspices of the Board of Trade (Foreign Samples Section of the Commercial Intelligence Branch). The exhi- bition is to be augmented by a large number of working samples as used by a gentleman re- cently.. representing a German firm in the Far East.

By the kindness of the British Cotton-Growing Association, the Manchester exhibition will take place in their large room, Commercial Buildings, 15, Cross-street, Manchester, on the 16th, 17th, 18th, and 19th of this month. Tickets of admission may be obtained by any firm interested at the offices of the Manchester Chamber of Commerce, 44, Mosley-street, or at the bar of the Royal Exchange, Manchester.

Amount to be provided from the Borough Rate for educati during the yeai, 1915-16

MODERN AMUSEMENTS.

Have They Taken Too Great a Hold

Upon Us?

" PHILOMATHS " SAY "NO."

"Has amusement taken too great a hold on the community?" This pertinent question pro- duced an interesting debate at a meeting of the Philomathic Society, held in Church Chambers, Bridge-street, on Wednesday evening, when Mr. Norman Nield. presided. The affirmative was taken by Mr. A. J. Newton, and the negative by Mr. A. Hodgkinson.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
"SILLY, MEANINGLESS PLAYS."

In the course of his opening, Mr. Newton said that in looking over the national life of to-day, one was struck by the lack of sincerity, the absence of serious reflec- tion, a certain impatience and a disinclination for hard work in all classes of society. If one examined the mode of spending the leisure time in vogue among the masses, could it be said that a reasonable portion was devoted to the acquisi- tion of real happiness, or that an undue portion of it was wasted in idleness and amusement? It was, perhaps, not unreasonable to. say that the average person passed his spare time in. three different ways—entertainment, reading and sports. Dealing firstly with entertainments, perhaps the theatre occurred most easily to one's mind. What sort of 'fare was it that drew the largest crowds to the theatres? It was the musical fare—performances which were nothing more than pretty pieces of amusement, about which there was generally no plot and less real music; the outstanding feature being a crowd of more or less pretty girls, dressed in “swagger" frocks, with low cut necks, and short skirts saucily slashed, and passing through a series of antics that were none too graceful or clever. The whole thing was an appeal to the senses, and, as Mr. Arnold, Bennett had said, no person of intelligence could sit through one of these silly, meaninglesi plays and retain his self-respect.

Music Halls and Cinemas Criticised.

isTet those were the amusements which drew the largest numbers to the theatres, whilst the plays of Shakespeare and the many other fine, thoughtful works in dramatic literature were left to languish in oblivion. As to the music halls, they catered solely for amusement, no at- tempt being made to appeal to the intellect. Then there were the cinemas, and these, he was inclined to think, were having a serious adverse effect on the national life, if only for the reason that they were so undoubtedly and seriously im- peding the work of the technical and other even- ing schools. The Principal of the local Techni- cal Institute had himself been driven to declare that the greatest enemy of the evening school was the picture show. The whole tone of the present day entertainments seemed frivolous and unreal; there was nothing genuinely pleasing or satisfying to the reason in them. Turning to another form of recreation, viz., reading, they found t4l the class of literature most in de- mand was that which was collectively termed "light "—cheap novels, fiction magazines, so- called "smart" papers of the " Bits " order—all of which were simply used to pass an idle hour and were forgotten as soon as read.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Play to Live; Not Live to Play.

Of all the aids to a reasonable and happy life, reading was probably the best, but that the majority of the borrowers from the Warrington Public Library had no desire to become ac- quainted with the thoughts of the noblest minds, but preferred the less profound and more amus- ing, was to be discovered in the fact that last year 78.7 per cent. of the books issued to adults were fiction: Perhaps no canntry in the world was more devoted to gatnes than Britain, ant starting on the assumption that people ought to play games to live, and not live to play games, it was probable that some excesses took place in this direction. The craving for amusement seemed to have diverted games from being pleas- ant exercises, which were intended to give health and beauty to the body, into spectacular shows for the amusement of the multitude. He knew it could be argued that aster a hard or.:iek's toil workmen ought to have a bit of amusement on Saturday afternoons, by way of a change from their monotonous labours, but it seemed to him that there were other and more profitable ways of spending a wet, wintry. afternoon than watching football matches.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
NO NATIONAL DETERIORATION.

Defending the negative point of _view,

Mr. Hodikinson postulated that before one could answer the point at issue in the affirmative it was necessary to prove that as a result of the desire for amusement there had been some de- terioration in character, capability, or intellect; or that the people were less religious and had weakened in their respect for sacred things;, or that they were less healthy. The average man of to-day lived at a higher pace than did the man of any previous age, and, far from their being harmful, he was convinced that without diversions of some kind, there would he far more nervous breakdowns among the workers than were, happily, heard of. Even if it were pos- sible to prove any deterioration in the quality of the community it was not the result of taking amusement, but would be ascribable to the pres- sure at which modern man, in spite of himself, was compelled to live.

A Check to Hard Drinking.

Far from being a bane, amusements, in at least one direction, had actually been a blessing to the 'country, for, he contended, that much of the decrease in the drinking habits of the nation was due to them. In their own time they had been able to note, alongside the growth of amusements, a big decrease in heavy drinking and an improvement in the appearance of the workers and their families and homes. If the supply of amusements were reduced, positive harm would be done. That the desire for reasonable amusement had done no harm was evidenced by the fact that to-day the capability of the workers was higher .than ever it was, and that there were no signs of weakening ,in either stamina or intellect. Look at the • response to the call for recruits. If the young men had ,been weakened by ' their love of Luzubenaeuts

WAR STORIES.

"CALLED BACK."

Warrington Seaman's Remarkable Escape from Watery Grave.

Ordinary seaman Frank Wilson, of 21, Willis- street, Warrington, a member of the First Bri- gade of the Naval Volunteer Division—which fought at Antwerp before the evacuation—has jast had a remarkable escape from a watery grave.

Along with nine of his mates, he was sent to serve on H.M.S. Vicknor. Just before the de- parture of that vessel, however, he was called back to his depot in the south of this country. A few days afterwards the Vicknor was sunk, and all the nine mates, whom he had anticipated rejoining, perished with the rest of the crew.! Curiously enough, only three or four days before this sad event took place some of the members of Seaman Wilson's family visited a local picture house to see the drama "Called back."

THE FIRST BATH.

Warrington Soap Makes Brand New

Men of Tired Soldiers.

The manager of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons has received the following letter: Dear Sir,—Of the incidents which stand out in my mind after five months' active service I think the most vividly pleasant is the one when I had my first bath. • We had turned a dyeworks into a bath-house for my division, and, armed with a c,oinplete change of undress, we marched down five miles each way to have our first bath during a lull in the cold days in December. , The tubs sawn in half, the heaps of hot water, and a tablet of the soap you presented UR with —" Pinkobolic "—we got thoroughly thawed through, perfectly clean, and came out, to use our own words, "absolutely brand new," thanks to your carbolic soap, which came through the ordeal with colours flying. I may tell you that you can hear more songs in that bath-house than would stock a good big music-hall for a year. It's a sight for sore eyes to go down from the trenches and hear the boys cheruping, see ,the clouds of steam, and smell the carbolic soap. Thanking you again for that tablet of soap.— Yours appreciatively, HENRY D. WADE, 20190, Gunner 111th Battery, R.G.A., 7t.li Div. MI. Army Corps.

UNREVOKED.

Oh the day wher} the Order came into force ,to close the public-houses in the Daresbury Petty Sessional Division at an earlier hour, under the Temporary Restriction Act, Mr. AUBREY WHITE, representing the whole of the licensed victuallers in theo division, appeared before the justices at Stockton Heath and asked them to revoke it. It was a hardy thing to do, but the magis- trates were impervious to shock tac- tics, and intimated that the Order woulfl remain in force for three months at least. At the end of that time it will be "reconsidered."

It is well to study carefully Mr. WHITE'S reasons for thinking that the magistrates might complacently eat their own words. These reasons are set out in full in another column, The rulQ had been made, he said, udder a civil and not a military Act, and there was no suggestion that civil order was in danger from drunken- ness. If the military element were suffering from drink and drunkenness the military authorities had power to deal with the question by means of the Defence of the Realm Act. But neither conditions prevailed, said Mr. WHITE, and the CHIEF CONSTABLE Of the County was not warranted in making the recommendation he did. With all due respect to the advocate for the Trade, we must adhere to-the opinion that Colonel MALCOLM 1S the , better judge of the needs of Cheshire.

It was the best hour of the day that had been taken from the public-houses, said Mr. AUBREY WHITE. That, we take it, is what Major. MALCOLM is after. it were useless to curtail the hours of public-houses during a period when they are normally empty. As to this best hour Mr. WHITE had a far stronger case, but he would 'have been on dan- gerous ground at once had he entered upon it. It is unfair, we admit, that restrictions should be placed on country inns in a district where there is only one licence to 6very 547 inhabi- tants, among whom proeeedings for drunkenness are exceedingly rare, while there, is no restriction over the boundary line in the neighbouring town, where there is one licence to every .390 inhabitants, and prosecu- tions for drunkenness place it second , on a list of Northern boroughs with which it is grouped for the purposes of comparison. Warrington provides fits courts Nvith 8.25 cases of drunkenness, per 1,000, against 2.17 in such a town as Burnley. But if Mr. WHITE had pleaded the injustice of the treat- ment of Stockton Heath and other parishes compared with the treatment of Warrington he would have been, as we say, on dangerous ground, . for there are some people about who are intent on levelling up and not levelling down. They would like to see War- rington brought up to the Stockton Heath level.

' TIIE LYING JADE.

That lying jade, Rumour, has been busily at work in Warrington of' late. From the starting point that the war must have " hit " tradesmen and others severely persons and firms have been somewhat ,indiscriminately se- lected for the purposes of gossip to be linked up with 'failures and finan- cial embarrassments that have been conjured up in the imagination of the pessimistic or Mischievous side of the community. As a matter of fact War- rington was never so prosperous as now. Never was the income of the working classes at a better all-round level. There is a shortage of labour and a surfc.qt of work. Even those who

A NEW OLD SOLDIER.

Comforts With Economy For Our New Army.

STRONG AND FIT.

Mr. Frank Kay, one of our reporters, whose first-day experiences as a raw recruit we gave last week, sends us the following this week:— In these days of rapid progress a man who has been in Kitchener's Army for a fortnight is considered to be somewhat of an "old soldier," and so I find myself in the happy position of being welcomed into the company of other "old soldiers," and also of being considered an authority on the "way about" by new-comers —and, incidentally, I might say, of being in the position to play the same joke on " freshers " as was played on me when I arrived here. I have had plenty of opportunities to get about and "see things." One thing that has struck me forcibly has been the fact that in making arrangements for the accommodation of men in training the Government are considering well the comfort of the men, whilst at the same time keeping the planet -" economy" well in view: Snug, warm and well-appointed huts are spring- ing up like magic, and labourers and joiners can find ,plenty of work. There is not the slightest doubt that those men who are now in training—to swell our Army at the Front, or to make good the casualties—will be fit, healthy and strong, and quite equal to the task which will be set them.

Sober and Clear-eyed. Apropos of a recent leading article in "The Examiner" on "The Sin of Treating Soldiers," and as regards the question of fitness, I may say that during my stay here I have only seen about a dozen soldiers under the, influence of drink. The public-houses are open to soldiers only for about three hours at night, and the punishment meted out to bibulous soldiers is quite sufficient to deter even the most inveter- ate "boozer." And what do you find? On parade every morning are hundreds of clear- eyed, straight-backed " Tommies," living pic- tures of health. But then those who drink might ask, "What else is there to do in spare _ .

time?" Well, I can straightway answer that, because the scene around me as I write is con- vincing enough that these men here think there is something else to pass away the hours. I am in one of the Soldiers' Homes, and round about are other men writing letters—for which there is every facility—and the other "Kitch- ener Kids" are crowded about in the room

listening to a red-faced "Tommy" sing "Henry the Eighth I am," and joining lustily in the chorus. It matters not that the vocalist is a bit husky or has forgotten a line or two—the pianist pulls him through—and then for the chorus. Do the men enjoy it? A look at their faces answers this. A look of mild expectancy when the verse is being sung, and then just before the chorus a contortion of the face and then a full smiling face as the chorus rattles along. In these homes one can get a nice cup of tea for a penny and little delicacies at a most reasonable figure. Then there are billiards, draughts, and other games, as well as reading- rooms, in which are the daily papers and books of various titles. Oh #. yes, the men enjoy their recreation, because. they have to work hard for it.

Soldiers Chat. When I was in Warrington engaged in inter- viewing wounded soldiers, I had reason to form the idea that they were all very reticent. But " Tommy " to the book and pencil artist, and " Tommy " to a "pal," are two entirely differ- ent persons. I was chatting with two or three wounded warriors the other day, and the stories of daring and individual bravery—never seen or recognised—made one proud to be Eng- lish. One thrilling story of a soldier's action at the Front was particularly impressive. After two men had been shot down in an attempt to bring in wounded, this gallant man went out with a party of three. They were holding the stretcher when a bullet struck one soldier in the shoulder. Only for a few minutes did he stop, and then on again to safety, where he dropped exhausted. My companions spoke of the good work done by the aeroplanes, and that reminds me that we see them out every day practising, and one day we saw an airship cutting along at a terrific rate. Observation proves that our airmen are a very daring and skilful set of men, and there is no doubt that they will be at least a match for those of the enemy.

I promised to say something about the work here, so here goes. ,

In Training as Self-helps. In barracks everything is scrupulously clean, and there not being any charwomen about, the work has to be done by the men, and all get a share whether it be sweeping the roads, acting as waiters for meals, removing " furni_ ture " from one block to another, or cleaning anything in connection with the barracks. Some of it is hard and what is sometimes called " dirty " work, but as qne chap marching next to me said, "Someone has to do it; it will do us good not harm, and it tends to make us efficient soldiers, and when we are at the Front we shall have to do all sorts of work, so we might as well prepare." By the way, I saw my friend who arrived in such style last week wield- ing a pitch fork, and actually he was chatting and laughing with the corporal in charge. What a spirit permeates these men! We have had very nasty weather this week, and you can probably guess what a route march is like alcrng roads, the surface of which has been pulverised into a thick layer of semi-liquid mud. Yes, it rains, too, but do we care? Of course, we grumble inwardly, somewhat unmeaningly, but then consider that we shall not take much harm because we are well shod and covered in mack- intoshes. Certainly, we are glad when told to dismiss, as everyone else would be, but we have the satisfaction of knowing that we have earned a little rest, my share of which I will go and enjoy now.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
Local Leather Industry.

The Warrington correspondent •of "The Leather Trades Review," writing in the cur- rent issue, says: "Trade during the week was steady, orders , in hand 'readily absorbing all the stock coming along. The chief concern just now is to accelerate production in order to clear off the arrears in certain directions, for some of the most urgently needed classes have accumulated to a considerable ,extent. One of the marked features of the week was the extraordinary number of leather buyers calling with the ob- ject of finding extra stout bends. It is under- stood this was due to the War Office having become more stringent as to the substance of the leather for Army soles, insisting this shall now be the full 5-16 in. Unfortunately, the search in this centre had very little result, the number of such bends being _extremely small, and even then were already booked up. It may be doubted if a tithe of the necessary Army work can be undertaken if the authorities per- sist in their specification. The demand for the domestic trade continues rather slack, as many of the factories have been practically com- mandeered for Government work, and are un- able to cater for their usual customers. The retail trade allege that it is exceedingly difficult to get their orders accepted, 'although they are prepared to pay advanced prices. Prices all round are easily maintained, and, 'indeed, any easing is not seriously anticipated by any sec- tion of the trade; raw goods advance regularly, and suitable foreign hides have become both scarcer and dearer. The great rise in freights is bearing very heavily on materials, and where it used to cost about 20s. per ton to deliver extract into, the tanyard it now costs from 40s. to 455."

" Only Stuffed!" The other day a local infants' teacher, talk- ing to her class about the Warrington monu- ments, was not a little puzzled by the follow- ing information imparted by a • yputhful prodigy: "Please, ma'am, the cannons at Arpley arena zeal. only stand!"

OUR BELGIAN GUESTS.

Successful Entertainment at the Star Kinema. BISHOP OF SALFORD'S TRIBUTE.

At the Star Kinerna, Church-street, on Tues- day afternoon, an interesting and highly suc- cessful performance was given in aid of the local Belgian Relief Fund, this effort on the part of the management of this popular house. of amusement being but one of several that- they have put forward in the Cause of the dif- ferent charitable funds associated with the war and with other worthy objects in the town; services for which the amusement lovers of the town and the people in general owe them a dis- tinct meed of praise. Tuesday's matinee per- formance was honoured by the presence of the. Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Pea- cock) and the Roman Catholic Bishop of Salford (the Rev. Dr. Casartelli), as Well as many of the- best-known and most influential members or the local Catholic community, included' amongst whom was Mr. Austin Crowe, the secretary of the local Catholic-. Belgian Relief Committee; • which body co-operated with the management of the house in the promotion of the matinee. It is satisfactory to be able to record that so worthy an object drew a splendid audience, the ordinary seating accommodation proving quite' inadequate and temporary seats having to be provided. . MAYOR THINKS WARRINGTON MIGHT HAVE DONE MORE. During an interval in the highly interesting. programme arranged, ' The Mayor said that he was pleased to find that Mr. Connor had once again allowed that- ; handsome picture theatre -to be used in aid of the Belgian Refugees Relief Fund—a fund which was certainly doing good work. The large audience which had assembled that after- noon was a just, reward of the kindness of the management in. assisting, and bore eloquent testimony to the exertions which the Catholic Belgian Relief Committee had put forth. Need he 'say more of the Belgians than that the- people of this country were all pleased to do something for them. Although Warrington had done much for the refugees, he was, how- ever, of opinion that, Seeing her size and how .wealthy she was, she had not done- too much compared with some of the smaller towns wll,ich he had visited in the matter of the number of Belgians she had .made herself responsible for looking after. Still they were 14Soking after a number of people from the shores of their gal- lant little ally, and, he. believed, were looking after them well.

His Worship concluded by wishing the Catholic (ommittee, and all other committees' that were working on behalf of the refugees, every success in the work they had undertaken. BISHOP CASARTELLI AND BELGIUM'S BRAVE PART.. Bishop Casartelli, who said he regarded him- self as half a Belgian, began by mentioning: that he received part of his education at the beautiful and historic University of Louvain, now a heap of ruins, thanks to the unspeakable vandalism of the Germans. To Belgium—the- little nation which had suffered such awful atrocities—the great allies owed a deep debt of gratitude. (Hear, hear.) That debt they were trying to express by extending their hospitality to refugees from the war-wrecked little country —poor people, expatriated by cruel fate, but who, happily, were for the time being finding. a new home—a second Fatherland—in Britain. By doing what they could for the exiled Bel- gians, Great Britain was fulfilling a duty to humanity, as well as paying a debt which she owed. There could be no doubt that had not the gallant Belgians stood Up to the Germans so nobly as they did in the all-important earl/ stages of the war the history of the conflict might have been very different to what it was and to what they hoped it was going to be Europe would have fallen victim to the invader but as it was she was free, and Paris, the goat of German ambition, was saved. kApplause.)

THANKS WHERE THANKS ARE DUE. The Rev. Father Wilson proposed a vote d. thanks to the Bishop for his presence and speech, to the Mayor and Mayoress for gracing the entertainment with their attendance, to the management of the house for the part they had- played in making the matinee possible, and to the committee who had 'worked hard in dis- posing of tickets, &c. The rev, father said that one reason-why more had not been done in War- rington was the great difficulty in finding suit- able housing accommodation in the, town, and, under all the circumstances, be thought the citizens had made a good response to the appeal on behalf of the people of the nation that had "lost all save honour." The audience signified their approval of the proposition. THE ENTERTAINMENT AND THE WORKERS.

Pneumonia Prevalent.

Pneumonia is still prevalent in the town, and last week five deaths were due to it. There were more deaths from pneumonia than from any other disease, bronchitis coming second with four victims.

Lent Lectures at the Parish Church. Each Friday during Lent special lectures will be given in the Parish Church, ,and those on Friday next will be by the Rev. Canon Bell (Canon Missioner of York), in the afternoon to women and at night to men.

Quarter of a Century's Services. At the last Newton Petty Sessions Mr. Henry Greensll, solicitor, Warrington, was congratu- lated by the chairman of the Bench (Colonel F. J. Wood) upon the completion of 25 years' most satisfactory service as clerk to the justices of the Warrington Petty Sessional Division.

We understand that at a meeting of the governing body of the Boteler Grammar School, held on Tuesday afternoon, it was decided that the annual Speech Day should not be held in the Parr Hall this year, but that the prize distribution should take place quietly in the school itself.

More Pay for Leather Workers.

At a meeting of the leather tanners of the Warrington district, held in Manchester on. Friday of last week, the question was con- sidered of the present high price of domestic commodities as it affects the working classes, and it was decided to make a grant of 3s. weekly to all tanyard employes over 22 years old, and Is. 6d. weekly to those under; this not to be considered as an advance of wages, but as a voluntary grant to tide over the difficult period.

A Week's Births and Deaths.

During the week ended February 6th, reports the Medical Officer of Health (Dr. (1. W. N. Joseph), 43 births (27 males and 16 females) were registered 1n Warrington, as against 53, in the corresponding period of last year. In he period under review 21 deaths (11 males and 10 females) took place, as compared with 28 last year. The death-rate was 14.4 and the birth- rate 29.6, as against 19.6 and 37.2 respectively last year.

The Great Insuted. At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Insurance Committee, held under the presi- dency of the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) at the Town Hall on Monday evening, it was reported that the grand total of insured per- sons in the borough who were at the beginning of the year entitled to medical benefit under the Insurance Act was 29,334, being an increase of 98 on the total for the, previous quarter. Of the total 381 .wdre deposit contributors—a de- crease of 26—and 29,908 .were members of approved societies—an increase of 133.

Dr. Bennett on Famous Hymns. A large. audience assembled in the Latchford Wesleyan Schoolroom on Thursday evening to hear Dr. Bennett deliver an interesting lecture on "The History of Famous Hymns, from the Reformation to the . Present Day." Mr. Howarth was in the chair. At the outset the lecturer gave a description of about twenty of the most popular hymns during the last 400 years, and made special reference to those written by Charles Wesley, the greatest of all hymn writers, and who composed over 760 hymns. The lecturer made special reference to the hymn "From Greenland's Icy Mountains," which, he said, was composed by Bishop Heber, whose mother was an Atherton and who was born at Bewsey Old Hall.

Emmanuel Debating Society. On Tuesday evening a small but apprecia- five audience of members of the Emmanuel Literary and Debating Society experienced great pleasure in listening to an admirable paper by the Rev. Wm. Reid on the life of " Savanarola." After briefly sketching the geographical setting, the lecturer lucidly set forth the peculiar moral condition of Venetian and Florentine life in the time of the Medicis. Touching briefly on the influence of the Renais- sance, he showed how the licentiousness of the times and the moral depravity of the Floren- tine government combined to make Savanarola a reformer in both spiritual and political realms, leading finally to his glory and his martyrdom. At the conclusion of his able and interesting paper Mr. Reid was accorded a hearty vote of thanks.

Warrington Photographic Society. "Simplicity and Success in Photography" was the title of Messrs. Burroughes and Wil- liams' lecture on Tuesday. Beginning with iictures of the oldest camera outfits carried on a handcart, the lecture contrasted with great effect the present pocket arrangements. Again the contrast was shown between the early de- veloping mixtures and the handy products of the lecturing firm. The list of illustrations was varied by snapshots of big-game hunting and the beautiful effects or staining lantern slides With various colouring agents. Perhaps the most useful section of the lecture showed the advan- tage of intensifying or reducing plates in order to correct errors made or to produce special effects desired by the photographic artist.

Walker.

Wheat Still Advancing.

Wheat, reports our market correspondent, still continues to advance in price. _

At the Warrington market on Wednesday it was quoted at Bs. 4d. per bushel of 701 b., the fluctuations of the previous week having re- sulted in a substantial advance of 44. per bushel. _ _

Oats. sold firmly at the same market at 4s. 6d. to 4s. 7d. per 451 b., and hay, which was in better demand, and best clover variety sold at from .23 15s. to o£4 ss. per ton, whilst meadow hay fetched £3 10s. Best flour was in good demand at £2 Bs. per 2801 b., and is. Rd. per 201 b. was the selling price of best bran.

Honoured in His Own Country.

"On the Road," the monthly publication of the ;United Kingdom Commercial Travellers' Association, says in its February number: "The offices held this year by Mr. W. P. Ashton, of the Warrington Branch, must almost break the record in the United Kingdom Commercial Travellers' Association. He has been re-elected chairman of the benefit society, was unani- mously elected chairman of the branch, and after holding the office of honorary treasurer for several years was unanimously re-etected. Although Mr. Ashton is very Tittle known out- side Warrington as a United Kingdom man, it is very evident he is held in high esteem at home."

In addition to the above, we understand Mr. W. P. Ashton is a trustee of the United King- dom Commercial Travellers' Association Local Unemployment Fund. He is also secretary of the Masonic Lodge of Rectitude, No. 3597. This is surely a case of a prophet not being without honour in his own country.

New Member of Insurance Committee.

The Rev. Father Neville Wilson having re- signed his seat on the Warrington Insurande Committee that body were at their monthly meeting on Thursday night—over which the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) presided— called upon to elect a successor. The Irish National Foresters, as representing 143 insured persons in the town, asked them to appoint Mr. F. W. Roddy, of 8, Salisbury-street, and the local branch of the Ancient Order of Hiber- mans and the Catholic Friendly Societies' Association, representing over a thousand insured persons in the town, submitted the name of Mr. Edward Rigby, of 208, Wilders- pool-road. Remarking that the difference of opinion between two sections of the Catholic community were of no concern to the commit- tee, Mr. Delaney proposed the appointment of Mr. Rigby, which appointment was duly seconded and carried.

Present for a Friendly Society Soldier.

There was an interesting gathering of mem- bers of the "Rose Bud" Lodge of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (A. 11. on Saturday evening, when a beautifully framed emblem of merit of the Order was presented to Private Bro. R. J. Bleasdale, now of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, and then home on a few days' leave. The presentation was in recog- nition of the gallant brother's services to the lodge and of his having passed through the various lodge offices. P.P.C.S. Bro. J. Mather (the secretary of the lodge ) made the presenta- tion, and in doing so said that in Bro. Bleasdale the lodge had a member of whom they were all proud, and one who could be relied upon to do his duty to his King and country faithfully and well; indeed in just so whole-hearted and con- scientious a manner as he had served his lodge. They all wished him every, success and a safe return from the war. P.P.C.S. Bro. Champion added a few well-chosen words in similar strain, and Bro. Bleasdale suitably responded.

Citizens' Guild of Help. The annual meeting of the Warrington Citi- ens' Guild of Help will be held in the Council Chamber of the Town Hall on Monday evening next, when the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) has kindly consented to preside. Two excellent speakers have been secured to address the meet- ing. One of them, Mrs. Muter Williams, in addition to being a most attractive speaker, has had great experience in social work among the poor women of Manchester, and she has specialised on the subject of the feeding of nursing mothers. Her, address should prove of great interest and help to the ladies engaged in the work of local welcomes and motherS' meetings. The other speaker will be Mr. D'Aeth, whose social work in Liverpool is known far and wide. He is a lecturer at Liver- pool University, and he acts as director of re- ports to the Council of Voluntary Aid, a body which forms the rallying point of all social and philanthropic work in the great city to which he belongs.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A.

Mr. Beaumont, a Derbyshire County Colin-, cillor, and a prominent 'member of the Temper- ance Legislative League, addressed the mem- bers of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday afternoon on the subject of "War and. Tempe- rance." In the course of his remarks he em- phasised the great need of the practical at the present day, observing that ideals were good, but were of little avail unless backed up by the practical. He spoke of the splendid Work being done by the Y.M.C.A in the great military encampments up and down the country. That assopiation was doing splendidly in the matter of looking after the social side of camp life, and thearien badly needed a well-regulated, uplifting social side after the multifarious and arduous duties of the day were done. They must have somewhere to go, and 'failing any other place .they were prone to seek their recreation in the public-house. The speaker adversely criticised the law which leaves the matter of the closing of public-houses in the hands of the Chief Con- stables of the various towns and. districts; and opined that the chiefs themselves would rather that the onus of responsibility were thrown upon the magistrates. He advised all who felt , strongly on that point to write to Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., expressing their views.—The Rev. T. T. James, on behalf of the members, extended

hearty greetings to two P.S.A.-ites who have "joined up "—one being a Warringtonian on furlough and the other a P.S.A. member from Norwood, at present quartered in Orford Bar- racks.

The Union Bank.

In consequence of the increase in the cost of food, the directors of the Union Bank of Man- chester, Limited, have made special allowances to all married men, and single men where they are the main supports of households, where their incomes do not exceed £3OO. The bank is providing for half of the increase in the cost of food.

A.‘ highly interested paper entitled "The Typist and the Times" was read by Miss Buckley before the members of the Warrington Shorthand Writers' Association at their last weekly meeting. The paper was very much appreciated by the members, and the secretary on their behalf expressed the wish that Miss Buckley would on some future occasion give a similar paper. • Boys' Brigade for Wycliffe. A goodly number of boys assembled in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall on Tuesday evening to hear a most interesting lantern lecture upon the life and work of the Boys' Brigade—it having been decided that a Wycliffe Church Brigade shall be formed. The Rev. T. T. James presided, and the lecturer was Mr. F. P. Gibbon, the vice-president Manchester Bat- talion, who has belonged to the movement for thirty years. ' At the conclusion of his dis- course the lecturer was heartily thanked, on the motion of Messrs. W. Brown and T. F. Wallington.

WAR ITEMS. COUNTESS ROBERTS' APPEAL FOR

FIELD GLASSES.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,---Although the appeal made by my father, Lord Roberts, to sportsmen and others, to lend their race, field .or stalking glasses for the use of officers and non-com:wane led officers under orders for the Front, has been most gratifying, a very large number of glasses are still required. Up to the present some 18,000 pairs of field mill stalking glasses have been received. These have been carefully examined and classified by an expert before being issued. ,The names and addresses of the owners are registered, and the glasses themselves are engraved with an index number, in order that the owners can be traced at the conclusion of the war, and their property, when possible returned to them. Many people who did not Tossess field glasses felt that they would like to contribute towards the fund' which has been raised for the purpose of purchasing them, and sent cheques instead. My father was deeply grateful for the generous response made to his appeal, but at the same time he realised that an even greater number of glasses would be required, the stock in hand being nearly exhausted while the call for them was continuous. He had made up his mind to appeal to the public once again, and a letter to this effect, had been written, but not signed, before he left for France. I therefore venture to make this further appeal in his name. All contributions will be dealt with in the same way as formerly and duly acknowledged. It is important to note that all glasses, cheques and communications should be addressed to the Secretary, National Service League, 7,2, Vic- -toria-street, Westminster, S:W. Englemere, Ascot, Berks

iTLEEN ROBERTS.

YOUNG SOLDIER'S UNTIMELY DEATH.

mpressive Military Funeral at Great Sankey. -

The death took place on Sunday morning, at the early age of 18, of Gunner Harry Silvester, of the Royal Field Artillery, *hose home was at Heath-road, Penketh. The deceased was formerly employed at the Penketh Tannery, and joined the Army only about six months ago. He was drafted South a few weeks back, and after an illness there lasting about three weeks, he passed away. The body was brought home for burial, and impressive Scenes marked the funeral, which took place on Thursday after- noon at St. Mary's- Church, Great Sankey, a large number of relations and friends gathering in the church and at the graveside. The burial service was conducted by the Vicar, the Rev. J. R. Jones. who made touching reference to de- ceased. The hymn, "Oft in danger, oft in woe," was sung at the graveside. A number of men belonging to the 4tl-1 Bat- talion of the South Lancashire Regiment at- tended, and at the graveside paid a last tribute of respect by standing at the salute as the coffin was lowered. Floral tributes from many relatives and friends were placed upon the grave, among them being those from "His sweetheart, Mary," "Brother Tom and family," "Jack and Walter," R. Acomb, J. Howard --laid J. Molyneux ; Mrs. Goulden and Tis, Mrs. David- son and family, the Penketh Tannery, and the officers and men of the 27th Battery of the Royal Field Artillery.

ASSOCIATION OF CHESHIRE TRAINING CORPS.

At an influential meeting of Cheshire gentle- men, held at Chester Castle on Thursday, it was decided that an Association be formed of the vAribus Volunteer Training Corps in the county, the same to be divided into battalions raised as far as possible in the areas comprised in the respective Parliamentary Divisions of the county. The Lord Lieutenant of the county (the Duke of Westminster) was elected presi- dent of the Association; Colonel George Dixon, the regimental commandant; and Colonel Alan Sykes, ALP., the hon. secretary. •

The objects of this Association are: To pro- mote recruiting for the Regular and Territorial Army; to encourage lanen not of age for servi..3e in the ranks, or otherwise disqualified for ser- vice, to drill and learn- the elements of mus- ketry in their spare time; and to co-ordinate all existing organisations with similar objects, and to promote uniformity in their rules and regu- lations.

Mr. Joseph Tynan.

We regret to record the death of Mr. Joseph Tynan, of 5, Dundonald-avenue, Stockton Heath, who passed away on Monday last. Mr. Tynan, who was in his 73rd year, had resided for some years in the village and had become widely known and esteemed. His early manhood was spent in the Army, and after fourteen years in India he came to Warrington in 1878, at the opening of the Warrington Barracks. He had resided in the town and neighbourhood ever since, and left the Army honoured for good ser- vice and with the esteem of his fellows, a silver watch being presented to him by those among whom he had served. He leaves a widow, a son and a daughter. The funeral took place on -Wednesday at St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, the service being conducted by the Rev. J. Collins. Among the mourners were: Mr. Edward Boscow Tynan (son), Mr. Harry Syers (son-in-law), Mr. Edwin Boscow, Mr. Frank Boscow, Mr. Torn Boscow, Mr. Arthur Boscow, and Mr. James Fisher. The coffin was carried by Messrs. S. Beckley, Rustage, W. E. Pile and W. Welsby. A fine floral tribute in the form of a Union Jack, sent by Mr. and Mrs. T. Boscow and Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Boscow, was Placed on the coffin. Messrs. J. Mounfield and Sons carried out the funeral arrangements.

The play is acclaimed by the critics as one of the most sparkling of the many sparkling farces imported from France. It deals with the woes of a young husband, who, on his wedding day, is suddenly brought face to face with the fascinating actress who engaged his affections in the blithe and irresponsible days of bachelor- hood, and whose troubles are intensified when he discovers that the charming Parisian flat in which it is intended that the honeymoon shall be spent is none other than the abode of his quondam inamorata, the aforesaid fascinating mime. The situation is one that lends itself to the farce-maker, and the makers of this par- ticular one have availed themselves to the full of the opportunities offered them, the result being a play so chock full of merriment that by the time the denouement is reached- every- body in the theatre, save such as are dead to all humour, is pleasantly prostrate with the exer- tion of over-inuch laughter. In a word "Oh ! I Say !" is a positive scream from beginning to end, and we are getting our risible faculties into special trim before going to see it.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

MERRY PANTO. AT THE HIPPODROME.

• A jolly little pantomime based on the ever- popular fairy story of Cinderella is meeting with great success at the Hippodrome this week, and deservedly 80, for it is a merry and bright pro- duction—full of harmless fun and.frolic, tune- ful withal, and played by as capable a"crowd" of comedians, pretty girls, and dainty dancers as could well be wished for. Wit and melody are the strong features of this version of the old nursery tale, with the result that when the audiences are not having their ears enchanted, they are, to use a colloquialism, "having their ribs tickled," by the latest and up-to-date wheezes evolved in the mirth manufactory; whilst all the time there is something for the eye to feast upon, the production being on a scale at once lavish and artistic—the forest glade and ballroom settings being particularly worth seeing. A dainty Cinderella is found in Miss Lilian Errol, who sings "and dances her way iiito the hearts of all beholders' whilst she has a dashing lover in the Prince Brilliant of Miss Edith Harmer, whose acting is full of vivacity, and who knows how to sing a good song and sing it well. Miss Vera Elmore is a big success as the Prince's Valet, and Miss Ada Vernon is quire' in the picture as the Marquess of Taxes, whilst Miss Ethel Brown and Miss Ethel Brailsford do good work as the village pet and the fairy god- mother respectively. The batch of comedians is headed by Mr. Ernie Crosby as Baron Crook- sted of Stoney Castle, and he extracts evety possible ounce of fun out of the part, his scenes with the ugly sisters, cleverly portrayed by the Sisters Sprightly, being productive of uproarious merriment. Mr. Leo. Fields as Buttons adds greatly to the fun, as also do Messrs. Engers and Escott, as Kuff and Kollar, two jolly broker's men. If you have not seen this amus- ing version of "Cinderella" you should seize the chance whilst yet it remains.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

First Tram to Cross on New,Track on Monday.

AN INTERESTING EVENT.

Warrington's handsome new bridge is rapidly nearing completion; in fact, little re- mains to be done to it save the putting to of the finishing touches. For some weeks now the hoardings which obscured the half of it on which work was proceeding have been removed, and those- using it have had the opportunity of observing its noble width and proportions and of genarally admiring it _

During the Kist few days the men engaged on the work have been busy laying the tram tracks, and on Monday the electric cars making the journey to and from Latchford will for the first time cross the bridge on the new rails, which have been laid'down in the centre of it; after which the temporary, tracks will be taken up and the new Stockton Heath lines will be

linked up, whilgt the temporary and unsightly standards holding the trolley wires will dio- appear.•

-The passage of the first car on the new per- manent Latchford track has been timed for three o'clock on Monday afternoon. There will, of course, be no formal opening of the bridge, as that ceremony was performed by the King in July of 1913, but it is anticipated that the Mayor, the chairman of the Tramways Committee (Dr. Joseph), and a number of mem- bers of the Council will ride in the first car to cross on the new track.

It is now over three years since work was commenced on the bridge, and by the time the job is complete it will have taken fully three and a half years. The bridge has a total width of 80 feet, 60 feet of which are utilised as a roadway. At present the whole width of the road is not properly crowned, only one side , having been done, and this has given rise to a stupidi rumour that one side of the structure has already begun to sink. By the time the great bridge building firm of Messrs. Alfred Thorne and Sons (Westminster, London) have completed their contract Warrington will be in possession of one of the finest bridges of, its kind in the country, a credit alike to the town, the builders, and the ,engineers—Messrs. Web- ster and Fitz Simons, the senior partner in which was an old Warringtonian, who took a special pride in this, one of his last pieces of designing work, but who was not spared to wit- ness its completion,- dying suddenly at his resi- dence near London a few months ago. Since August last Mr. Hodgson has had charge-of the building operations on behalf of the contrac- tors, succeeding Mr. Cobb.

TERRITORIALS' CURTAILED LEAVE

Warrington "Lads" Called Back By Mire.

The last detachment of the members of the ' 4th (Territorial) Battalion of the South Lanca- shires, who came up from the South, where they have been training, to spend a few days with their relatives and friends, had their leave cut short in dramatic fashion. They arrived in Warrington on Tuesday and they were due back yesterday, but on Wednesday morning each of them received a telegram ordering him .to report at local headquarters immediately and to be prepared to return that day. Hard on tfie heels of the telegraph messengers followed police- men, with instructions to find out the where- abouts of such men as might not be at their addresses when the telegrams were delivered and to acquaint them of the fact that they were wanted at once. The men were'soon collected, and they left Warrington in the early afternoon, marching to Bank Quay Station to the music of the Battalion Bugle Band, which was in the town on a recruiting mission. On Thursdaysnight about sixty men who have recently joined liitchener's Army and who had been quartered in Orford Barracks for some &Ts, left Warrington for a.town on the South coast, being accorded a' hearty send-off by a huge crowd of people. They marched to the station behind the Buffaloes' Baud—who played the National Anthems of the Allies, tts well as " Tipperary " and other popular airs—and the procession included a detachment of Crosfield's Boy Scouts, carrying torches.

FINE DRAMA AT THE ROYAL

COURT.

"The Ever Open Door" introduces us to George It. Sims at his best—to the Geprge Sims, who, discarding the virulent pen which he sometimes dips in the gall-pot for use for poli- tical purposes, can .always give us intense melo- drama without descending to the level of the more or less revolting mixture of gore and im- probability, which generally masquerades under that name; to the George R. Sims, who, in the intervals when he is not producing "Mustard and Cress" or inventing hair restorers, can give us—none better—homely, well-written pathos that never descends into the bathetical; to the George It. Sims who can construct for us a swift-moving play that grips from the rise to the fall of the curtain, and lacks nothing in the way of enthralling excitement, and yet has about it that which is not merely meretricious. George It. Sims is too good an artiste to be merely clap- trappy, and though he does not figure in the front rank of British dramatists, he has never- theless given much good, wholesome stuff to the British stage—stuff that makes a powerful appeal to that great section of the playgoing public who revel in bestowing a hearty hiss on heartless villainy and delight to shed a furtive tear over artless innocenciPheartletslf wronged. Time was when it was fashionable to sneer at homely all - British - made dramas of the Simsian order; to turn up,the super-supercilious nose at "unblushing and confessed" sentimen- tality, and to demand as dramatic pabulum in its place the play that made.light of promiscuous and more or less unsavoury relationships be- tween the members of' "the eternal triangle " 'the husband, the wife, and the other man. But of late we have shown signs of losing our zest for the play that, loosely named and somewhat misnamed problem, has dealt more or less super- ficially, albeit, from the point of view of mere dialecticism, cleverly with the riddles that daily present themselves for solution to the Judge of the Divorce and Admiralty Division of the High 'Court of King's Bench. And in losing our taste in that direction we have revivified our latent predilection for "the straight story "—the story of laughs and tears, the story that moves and holds and thrills. And it is just such a story as this that is told in "The Ever Open Door "—a

story of the heartless wronging of a good woman, of the suffierings and ultimate rescue from scenes of shame and crime of her hapless,name- less son; all ending, of course—for even a George R. Sims has to make this concession to the canons of melodramatic conventionality—" as happily as the proverbial marriage bell." Yes, "The Ever Open Door" is an excellent melodrama, and it is excellently played, and if you like drama and have not seen it, you should make a point of doing 80, whilst if you have seen it, and you are a discriminating patron, it goes without saying that you will want to see it again. The piece is being played twice nightly —it loses little in the necessarily condensed ver- sion—and every artiste in the cast is—well, an artiste.

Sparkling French Farce Next Week. The London Criterion Farce Company in " Oh ! I Say !"—the clever adaptation by Sidney Blow and Douglas Hoare of the famous French farce, " Un Nuit de Neces "—which enjoyed an unbroken run of over eight hundred nights in Paris and of over three hundred at the Criterion Theatre, London, is to be the attraction at the Royal Court Theatre next week. The piece will be presented twice nightly., and there will be a matinee on Monday.

STAR KINEMA'S FINE PROGRAMME

As usual, this week's splendid programmes at this popular house have left nothing to be de- sired. That shown during the first three days included: " Pathes' Animated Gazette," which, dealt with the effects of the war in different parts of the British Isles; "The King's Will" can instalment of the serial "Adventures of Kathlyn) was a long and breathlessly interest- ing picture-story, dealing with a search for gold buried in a region of danger! " Father " was a most moving Italian drama, which included a great fire scene; whilst "George Robey Turns Anarchist" proved to be a remarkably funny " bomb " comedy. The second programme will be shown for the last time this evening, and is well worth seeing. The chief film is a drama of the wild: "The Call of the North."

For Next Week.

"The gouge of Temperley," a fine filia based on Conan Doyle's famous story of the boxing ring, will form the big attraction at this popu- lar picture-house on the first three nights of the week, whilst "The Scales of Justice" will top the week-end bill.

"FOLLOW THE DRUM."

Recruiting Bands Visit To Warrington. ••••••1=••••••••• THE APPEAL FOR MORE MEN.

The clear clarion call of the bugle and the martial strains of a fine military band have this week been called into service with a view to stimulating recruiting in Warrington, visits having been paid by the Bugle Band of the Re- serve Battalion of the 4th South Lancashire Regiment and the Band formed by Lord Derby, which has been touring up and down the North "playing young men into the Army," as a local speaker not inaptly put it. The Bugle Band arrived last week-end, and on Saturday afternoon they played on the foot- ball ground at Wilderspool, following that by sounding a couple of tattoos at Market Gate in the evening, whilst on Sunday they accompanied the members of the Warrington Preparatory Training Corps to the Parish Church, and on Monday and Tuesday made many parades of the principal streets of the town. Their special object was to help in securing another three hun- dred recruits for the Territorial Battalion. Lord Derby's Band came on ,Tuesday, their main object being to fire the blood: of the young men of the town to the point of inducing them to join either of the two brigades of Field Artillery that are in course ,of formation in 'Veit Lancashire. Tbe band arrived at the Central Station on Tuesday, and, followed by a number of recruits from Orford Barficks, played their way through the streets to the Town Hall, where they formed up and gave an impromptu al fresco concert, among those who gathered to listen to them being the Mayor and Mayoress' (Alderman and Mrs. Peacock), Lieut.- Colonel D. E. Mocatta (chief recruiting officer for the Warrington district), Alderman Bennett, the Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls), and Dr. Peacocke.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR'S RECRUITING SPEECH. Men Who Might Go.

The Mayor addressed a few suitable words to the bandsmen and to the throng of listeners. He began by expressing thanks to Lord Derby for sending the band and .hoped that it would be successful in the object which it had in view. Great efforts had already been made in War- rington in the way of recruiting, and thanks were due to the young men who had already come forward. But further efforts and more men were still wanted. "Yes, still more men, and after those still more are required," said his Worship. "We know that many of the firms are deing Government work, but I am sure that even those firms can spare more men than they have already supplied. I want, particurarly, to appeal to those who have no dependents, or who are not bound by such close ties as others may be, to do their duty at This time. I don't care whether you are -working for any particular firm. You know whether you would hinder your firm by leaving it. If you are doing work which our Army and Navy need, you know in your OWD mind that you should not leave that work. But I cannot help thinking that there are hundreds more men in Warrington who are working for those firms who could be spared—and spared easily. I leave it to the consciences of those men, as to whether their place is not really among our gallant soldiers who are fighting our battles. To the shop assistants and clerks, whose places could for the time being easily be filled, I make a special appeal."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYORS HINT TO THE GIRLS.

What About_ Persuading Your Sweet-

heart to GO- ?

In the evening the band and procession of soldiers again paraded the town, and once more formed up in front of the Town Hall, and con- cluded the day's programme with a few spirited musical selectionq in front of the steps. A large crowd of interested Warringtonians gathered on the scene, and when the Mayor, accompanied by niftily ladies and gentlemen, appeared at the top of the steps to address them, he was accorded a 'hearty reception. His Worship said that Lord Derby had sent that band in order that Warrington might, if possible, improve upon what it had already done, and send some more recruits to fight their country's battles. He urged all the young ladies to try and prevail upon their sweethearts to do their duty. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150213_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
"A MOST HONOURABLE TITLE."

Sergt. C. S. Mclnnerney (pf the King's Liver- pool Regiment) then made a rousing recruiting speech. Every capable man, he said, was asked to become a soldier. Imagine the meaning of that word! It was a most honourable title, which would bring honour in future life wher- ever they might go. And they who, knowing what danger their country was in, did not take up arms to-day would suffer to-morrow. Did they realise that they might be called cowards? The soldiers ,who stood before him had given up comfortable lives and homes to serve their coup- try. Could not the others do the same? They depended upon the ladies, who could do a lot towards the. recruiting. Mothers and father, would be ashamed to have their sons called " cowards "! And for young nAtn It was a dis- grace to be seen ,in civilian clothes to-diky. (Loud applause.) In the Town Hall the Mayor afterwards pro- vided the visiting officers and bandsmen with refreshments.

TERRITORIAL TOPICS.

By a Valued Correspondent.

There has been plenty of activity lately in local Territorial circles, and if report speaks truly, there is likely to be even more in the near future, when our Warrington lads have a chanee to show the results of the strenuous training which they have undergone during the last few months. At the seaside the men of our reserve War- rington Battalion have reached a more inter- esting and more active stage of training than mere recruits' drill. Their very appearance bears witness to their military capacity. Bayonets by their sides, belts tight and work- manlike, uniforms well-fitting, and buttons bright, the 2/4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment present indeed a very smart 4i-0-is- ance as they march up the Promenade. One visiting Colonel remarked of them that he had been admiring the men's marching for several days without knowing to what unit they be- longed, and was not surprised when he heard who they were. Recruits come in steadily, but many more will be still needed .from Warringtm and district to meet the requirements of the War Office. One of the facts which has most impressed recruits has been the rapidity with which they are fitted with clothing by the Acting Quartermaster. Some twenty recruits early this week arrived one day about tea-time, and by 8 p.m. had been issued with full uniform and all necessities. This quick clothing was much appreciated by the men. Another feature of the Battalion has been the , magnificent playing of the Bugle Band, under Sergeant Lawless. Crowds attended to hear the band play "The Last Post" when they were recently on duty for the division. The musical talents of the men are being encour- aged by the enthusiastic Adjutant, with excel- lent results on the esprit de corps of all ranks. As Rudyard Kipling said at the Mansion House: "A band opens and unites the hearts of men more surely than any other appeal can." . The men continue out or working hours to enjoy the town to the full. Landlords of billets have lately promoted whist drives for the men in their lodgings, and apparently provided prizes for almost every man, .

It ha 4 been stated that the sight of our Itesulars in their trenches abroad would raise shame in the hearts of the most shameless shirker. It can be sold with equal truth that the sight of half a dozen Warrington lads eating their ample dinner in a seaside billet after four hours' work on the sands would raise envy in the heart of anyone who still hesitates to join our local Battalion.

NITINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Good Word for Publicans.

Apropos the controversy respeaing the earlier closing of public-houses in Warrington, I, am assured that in the town in which I began work as a journalist the .publicans have behaved quite magnanimously in relation to a compulsory closing order depriving them of two of the best hours of the day, 'briZA, 9 to H p.m.. There has been very little grumbling, and no attempt at evasion, although the restrictions are irksome, and hit the licensed victuallers—already heavily burdened by increased 'taxation and other charges—very hard indeed. I commend this spirit of loyalty to whom it may concern in this district.

How Wounded Spend Their Time. As I read of the work at Brookfield, the Lymm Red Cross Hospital, I thought of another insti- tution of the kind in a distant part of Lanca- shire which I visited during the Christmas season. Three of the soldiers, although bed- ridden, had full use of their hands, and spent most of their time knitting woollen comforters for comrades at the Front. I handled mittens and mufflers done by these excellent fellows, and, although I cannot pretend to be a judge of such matters, lady visitors admiringly testified to the first-class character of the work. Face.

Councillor Pemberton's allegation that a fellow member had "a cast-iron face "'perhaps did more credit to his powers of invention as a phrase-maker while you wait than to his good taste. Anyhow, he reminds me of a member of another Town Council whose meetings I at- tended many years ago. Said this councillor on one occasion: "We sometimes hear a man described as two-faced, but Alderman J— has faces all round him !"—far outstripping Bun- yan's "Mr. Facing-both-ways"! Printers' Errors. ,

In the course of tlae discussion, just referred to, Alderman Evans mentioned an amusing mis- print which set me rummaging among a big col- lection of the kind which have accumulated in my desk as the rears have passed. Among them is a copy of the dedication of a book to a states- man said to be "Worthy to rank with Cats and Brutes and the other heroes of Rome"; also an account of a first night at Covent Garden by the musical critic of an evening paper, who had written. "At the end of the performance the whole house rose and cheered," but the com- positor substituted "white horse" for "whole house."

"Pie" Examples. The 1905 edition of "Printers' Pie" had a chapter of such blunders which had occurred in the contributors' own books. Mr. F. G. Aflalo was made to say that he "lied over those tense moments again and again" when he meant "lived." Miss Braddon's "helpless orphan girl" became an organ girl." One of Mr. G. B. Burgin's heroines appeared with "a livid blush" instead of a vivid one, and gazed into "the dim vestas of the future." Mrs. Ada S. Ballin was made to use the phrase "as the sharks fly upward"; and the words "beds of ivory" became "beds of worry." Mary Stuart Boyd wrote, "She stuffed paper into the grate, and soon there was a merry blaze"; but the word " paper " was printedlas "papa." On another occasion one of her heroes burned his boots instead of his boats. In one of Miss Betham-Edwards' books it was remarked, with the aid of the printer, that "love is pickle." Mr. Bernard Capes' poem, "The llpas Tree" came out, he said, with a bad attack of hiccups, and was printed " Uaps " throughout. Mr. A. P. Graves quoted a matter-of-fact printer who amended Shakespeare by putting "Sermons in books, stones in the running brooks." Mr. Wil- liam Le Queux, when sub-editing on the "Globe," once discovered that instead of the House of Commons report ending with the words, "Left sitting," they were "Left spitting." For. myself, if I may presume to make the acknowledgment in this connection, I have con- stant reason to praise "The Examiner" staff for their loving kindness and tender mercies so far as this weekly column is concerned, and I feel certain that other contributors will be ready to rejoice with me on account of the remarkable freedom from sttch undesirable attentions as I have just indicated.

"Save Me From My Friends." It Was this well-worn tag that Councillor Roberts had in mind in retorting upon Council- lor Pemberton at last week's Town Council meeting. Of course, there have been many beautiful as well as caustic sentiments expressed about friendship. Some cynic has observed that a man is judged by his friends and is known by his friendships. While on the one band we have it that the man who makes no friendships deserves none, Pascal, on the other, suggests that if every one knew what every one said of another, there would not be four friends in the world. Shakespeare ennobled loyal friendship as the ideal of unselfishness, the highest attainable good, having in it a foretaste of the heavenly; but a Scottish proverb hath it, that "Friends are like fiddle-strings: they manna' be screwed ower tight." The word calls for careful defini- tion; distinction should be made between " friend " and "acquaintance." It has been said that while a nice roomy church might be required to hold a man's acquaintances, the pulpit would comfortably• accommodate his friends.

I think it was in "La Fontaine" that I met with a remark to the effect that even a prudent enemy is preferable to a friend without discre- tion. There is, for example, the kind friend in front at a first night performance—the tactless enthnsiast (as a brother scribe put it years ago) who claps his hands at every change of scene and dress, and who, when the curtain has already risen three times upon the close of an act, endeavours to raise it still again by leading a few loose and disconnected " reports " of ap- plause, which succeed only in sending a chill through the house, by reason of their melan- choly resemblance to the desultory firing of a retreating enemy. We all know something of this indiscreet—this deadly nuisance. At a wedding he is (to further quote briefly from the. same authority) the obtrusive person who makes everybody uncomfortable, and in a curious sense ashamed, by insisting that all concerned in the proceedings—the bride, bridegroom, the fathers and the mothers, the grandfathers and the grandmothers, the beat man and the bridesmaids —shall be separately serenaded, so to speak, with "For he's a jolly good fellow I" When the three cheers have been given, the kind friend in front is not satisfied until he gets a response, gener- ally feeble enough, to a fourth; whereat a sense of insufferable gloom pervades, for an eternal minute, the heart and soul of the company. The kind friend in front is by nature and of neces- sity ' unobservant, and the real victims of the freezing situation are the unfortunate people who, against their wills, have perpetrated the feeble fourth cheer. But my pen runs away with me. I may just conclude with the remin- tier that if "on some occasions one likes to be paved from one's friends" (as Councillor Roberts expressed it), "A friend in need is a friend indeed."

Ambition. "Oh, mother! how I wish I was an angel!" "Darling! what makes you say that?" "Oh, because then, mother, I could drop bombs on the Germans."

THE EARLY CLOSING OF PUBLIC-HOUSES.

Sir,—The publicans of the Daresbury district —from purely patriotic motives, of course— made a strong appeal at the Sessions on Tues- day for a reversal of the order of the magis- trates ,which reduces the hours for the sale of drink. I am told that this order was made in re- sponse to the. request of the Chief Constable of the County of Chester, who is responsible for and may be assumed to know quite as much (or possibly more) as to what is necessary to maintain good conduct as the gentleman does who appeared as the paid advocate of the trade. MAX.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE TRADES" RECORD FOR 1914.

Sir,—At the annual Licensing Sessions for the County Borough of Warrington, held last Friday at the Police Court, in the presence of fourteen magistrates, presided over by the Mayor, a review of the doings of "The Trade" was given by the Chief Constable in. his annual report, which covered the year 1914. I presume there would be a large attendance' of publicans and licensed victuallers, who would be interested to know what the report had to reveal. They were told that Warrington holds the unenviable position of being the second most drunken town out of eleven Northern towns, and also that 61 additional arrests for drunken- ness had been made, making a total of 615 for the year 1914. We are repeatedji- told by "The Trade" that the drunkard is their greatest enemy. If that is so, to go on increasing drunk- ards at this rate must be bad policy on their part. Is it not a fact that those who serve a person with intoxicating drink until he be- comes drunk, are equally liable to the penalty of the law? If this is so, and we believe it is, the greatest offenders have evaded their share of punishment. The report states that only one member of "The Trade" has been proceeded against.

I cannot set how the inhabitan, who., are working for the moral, social and economical improvement of the people of Warrington, can give their approval, much less their commenda- tion, to a trade that has so signally failed, as evinced by the Chief's report. In the great crisis through which Europe is passing, Russia has given us a splendid example. We are told that the wave of temperance already at work in Russia has had the most wonderful effect upon moral and economic Con- ditions. The savings banks have increased from £3,594,000 in 1913 to more than ..t8,882,000 in 1914. Since the prohibition of vodka, the people have developed greater energy in their work, and home life has become beautiful and more secure, so that women have gained happiness; it has created new springs of health, and throughout Russia crime has diminished to vanishing point.

His Excellency M. Bark, Russian Minister of Finance, declared that if he desired to revert to the conditions that previously obtained, the people wiculd refuse to allow this evil thing to come back.

In face of the fearful amount of drunkenness that has degraded many of the inhabitants of Warrington during the past year, and the devas- tating influence it must have had in many homes, and especially the cruel and indescrib- able suffering it has inflicted upon many of the innocent little children of our town, I ask, Why do not the authorities apply the powers given to them, and at once shorten the hours of sale of this dangerous,beverage, and thus endeavour to restore our town to a more sober condition? SOBRIETY.
