WILD BIRDS PROTECTION.

Challenge Shield For Lancashire Schools.

Sir,—Will you allow me a brief space in your column's to draw the attention of all who are interested in elementary education—especially the teachers—to the Bird and Tree Challenge Shield Competitions organised by the Royal Society for, the Protection of Birds? The scheme-iviis tentatively tried in Lanca- shire last year, with the result that one school in the county (Newburgh, near Wigan) gained-a first prize, and others lesser awards, in the open class, in which ten counties were represented. In view of the good work done and the interest aroused, the society offers a County Challenge Shield for competition this year, if the number of entries wgrrant it.

In all counties where it is working, the scheme has the full approval and sympathy of the Edu- cation Authorities. The Director of Education for Hampshire, where it was first instituted 12 years ago, said at a gathering of teachers: "I put in a plea for bird and tree work as a sub- ject that ought to be taken up, not only in rural schools, but in all schools, and one which should be' regarded earnestly and seriously." The Director for Warwickshire says: "No subject is more valuable to the children than this study, which rouses their interest and their powers of observation."

Through the kindness of Dr. Lloyd Snape, Director of Education for Lancashire, particu- lars of the competition are being sent, to schools within the County Council administrative area. I shall be glad to answer any inquiries and to forward all particulars to those of your readers who may care to hear more of the work which is promoting' bird protection and Nature study on the soundest and sanest lines.. (Miss) L. GARDINER. Secretary, Royal Society for the Protection of Birds. 23, Queen Anne's Gate, S.W., February 10', 1915.

BOVRIL RECORD SALES.

Presiding at the annual general ineeting of Bovril, Limited, the Earl of Erroll, K.T., C. 8., in moving the adoption of the report and ac- counts, stated that though the past year had been a difficult one, Bovril had come through with flying colours, and they were able to show both record sales (apart altogether from Government orders) and largely increased pro- fits.

The balance-sheet showed gross profit on trading A:304,186, against 1'284,229 for the pre- vious period. There was not a single German or alien enemy employed in their factories either at home or abroad.

The report and accounts were unanimously adopted. A resolution which was moved by a shareholder at the meeting voting a hundred guineas to the funds of the Red Cross Society was agreed to. land the proceedings closed with a vote of thauks to the chairman.

IN THE COURTS.

" TOMMIES" WIVES AND THE DRINK.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
Bent Collector Thinks They Have Too Much Money.

At the Borough Police Court, on Friday, before the Mayor (Alderman. P. Peacock) and other magistrates, Margaret Sullivan, of 14, Gandy-street, and Mary Burrows, summoned each other for assault, committed on February 6th. They were both bound over to keep the peace, and were ordered to pay the costs, 11s., between them.

A gentleman, who described himself as the rent collector for the houses in which the women live, stepped into the witness-box and

said that the cases appeared to be the outcome of a drunken quarrel, which took place between 1130 and 12 o'clock at night. The woman Bur- rows was in. -the llabit of 'lending -money to soldiers' wives., and Sullivan's husband was in the .army. He had seen inure drinking in the neighbourhood during the last three months or so than he had seen in twenty years. One of the women.would be;the subject of a further .preseeution on a chazge. of wilful "damage by smashing the other woman's door in. These soldiers' wives and others congregated in one another's houses for drinking bouts, and it ap- peared to him that the wives had foo much money altogether, or at any rate they did not seem to know how to make use of it when they got it.

YOUNG DOMESTIC'S THEFTS.

Girl Said to Have Robbed Two Mistresses.

At the Stockton Heath Police Court on Tues- day, Emily Monaghan, a young domestic ser- vant, of Sharples-street, Warrington, was charged with stealing a quantity of wearing apparel, valued at ot2 us. 6d., and 2s. 7d. in money, the property of her mistress, Emma Watson, Rose Villa, Thelwall New Road, Latch- ford Without, between December 30th of last year and January 20th of this.—She admitted the offence.

Supt. Owen told the Court that the prisoner was engaged by Mrs. Watson as a domestic servant on December 30th last. She went home to sleep each night. Soon after the girl started work the prosecutrix began to miss things, and suspicion fell upon the new servant. On the 20th of last month Mrs. Watson noticed some beads belonging to her hanging out of the girl's pocket, and spoke to the girl on the matter, whereupon she denied that she had anything belonging to her mistress. She was dismissed, and after she had left it was found that many articles of apparel were missing, and a warrant was taken out for her arrest. It was discovered that the girl had pledged a quantity of the stolen property at different pawnshops. In answer to the Bench, the prosecutrix said that she did not know anything about the girl prior to engaging her. Supt. Owen said the girl was living with her mother and a stepfather. Prior to going into service at Latchford Without she had a place in Warrington, and since her apprehension it had been discovered that she took from that place a cover from a perambulator. The Chairman (Mr. F. A. Frost), in announc- ing that the girl would be bound over and placed under the Probation Officer for eighteen months, told her that she had had a very narrow escape of being sent to prison, but the Bench did not like to make gaolbirds, especially of young girls, who still had the best part of their lives before them.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKING AN UNFIT HORSE.

A AVarrington Master and His Boy

Fined.

Before the magistrates at Stockton Heath Police Court on Tuesday, William Howard, a lad, of 6, Brierley-street, Warrington, and Moses Walker, his maker; of • Froghall Bridge, War- rington, were summoned for cruelty to a horse at Grappenhall, the former by working it whilst in an unfit state and the latter by causing it to be so worked. The evidence was to the effect that on Febru- ary 2nd a constable saw Howard in charge of a brown mare, attached to a lurry laden with mineral waters. Howard was trying to start the horse, but it refused to -go. The constable examined the animal and on the, left shoulder found an old, deep-seated wound about the size of a ss. piece. It was very sore and tender to the touch. When questioned, Howard said: "The boss asked me to take it out, and, knowing; it had had a sore shoulder for a month, I ex- amined it and the sore seemed to be dry. I thought when it was dressed that the horse could go on working." Inspector Dee, of the R.S.P.C.A., deposed to examining the animal—a large mare—and to finding it in very poor condition and suffering from an old wound on the near shoulder. When witness interviewed the master, he said, "I am very sorry. I am to blame. I should have in- spected the animal before it was sent out. The only thing I can do is to plead guilty." -- _

Howard- was fined ss. and costs, and his master, who did not appear, 10s. and costs.

MONDAY.

Drunk in Court—When ,a man appeared in Court to answer a summons brought at the instance of his wife it was evident that he was the worse for drink, and the police officer told the Court that he was quite unfit to plead.— The Bench remanded him in custody until this morning, and he was led to .the cells hiccough- ing something about wanting to "tell the magistrates all about it."

STOCKTON HEATH

An Alleged Obstruction.—The Main Roads Committee reported to the Cheshire County Council on Thursday that they had instructed the County Surveyor to use his best endeavours with the Manchester Ship Canal Company to meet the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee in the matter of the alleged obstruction caused by hoardings at the junction of Ellesmere-road and London-road.

Parochial Tea.—St. Thomas' annual parochial tea—held on Thursday evening in. the Church Room—was well attended. Tea finished, the Vicar (the Rev. Jas. Collins) presided over an excellent entertainment provided by a ladies' orchestra (conducted by Mrs. Willett), Mr. J. Rabbitt, the .Misses C. and S. Broadbent, Mr. W. Forshaw, Miss Broadbent, and Miss .Corney. A pupil of Miss Partington gave a pretty bal- loon sdance.

Wesleyan Choir 'Festival.—The annual choir festival in connection with the Wesleyan Church was held on 'Stinday, the preacher for the day being the Rev. W. A. Beckett, the late pastor of the church, and now of Crewe. In the afternoon a sacred cantata entitled "From Manger to Cross," was given by the choir, the solos, duets, and quartets being taken by mem- bers, assisted by Mr. Richard Tranter, of St. Helens. The chair was occupied by Mr. Frank Hickman, who congratulated the choir on their efficiency, and spoke of the value and effects of music in church worship. The choir rendered Special anthems at the "morning and evening services, Mr. Richard Tranter singing at the evening service "Why do the Nations" (Han- del) and "My Lord and I." The congregations were large, the church being crowded for the evening service.„.Great credit is due to Mr. F. E. Penketh (the choirmaster) and Mr. G. A. Whitfield (the organist) for the success of the day's services.

Highway Offences.—A batch of defendants ap- peared before the Stockton Heath magistrates on Tuesday to answer summonses for various offences under the Highways Acts.—Matthew Chorley, of 118, Liverpool-road, Warrington, was fined 2s. 6d. for not having had a horse and van under proper control in London-road.—Five shillings was the penalty imposed upon Frank Hughes, sen., of Brook Cottage, Walton Inferior, for failing to have a lighted lamp on his cart.— For failing to have adequate control of a gover- ness car at Walton Inferior on January 14th, Walter Mannion, of 13, Clifton-street: Warring- ton, was fined 2s. 6d.—James Hall, Kingsley, Frodsham, was fined ss. for failing to have suffi- cient men in charge of a heavy locomotive at Preston Brook on January 23rd, the evidence showing that there were only two men in charge of the locomotive, instead of three, and that they were both riding on the vehicle. De- fendant explained that he had engaged a third man to go with the engine, but after it nad gone part of the way, he turned back.—Erick Downing Naden, of 16, Kennedy-street, Man- chester, who failed to appear, was fined 10s. for not having a rear light on a motor car which was driving at Latchford Without on January 23rd.—Joseph Knowles, of Hatton House, Apple- ton, for whom Mr. T. S. Steel appeared, was summoned for permitting thirty-five sheep to stray in Tarporley-road, Whitley Inferior, on January 3rd, and set up the defence that a mis- take had been 'made with regard to the owner- ship of the sheep, and' the case was dismissed.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
A SOBER DISTRICT.

Only 21 "Drunks" in a Twelvemonth.

A falling off in the number of persons con- victed for drunkenness was reported at the annual licensing sessions for the Daresbury Petty Sessional Division of Cheshire, held at the Court House, Stockton Heath, on Tuesday morn- ing, Mr. F. A. Frost presiding, and the other justices on the Bench being Messrs. R. .C. Parr, Jas. Hepherd, C. Jackson Holmes, J. A. Ran some and T. Baxter.

The annual report was presented by Supt. Owen, who stated that there were twenty licensed victuallers in the division and three beer-sellers. There was one licensed house to every 547 inhabitants. No proceedings had to be taken 'against licensees during the year, nor were any summoned the previous year. Twenty- five persons were proceeded against for drunk- enness and 24 of them were convicted, as com- pared with totals of 29 and 28 respectively in the previous twelve months. Of the 25 prose- cuted during 1914, 24 were males. The previous year no females were prosecuted. Of the 24 males convicted; 13 were strangers to the dis- trict. The one woman prosecuted was a resi- dent. The whole of the 24 Men were classified as labourers. The Chairman announced that all the licences in the division would be renewed, and that the adjourned licensing sessions would be held on March 9th.

FINED FOR KICKING A HORSE.

Said Ile Would Do It "As Often as He Had a Mind."

"This is the first time I have ever been up at Court, and I hope it will be the last." said a youth named Harold Wibberley, of Taylor- street, Lower Walton. when summoned at Stock- ton Heath on Tuesday for cruelly kicking a horse at Appleton on January 27th. _

John 11.-T. Norris said that at about 4.45 on the afternoon of the day in .question he was out walking with a friend, when he saw the defend- ant in charge of a horse and cart. The horse became a'bit restive, and defendant took a run- ning kick at it, catching it in the stomach. When witness spoke to defendant he said: "Mind-your own business. You have nothing at all to do with it." He then ran at the 'Corse as if to kick it again, at the same time "coming out with some very nasty language." Witness asked him for his name. He refused to 'give it, and wit- ness said, "Very well; I'll take the number of your cart !" After witness had done so, defend- ant said he would kick the hose again, "as often as he had a mind."

A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed and paid

England, who spoke in the affirmative, said religion was never designed to make our pleasures less. Many people were under the impression that the Church condemns recreation, and rightly so,- especially when it took the Arm of bull-bliting and cock fign ng. Th,) Church, however, he t'ought, oould en- courage all forms of healthful recreation, such as reading, cricket, football. &c.—Mr. Plinston, spoke'iii the negative, and said recreation was not, the right way to attract and hold members of the Church. Employers tc-d•Ly made ample pro- vision for the recreation f.f their workpeopie, and he went on further to show that St. Paul, when converted, asked for work, not pleasure.— Speeches, for and against, were made by Mrs. Dakin. Mrs. E. Andrews, and Messrs. Duck- worth, Leech and Seed. M. E Lunt presided.

LYMM.

Football Hopes.—All being well, the Lymm Wednesday Football Team will play a match in each of the next three or four weeks—if recruit- ing or other unforeseen happenings do- not decree otherwise!

Injured Motorists.—We understand that Messrs. R. Wilkes (of Statham), .W. G. Jeeves, and H. S. Saville, senr.—all of wham were in- jured in a motoring accident last week—are making satisfactory progress.

Red Cross Patients.—The sixteen warriors recovering from their wounds at "Brookfield" Hospital are all progressing very nicely, and some expect to leave in about a 'week.

Home Defence. Corps' Affiliation.—The meet- ing which was to have been held this ,week to decide whether the Lyn= Home Defence Corps should affiliate with the Central Association of , Volunteer. Training Corps (as had been sug- gested) has been, unavoidably and indefinitely post voned.

Lymm at the County Council.—The name of Lymm figuNd frequently in the various reports of the Cheshire County Council dealt with at the quarterly meeting on Thursday. .It was announced that the Urban District Council had applied to the Roads Board for £4,570 for the purpose of reconstructing the Ditchfield Canal ,Bridge. Lymm had joined with Grappenhall and Thelwall in' suggesting that another con- ferente should be held on the subject of the inclusion of those places in Lancashire instead of Cheshire, but the Parliamentary Committee reported that they did not see that any advan- tage could be gained by holding another con- ference.

Death of an Old Resident.—The death took place on the morning of Tuesday of last week of an old Lymm resident—Mrs. Mary Newburn, widow of the late Mr. John Newburn, who was a well-known builder and a prominent Wes- leyan in the township. Mrs. Newburn, whose age was 74, had been suffering for. some time from bronchitis, and was under the care of Dr. Burrows. The funeral took place quietly at the LYmm Church last Friday, the rector (the Rey. G. B. Thurston) officiating. With the exception of a few years, during which she resided in Widnes, Mrs. Newburn had passed all her life in Lymm, living with Miss Machin at "The Grove." She was greatly respected by a large circle of her friends. She leaves a son, who lives with his wife and family at Birkenhead.

Presentation to Lady Organist.—At the con- clusion of the service at the Lymm Independent Methodist Church on Sunday evening, an inter- esting presentation took place. The recipient was Miss Maud Greenman, of Rose Cottages, Booths Hill, who is going to make her home in Scotland. Miss Greenman has for twelve years faithfully fulfilled the lutias of organist at the church, where she is held in iiigh esteem. On behalf of.her Many. frier:ids there, gr. INJ;liem Mort (who had been conducting the 5er.7,e..-.,5) handed to the retiring organist a beautiful handbag, expressing at the same time the Church's appreciation of her services, and assur- ing he that their best wishes went with her to her new home.—Miss Greenman comm.uni,tateu her sincere thanks to the kind donors.

LIBERALS' PROGRESS.

Year's Excellent Work Recorded at

Annual Meeting. MANY APPOINTMENTS.

Progress! This one word sums up the whole year's work reported at the annual meeting of the Lymm Liberal Association—held on Thurs- day evening in Hirst's Pavilion. Mr. J. Tilzey presided over a fair attendance, and many en- thusiastic speeches were delivered, emphasis being placed upon the political truce which the Lymm Liberals are at present observing so faith- fully. The usual large number of appointments were also satisfactorily and unanimously made.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
SECRETARY'S REPORT:

The Secretary (Mr. Reginald Barker) pre- sented the following report : • Th Association's year opened with a highly successful social and dance on the 11th of 'February in the Drill Hall, which provided an opportunity to introduce to the Lynam Polling; District Mr. J. H. Whitworth—who had suc- ceeded Mr. W. E. Thompson as chairman of the Executive of the Altriticham Division Liberal ,ts,ociation.

Registration Work.

"Your officials- and cormittee were urged during the early part of the year to concentrate their attention upon the necessary registration work in order to assist the central office as much as possible. This was in view of a possible ap- peal to• the country on the register to be corn- piied in the autumn. In July a rally of the hole division was held at the Belle Vue Gar- dens,- Manchester, in order that Mr. Edward Powell (the prospective Liberal candidate) should have an opportunity of meeting as many of his supporters as possible. An excellent tea Ts as provided, and the meeting was afterwards addressed by the Right Hon. Sir John Simon, K.C., M.P., Attorney-General; Sir Arthur A. Haworth, Bart., Mr. Edwin Powell, and other gentlemen. Lymm Polling District sent a strong contingent, which was conveyed by special train to and from the Gardens. Almost immediately after this most successful gathering the present disastrous war broke out, bringing the whole of the ordinary political and social life of the: country to a .standstill. A political truce was declared, and negotiations were put on loot be- tween the party agents which resulted in no opposition being made at the Revision Court to any voter who was serving with the Army or Navy.

Patriotic Liberals.

"it is especially gratifying that such a large proportion of the members of our Association have answered to the call of their King and country,and are serving in many capacities. From the 11 representatives from Booths Hill Ward alone no less than seven are now with the colours, and the proportion of ordinary members of the Association is almost as high. This should be a, conclusive answer to those who are never tired of suggesting that Liberals ate less loyal than other memb•!. a of the commanlv. Referring to legislation, as Liberals, we have every reason to congratulate ourselves upon the present position of those measures for which one party has fought so hard and so long. I have taken particular care to ascertain the present position of the three .great measures Is hien we all hold so dear, and which have been the cause of such bitter and fierce controversy in the past: (1) The Home Rule Act and the Established

Church (Wales) Act, passed through all their stages by the House of Commons in -the three consecutive sessions of 1912-13, 1913 and 1914, were not passed by the House of Lords, and were accordingly added to the Statute Book under the provisions of the Parliament Act. The Suspensory Act of 1914 provides, however, that no steps are to be taken to pot the Home Rule Act into operation or to disestablish the Welsh Church for twelve months, or, if the war has not by then ended, for such further time as may be fixed by His Majesty in Council. (2) The Plural Voting Bill was rejected by the House of • Lords, and its present position is a little obscure. But it is the earnest hope of all Liberals that this measure may not 'be sacrificed because of the present unfortunate war, as it will sweep away something like 525,000 votes which have been used to thwart and hamper the legitimate aspirations of constituencies all over the country.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
"Extraordinary Position."

"The present Government is in the extra- ordinary position -(for a Government) of being more popular at the end ot its natural term of office than at the commencement. (Hear, hear.) The manner in which it has handled the enor- mously complicated questions raised by the out- break of the war, has called forth the unstinted praise of all parties, especially in respect to the prudent measures taken to guard and maintain our financial stability—the evidence of which is shown in the unanimous and cordial support of all the largest bankers and financial houses in the world. We .can; I-colisider, look forward with every confidence to the daY-ivlien the pres- ent Government will render to the country an

account of its stewki-dship, and ask for the re- newal of our confidence. It is our duty and our obligation as Liberals, when that day comes, not to be found lacking in effort and deter- mination to give them that assistance which, on their record of work accomplished, they have a right to demand. (Hear, hear.) It is to their eternal credit that in all things they have stood for right against might, for freedom, for jus- tice, for a fuller and better life for the sweated worker, and for those who by their very circum- stances are unable to help themselves. As for the individual, so for the smaller and weaker nations they. have , not hesitated to draw the sword and.stand before the 'whole world, trust- ing only in .the, justke of their cause and the freedom loving instincts of their countrymen.

tive. Major England (of Thelwall) holds a similar position.

TWO CHAIRMEN THANKED.

Before dispersing, the meeting tendered its thanks to Mr. Tilzey for presiding over the meeting, and also passed a resolution of appre- ciation of Mr. J. D. B. Smith's services as their chairman during the past year. Mr. Smith had, it was stated, always done his best for the Association, and they wished him well in the new sphere to which he was presently to go. (Applause.)

Liberal Women's Meeting and Social.—The annual meeting and social of the Lymm Liberal women was held on Wednesday, January 27th. Excellent refreshments (superintended by Mrs. Edwards) were served, and afterwards Mrs. Dodds presided over a most enjoyable enter- tainment, which had been arranged by Mrs. Wyatt (chairman of the Association).

"Fight for the ,Faith !"

"I appeal therefore on this record 'to all Liberals in the district to shake off their sloth and come out to fight for their political faith. Not all of us are able to take our place in the Army or Navy, and it is therefore a greater obligation upon us to do what we can to support our country, its King and Government, in every possible way. Surely, active participation in political life—in however small a degree—is nobler than to sit at home criticising and grumbling !" (Applause.) The Chairman said he thought they would all agree that that was a splendid report,- and would endorse most heartily every word Mr. Barker had said.

This the meeting did by passing the report nem. eon., on the proposition of Messrs. D. Daniels and T. G. Bate.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
TREASURER'S REPORT.

The Treasurer next read his report, which was as follows :--- "The Association commenced the year with a satisfactory balance in hand, and in spite of the many extra calls upon the members gener- ally, it is pleasing to report that the subscrip- tions collected show an increase on the year 1912. On the other hand, a portion of the expense for the hire of rooms and cost of opening social for the session 1913 was unavoidably brought into the accounts under review. Notwithstanding this, we have sent our usual contribution to the Central Association, and after meeting all ex- penses have a satisfactory balance to carry for- ward. (Hear, hear.) As you are aware, the Association depends to some extent upon a large number of small subscriptions. Tinder the ex- tremely difficult circumstances in which so many of the less fortunate ones were placed by the European War, it was not considered reasonable to press for subscriptions in that direction, and a special appeal was made to those in more settled circumstances for additional help. I wish to place on record the unselfish manner in which this appeal was received. In only one instance was a subscription withheld for what may be called 'war excuses.'

===== 0002601_19150213_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
"An Unqualified Success."

"As you are awaie, a new method of collect- ing subscriptions was put into operation two years ago and has now had a fair trial. That it has been an unqualified success is shown by the continued increase in membership over that period, and if only every member will determine in the future to take his part in the work, the membership—and thereby the subscriptions— will show even more elasticity! The balance on social account is held in the bank pending the time when it may be considered desirable to expend it in the manner resolved on by the General Committee.

"I appeal to all Liberals resident in the Lymm polling district who are not subscribers to the Association to 'send a subscription, how- ever small, either to myself or a member of the committee. Although political iv,ork is for the time suspended, the standing charges for agent and revision work cannot be dispensed with, and Lymm being a scattered area is ditfi- cult and expensive to efficiently survey. This is more urgent when you consider that a general election may be forced upon us almost imme- diately, and although I do not personally expect an election until the end of the present war, yet I should fail in my duty to you if I did not call your attention to the desirability of accu- mulating as great a balance as possible in order to be prepared for all eventualities." (Ap- plause.) Mr. J. Tilzey proposed, and Mr. D. Daniels seconded, that "this very satisfac4ery report" be passed. This was agreed to unanimously.

A man must not only say he supported the Government and would vote for it, but he must work for it. Because, although they were having at present a political truce, there could be no doubt that the Government would be most vigorously assailed as to the support it had given to-soldiers' wives and on other war questions. The Tory party would not for a moment hesitate to again use such tactics as it had used in the case of the 'lnsurance Act. (Applause.) ===== 0002601_19150213_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
TRIBUTE TO LITIVIM HERO.

Captain's Reference to Soldier Who

Died to Save Him.

Captain J. D. Venables, of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, owes his life to a Lymm Tommy," who died in an effort to save him. This hero was Private Isaac Skelhorn, who was a member of the 2nd Welsh Fusiliers, and who lived at "The Rookery," Booth's Hill. His period of army :-,rvice was to have concluded in the middle of August last, but the outbreak of war caused him to be called up immediately as a Reservist.

The incident—for which, had he lived, Pri- vate Skelhorn would in all probability have received some recognition, thus bringing lasting honour to the village in which he had been born and had spent the whole of his life— took place during' an action at Gheluvelt, in Belgium, on Wednesday, November 11th. Cap- tain Venables had been severely wounded, and "very gallantly" (to use the captain's own words) Private Skelhorn ran to his assistance, but was shot dead while doing so. Through the Soldiers' and Sailors' Wives and Families' Association, however, Captain Ven- ables has traced the whereabouts of this brave soldier's mother, who has just received from him a most touching letter. In it the captain pays a very high tribute to the splendid con- duct of his would-be rescuer, and expresses the hope that he will shortly have the opportunity of describing the incident personally to Mrs. Skelhorn.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====

WARRINGTON HALVE THE POINTS.

InIPrOV3S a3wsbury Team Give Them a Close Game.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
MUD AND SOME MUDDLING.

kotes by "Centre."

Most of the prophets—at any rate, most of those of our acquaintance did—anticipated some- thing in the nature of a more or less easy win for Warrington on Saturday, but the result of the match with Dewsbury once again exempli- fied the cautious wisdom of the old saw, which abjures one never to prophesy until one -knows. Dewsbury proved something in the nature .of a surprise packet. Considering their , recent mediocre performances, and taking into account their relatively low position in the League table, Warrington should have found them easy to I°.anage. But they did not; indeed, they had 'heir work cut out to make a draw with them— a result which just about represented the run of- and the respective merits of the teams, as ,J4dged on the day's game. Of a verity did "ewsbury prove to be a tougher handful—a ,ainarter and more workmanlike combination— 'than rumour had reported; at least, it was either that, or they had .brightened up consid- erably in all departments since the critics last "weighed them up" in cold type. " The playing conditions on t Saturday were scarcely_ ideal. True, there was nothing wrong with - the weather, but the turf—well, it wasn't so much turf as mud, and before the game had been in. progress many minutes there was scarcely a player who had not been bespattered from head to foot, whilst one or two of them had become so o'er layered with the sticky sort as to be all but "unidentifiable."

kick at goal was a "wash out," as Mr. Atkins says.

DEWSBURY'S EQUALISER.

The equalising points came before the second half was very old. A mistake by the Warring- ton halves gave the-Dewsbury forwards a chance and they seized it. Jolley tried hard to stem The rush, but his effort was unavailing,.. and Abbishaw nipped across the line, Stringer plac- ing the scores equal with the goal kick—a good, hefty lift. The home team endeavoured all they knew to regain their lost lead, but chance § were few, the fates were unkind, and—well, the York- shiremen would not let them. In this half the visitors' front rank were at their best, and War- rington experienced considerable difficulty in holding them. They occasionally ventured on the combined game, and, when they did, they proved a toughish handful, and they kept the game in home-territory .most of the while. War- rington's defence was seund, however, and though severely tested many times, always man- aged to prevail, whilst occasionally the forwards got away and gave the visiting backs as good af.3 their own defenders were getting, though; un- happily for them, their very best efforts always fell just short of success. In the last few min- utes excitement ran pretty high, penalty shots following in turn, to both teams. The kicks, ...however, were in each instance indifferently taken, good chances of netting winning points thus going a-begging. When the final whistle sounded—with the score at 1 goal .1 try (five points) each—Warrington were busy defending and the Yorkshiremen were obviously the fresher of the two teams after their bard work on the heavy ground—work which, whilst it fre- quently lacked excitement, had nevertheless been fairly full of interest for the 4,000 specta- tors.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
A BATTLE OF THE FORWARDS.

Saturday's match resolved itself largely into a tussle between the forwards, the great bulk of the Work falling upon the front rankers, who, all things considered, put up quite a lively game, though it was never distinguished by anything in the shape of superlatNe brilliance. And there was not much to choose between the two Packs, for, whilst Warrington were undoubtedly the cleverer set when it came to loose work, Dews- bury were more robustious at close quarters, and Were certainly better in the matter of hooking. They were also the heavier, though Warrington Were the speedier, but the latter's advantage in that direction was seriously discounted by the slipperiness of the turf. One needs somewhat of the attributes of a Blondin to maintain himself erect through all the mazes of a Rugby game, on such treacherous ground as did duty for a Playing pitch on Saturday. There were moments —many of them very long-drawn-out "moments" —When. play degenerated into the scrappy and irregular order, though Warrington strove hard to introduce and to preserve something like sYstera into their play, abandoning largely the °Pportunist tactics which served them in such geed stead the previous week, and which, 'also, should have proved of great service on Satur- day. One advantage the visitors had rested in the superior trickiness of their forwards, who lvere "all there" and displayed, more stamina than did their opponents. being much the zresher of the two packs in the closing stages of the game.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AHEAD AT HALF-TIME.

At the end of the first half Warrington held advantage—a goal and a try to nil—though they had by no means had the best of the run of Play, which had waged backwards and forwards With something approaching to monotonous regularity, and had been, perhaps, slightly in favour of the visitors. The opening points came from a penalty, when the game was but a few minutes old. Price was guilty of a breach of rule, and a " free " being awarded to ,Warring- ton, Brookes took it and, with a well e j dg d kick, placed a goal from the quarter gag. uThis initial success temporarily proved a big " heartener " to the home team, and they put all they knew into their work, but the Dewsbury defenders showed good form and ready wit, and, time alter time, sent the attackers to the right- about. Then the forwards fra' Yorkshire began to feel their legs, so to speak, and they re- sponded with some brisk onslaughts, which only lacked finish to have been converted into com- plete successes. But, joy be, that finish was not forthcoming, and Jolley, and the merry quar- tet in front of him, experienced no great diffi- culty in extracting the sting from the attacks levelled against their citadel. Warrington's first and only try was the result of a smart pass- ing movement, instituted by Laws, who was aided by Brookes, Fearnley and Bradshaw, the last-named, amid much excitement, just suc- ceeding in getting over at the corner. Jolley's

NUTS & SLACK, WASHED

NUTS, BEANS & PEAS,

===== 0002601_19150213_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME TEAM DISAPPOINTING.

- - --- One has to confess that on the whole there was that which was disappointing about Warring- ton's play. Had they been up to their recent form they should have annexed both of the points in the League table, instead of having to divide them. Still it must be admitted that the going was hard and that Dewsbury were a tick- lish handful to manage, showing wonderful im- provement on their work of the last few weeks, and playing with a determination and 4. degree of skill that came as something of an'eye-opener in view of their previous reputation this season. In the earlier stages of the game Warrington's play was rather too slovenly. It maybe that they held their opponents too cheaply, or, on the other 'hand, perchance Micawber-like, they trustO to something turning up. Anyway, they gave little evidence of trying to make openings, or .of persistency in following up such chances as did present 'themselves. • On the other hand, 'the Yorkshiremen .were triers all the way through, and the result of the match was no more than their desserts.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
CONCERNING THE' PLAYERS.

Jolley was not up to his usual form, his kick- ing lacking its customary length and his field- ing being at times unaccountably ragged. The Warrington three-quarters also gave reigns of being somewhat "off-colour." At ties they were exasperatingly ineffective, whilst, speak- ing generally, they were uncertain. Brookes mulled frequently, and Bradshaw, apart from scoring an excellent try, was a disappointment, Tranter being far and away the best of the quar- tet, though Baker got in some useful defensive work. Laws hardly repeated his success of the previous week, but, considering the little sup- port he got. Young played a surprisingly good game. The forwards lacked cohesion, but on the whole they worked hard, and had they had a leader might have turned their efforts to better account. Fearnley and W. Cunliffe were, per- haps, the outstanding players among the sextet. Stringer played a good game for Dewsbury, at back, his fielding of the slippery ball being accomplished in fine style. Rhodes was the smartest man on the visitors'Aliree-qnnitei• line, though Farrar and Ward gave him _valuable assistance. Dyson proved a tricky half-back, and Abbishaw and Brown worked hard on the front rank.

During the interval the bugle band of the 4th Second Service Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment played its way round the pitch by way of a stimulus to recruiting, a reward of hearty hand-clapping being bestowed upon them by the crowd.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
ANGLING.

AVarrington Working Men's Association. N.A. CENTRE.

THE GOWY.

Half a dozen members made the journey to the Gowy last week-end, but the condition, of the weather on arrival put an end to any pros- pect of sport. Even if they had intentions of travelling any length of the stream, after view- ing the water from the road bridge, they were convinced that it would be in vain to try to find peaceful angling, and wiseli contented them- selves to spend a iew hours behind the mill, where the run was checked.

But results from the mill pool were nil. One member did venture out for half an hour, and was trying his luck in sailing down stream with his bait. A brace of trout were hooked and gently returned to the water. After a few hours, however, all wisely gave up; and returned to more comfortable quarters, and the 11b. .roach special, given at the Cooper's Arms for the first roach weighed in over 11b., has yet to be won. We hear that our Vice-President, Mr. Harry Worthington, of the Shrewsbury Arms, Mickle Trafford, has left the business, it, now, being in the hands of Mr. Ernest Hassall, who is a mem- ber of thern Warrington N.A. He has promised to give specials to the club, to be competed for during all the season, providing they are weiglied in at Mickle Thfford, and are caught -in the waters on the mill,side of the road bridge, and leased by the Northern Anglers. Members visiting the Gowy can have all par- ticulars on application from Mr. Hassall, and they will be published later in "The Examiner."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTICES.

The names of all officers elected for 1915 will be published next week, along with the altera- tion of rules and any items of interest passed at the general meeting this week. Members can no longer travel by their old cards. They can have their new ones by apply- ing at headquarters any Monday night between and 10 p.m. Cooper's Arms general meeting, Thursday, February 18th, at 8 p.m. A.S.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOUTY'S GLORY.

Last _week Mr. Peter Knight, replying to " N.A.A. Delegate," .whose letter appeared in • our issue of January 30, wrote :

The paragraph in the annual report of the Warrington Anglers' Association seems to have aroused quite a wail of despair in the attempt to reply to my criticism of the sport obtained in the Gowy during recent yeah. If "Delegate" had taxed his memory better he would have remembered that I have frequently referred to the paucity of specimens and catches obtained froth that fishery, and why he should defer his protest to this late day I cannot understand'. It has always been my endeavour to report faithfully the information put before me, and whoever the waters belonged to, or -whether it was public Water, I have always criticised with even justice all round, so' how. this can ,be con- strued into an injustice to any society in par- ticular I ,fail to see. I still maintain that the Gowy as a fishery is "deplorable" as compared with 10 or 20 years ago. - I should not have troubled to reply to " Dele- gate'S " letter if he had avoided the statement that neither I nor my Association have any con- nection with the river. We pay rent for a por- tion of the river, and have an pWner's'eonsent in writing to use another, and at the same time the best length of any.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—The tone of condescension which Mr. Knight adopts in his reply to my previous letter is very entertaining. He suggests that my memory has been at fault, and protests that his criticisms have always been fair. As to that I shall have some- thing to say, but I would first of all point out that whilst he is concerned to impress readers with the idea that his Association has fishing rights on a portion of the river, and has permis- sion to fish in another portion, he does not chal- lenge my contradiction of his statement that only certain fish have been caught (hiring the past season, on which statement his report ap- peared to be based. Your readers will, there- fore, draw their own conclusions. Now as to my memory. It is precisely because I and other members of my Association remem- ber his past criticisms, and because of his latest remarks, that we became what is known as "fed .up," and far from being in despair, I issued a vigorous protest, in the full knowledge that Mr. Knight was absolutely wrong in his statement. But perhaps Mr. Knight will carry his memory back a little,, when he may recollect that the writer and another angler who was interested, paid him a visit at his house and pro-tested to him personally in a friendly manner against his reports. Had he merely stated that the fishing in the Gowy is not as good as it was 20 years ago, then I could not have objected, as I do not know what it was like 20, years ago, and moreover do not think anglers are much concerned to-day with what it was like then, but when he spreads the impression, as his remarks certainly wozild, that the Gowy is not worth fishing in, whilst as a solid matter of fact, it is the best water in this district; he discourages membership of those societies and associations which. are interested, and creates, a feeling of bitterness, which i 8 bound-to- react against the interests of the sport itself, and might jeopardise that financial sup- port which he must be aware is obtained from sources outside the town.

I propose, with your permission, to publish at a later date, some details as to the last season's catches from the Gowy, which I am sure your angling readers will find interesting, and which will pr9ve conclusively that there is plenty of "glory" to be obtained from this river. Yours truly, N.A.A. DELEGATE.

" Bookie's Plea." This headline in last week's Police Court news brought to my mind, a story of a fond and credulous wife who confided to a chance. ac- quaintance that her husband was a great archeologist, and, on being belted to explain, went on: "Well, you know, an archwologist is one who takes especial interest in monuments and records of bygone ages, very ancient build- ings, and all that sort of thing. I find that my husband has been interesting himself in arche- ology for some time,..and I'm glad; it's a good thing for, arman to have a hobby, partieultirly a harmless one like this. A while ago, I discov- ered a bundle of tickets in one of his drawers, and I asked him about them. I picked up one which read '6 to 4 Bendigo,' or something like that—they were all after the same style—and I asked him what they meant. Oh, .my- dear,' said 'Lep. thefre rcliz of !ost raceg."!

RAPID SCORING AT LOPIGFORD.

Grappenhall Give Fairfield United a Good Game.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
MISSED PERIALTSES AT EitiLLSMS GREEN.

Notes by "Linesntan."

Saturday saw the first round of the Starkey Cup ties played, and out of the four games played a definite issue was arrived at in three instances, whilst the other game was a 'draw, and will have to be replayed to-day in Victoria Park.

In three of the ties it so happened that the same clubs were in oppOsition the previous week playing their League fixtures, and the result of these games had much influence on the Cup ties, and stirring games were witnessed.

The game between Hollins Green and War- rington Central ending in a draw will be the means of, these teams being in opposition to each other on four consecutive Saturday after- noons in League fixtures and Cup ties.

The Earlestown district was well served in the ties, by the • meeting of the Mission and the Newton_ Coligregationalists, which resulted in an easy will for the Mission, .the latter thus avenging their defeat in their last League fixture.

All the League clubs with the exception of Elmwood Congregationalists are taking part in this competition, Walton being the only outside club to enter.

Davenport in goal, Shaw having to deputise, but no blame could be attached to him for the shots that scored: Holden, at full-hack, Smart, at centre-forward, and Sherratt and Hands, for- wards, were the best of the United, whilst the Saints were best served by Lemons, Vernon, and O'Neil.

The home captain won the toss. Good com- bination by Lemon, Deakin, and Vernon car- ried the play to the home end, where Mason cleared and Sbelmardine. obtained possession, but ran the ball over the line. From the goal kick Challoner tested Sturgess, but the. latter was on the alert and cleared smartly. Eccles- ton and Sherratt, on the United right, were next dangerous, and the latter shot over the bar when . well placed. The players had now warmed to their game, and the ball travelled very quickly from end to.end.. The defenders on both sides were very prominent with good clear- ances, and the interval arrived without ttny score being registered. The second moiety opened in a sensational 'manner. From the kick-off Lemon and Deakin gained possession and made tracks for their 'opponents' citadel, and the former sent in an oblique shot from twenty yards' range and beat Sturgess all the way. From the centre kick the home lot retaliated in a lively manner, and Hughes made the scores equal. - Both teams, encouraged by their successes, raised the enthu- siasm of their supporters, responded to the call, and in a short time Vernon, beating Smart, Holden, and Mason, scored with a splendid shot, and the Saints regained the lead.' At the end of another five minutes the scores .were made equal again, Hands doing the needful. After this the game became more normal but evenly pnntested, and as the end was approaching the homesters, well served by their height and weight, again assumed the aggressive. -After the visitors' defence had .repelled several clan-. gerous onslaughts they conceded a 'corner to their opponents, and this being well placed Smart beaded the leather past Shaw and gave the United the lead. A few minutes later, from another corner kick, Hands scored the fourth goal. Result: Longford United, 4 goals; St. Barnabas, 2.

Longford United: Sturgess; Holden, Mason; Fearnley, Smart, Stevens; • Eceleston, Hands, Sherratt, Shelmardine, and Hughes: St. Barnabat : J. Shaw; Jefferson, E. Long; O'Neil,. P. Challinor, Ray; J., Long, Deakin, H. Challinor, Vernon, and Lemon. 1 Referee, M. J. Kite.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
STARKEY CUP COMPETITION.

FAIRFIELD UNITED v. GRAPPENHALL These teams met in the above competition on the United ground before a good number of spectators. Much interest was added to the game owing to the teams having met the pre- 's -ions week in a League match, when the G'rap- penhall team was not at full strength. Unfor- tunately on this occasion neither team was able to put out its best. The United were short of Woodhouse at full-back, and Gleave (who has been dubbed the mighty atom) at half-back, and the visitors were minus their extreme left- winger. Much credit is due to the Grappenball lads for the game they put up. For nearly three- parts of the game they fully extended their more experienced opponents. They were best served by Burrows, T. Ford, Hankey, Shake- shaft, J. Ford, and Thomas. For the United Potter, Scott, Hunter,, Lewis, Long, and Mill- ward distinguished -themselves. The visitors won the toss, and defended the Padgate-lane goal. Millwarcl set the ball roll- ing, and the play at once was very fast and keen, and after a visit to both ends bad been made, and several .free kicks for offside had been taken, the players became more settled, and the home goal was threatened, Potter fall- ing ,full length to clear a low ground shot. The corner kick was clearedkand after a brief spell in midfield the visitors returned to the attack. Potter made a splendid save from Thomas, and again later by putting the ball over the bar. At this period the visitors were having the best of the game, and although Shakeshaft and Ford were closely watched by Hunter, Ford got in a .smart shot, .which Potter distinguished himself by finely saving.. After • further midfield play and an occasional visit to the visitors' goal area the home goal was the most dangerous zone, and from a corner kick Shakeshaft scored the first point, for the Visitors after thirty-five minutes' play. The home team, nerved by this reverse, played more strenuously and forced the game to the visitors' end, but Ford, Peters, and Burrows were hard to beat, the latter making a splendid save from a high shot. However, Long succeeded in making the score equal. Interval, one goal each. The pace did not slacken upon resuming, and the players being urged by their supporters play was keen. The defence at both ends-was quickly tested, and after thirteen minutes' play Mill- ward received a centre from Long and put the home team in front. Following this the players contested every mph, and the visitors made strong efforts to get on equal terms, but Shake- shaft did not appear to be in his best form. .Hunter did not allow him much liberty, and the most danger' came from Thomas, who made several accurate centres and placed the home goal in difficulties. Owen on one occasion missed a very easy. opening, but shortly after- wards made amends by equalising the score. After a couple of fruitless corners the home de- fence relieved the situation, and the game was gradually forced to the visitors' end. T. Ford and Peters made several good -clearances, but the latter conceded a corner. This being cleared the ball was returned to the goalmouth, and ' Burrows saved well, and on the second attempt Millward headed through, registering the third goal. A few minutes after this Short sent in a long shot, which Burrows miskicked, and the ball went through the goal. This to a certain extent spoiled his previous good display. After several other attempts on the home goal, which was lucky to escape, time was called, the result being: Fairfield United, 4 goals; Grappenhall, 2 goals. Fairfield United : Potter; Scott, Bebbington ; Lewis, Hunter, Cole; Taylor, Woodhouse, Mill- ward, Short; and Long. Grappenhall : Burrows; T. Ford, Peters; Hankey, Parr, Hough; Thomas, j. Ford, Shake- shaft, Owen, and Rathbona. Referee, J. Smith.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWTON CONGREGATIONALISTS v. EARLESTOWN MISSION.

The, Congregationalists gave up their privi- lege at the draw and agreed to play on the Mission ground. The teams are keen rivals, and on the last occasion that the Congregationalists visited this ground they gathered both points and quite surprised their opponents by their display. As this Cup tie was regarded as the " rubber " game, and the clubs were well represented, both teams were anxious about winning. There was a good number of spec- tators.

The Mission won the toss and decided to play uphill. Rubotham set the ball rolling, and the players entered into the game with earnestness,, the ball travelling quickly from end to end, and the respective defences were on the alert. For the first half hour it was a ding-dong game, with little to choose between the teams, but as the interval was approaching the Mission gained the upper hand and strongly attacked the Con- gregationalists' goal, but the defence of Tithers, Lawrance, and Dagnall was splendid, and at half-time no score had been made.

The Mission, with the slope in their favour in the second half, soon made(their presence felt at the Congregationalists' goal, where Dag- nail brought off a brilliant clearance, but directly afterwards Rothwell scored , the first Mission goal. The Misision lads had much the best of the game. While the visitors failed to get past...the defence of. Winders, Eaton, and Wallas, further goals were scored: for: the Mis- sion by Wallas, Rodgers. (2), and F. Eaton, and at the call of time the result was: Earlestown St. John's Mission, 5 goals; Newton Congrega- tionalists, 0. _

Mission': 11. Wallas; Winders, A. Eaton; W. Aldridge, F. Eaton, Theobald ; W. Wallas, Rod- gers, Rothwell, Sherlock, and Plumpton. Newton Congregationalists: Dagnall; Low- rance, Tithers; Jones, Clark, Porter; Gleave, Dodds, Rubotham, Beresford, and Aitken. Referee, IL G. Brandreth.

ST. BARNABAS v. LONGFORD UNITED. This game between the above clubs, was originally drawn to be played on the Lovely- lane ground, but owing to the unfit state of the hitter it was agreed to play on ,the Horse and Jockey ground. The teams met on the same ground the previous week in a League fixture, when the United won, and it was expected the United would win the Cup tie, although the game would be more strenuously contested. KOLLINS GREEN v. WARRINGTON CENTRAL. -

The meeting of these two clubs on the former club's ground for the second week in succession gave the home team the .opportunity of retriev- ing themselves for the defeat they sustained the previous week. Owing to that result there was the largest crowd of the season present:. The home team were strengthened by the inclusion of the Brothers Blundell, Etehells and Taylor.

. . The -home team won the toss and Sankey started for the visitors. Hands and Mason worked their -way.. to, the home goal area, when Clarke relieved with a fine kick, and then good play by the home forwards was spoiled by Etchells getting off-side. Mellor at centre-half was prominent, and . passed neatly to his-for- wards. Taylor was 'preparing to shoot, when he was brought down, a penalty kick resulting. Mellor was entrusted with the kick, but owing to an infringement it had to be re-taken, and on this occasion was sent over the bar. The game continued to be very exciting and evenly contested, the forwards on both sides doing good work, only to be met by a stubborn defence. A free kick for the visitors against Mellor came to nought, and after further strenuous play the interval arrived without any score being made. The spectators were in a happy mood on enter- ing upon the second half, anticipating that their favourites'would have the best of the play, but the visitors showed determination, and made the first - advance. They rushed .on the home de- fence, and Harrison and Clarke were severely tested, but succeeded in relieving the pressure.. The visitors were soon hack again in the home quarters, owing to a well-timed pass to Hands, and from the latter's centre Blundell fisted away with the visiting forwards round him. The home half-backs were working very hard to clear away their opponents and Buckley sent out to W. Blundell. The latter was neatly robbed by Gilbert, and the ball was returned towards the home goal. A melee.ensuing in the goalmouth, Hands scored for the Central. Nettled by this reverse, the home team several times resorted to unfair methods,, and lost ground by it. Then Brown rushed in upon Cleworth when the latter was getting dangerous, and tripped him up. Mellor was entrusted with the ensuing kick and again missed. Some very keen and exciting play followed, and after even exchanges in midfield the home -forwards brought pressure to bear on the visitors' defence, and from a capital centre by Atkinson, Cleworth scored a splendid goal, and equalised the score. Both teams now made -strenuous efforts to gain the lead, but the .de- fence .on'both sides succeeded in stewing off the attacks. Result: Hollins Green, 1 goal; War- rington Central, 1 goal. Hollins Green: G. Blundell; Clarke and Har- rison; Buckley, Mellor and Atherton; W. Blun- dell, Taylor, Etchells, Cleworth and Atkinson. Central: W: Ashcroft; R. Ashcroft and ' Glover; Brown. Gilbert and. Williams; E. ',Mason;, Hands, Sankey, Farrell and T. Mason. Referee, B. Howell. DRAW FOR:THE SECOND ROUND-.

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

PADDINGTON v. WALTON

On Saturday the Paddington Club having no League fixture, arranged to play a friendly game on their ground with the Walton Club. Both turned out fairly representative elevens. The visitors, defending the village goal, made the first advance, which was repulsed, but the Wal- ton lads had the best of the exchanges, and Green registered the first goal. After a spell of even play the visitors again scored, • Andrews doing the 'needful. Beswick added a third for the visitors. . Half-time: Walton, 3 goals; Pad- dington, 1 goal. On resuming, the game was of an even charac- ter, and mostly confined to midfield, but eventu- ally the visitors got to close quarters and after several attempts had been cleared by the home defenders, Green again scored for the visitors. From the centre kick the home team on several ocasions became very dangerous, and eventually broke down the visitors' defence and reduced the arrears. Even play followed, but no further goals were added. Result: Walton, 4 goals; Paddington, 1 goal.

LEAGUE MEETING.

Grappenhall's Successful Protest Against Fairfield.

Last Tuesday a committee meeting of the War- rington and District League was held at the Lion Hotel, Mr. J. Wright presiding. The results of the Starkey Cup ties were con- firmed.

There was a protest by Grappenhall against their match with Fairfield United owing to the ground not being marked out, and also against the registration of two players After baring the evidence of both clubs, it was decided that the match be re-played, but the date for the re- play was deferred pending further inquiries re the registration of players. These to be brought forward at the committee meeting next week.

THE TEAM THAT LEFT THE GROUND. There was ,also a protest by Earlestown St. John's Mission against trieir League match with Orford White Star. It was decided that the referee's evidence with respect to the disputed decision be upheld.

It was also decided to recommend to the General Committee, that, owing to the game having been in progress according to the referee's report sixty-four minutes at the time the visiting players left the field', the result stand as a win for the White Star team, namely, 2--/ in their favour.

Re thevisiting players leaving the field of play before the expiry of time agreed to play will be dealt with by the Liverpool Association Committee.

Noise Needed Explaining. The celebrated soprano was in the middle of her solo when little Johnny said to his mother, referring to the conductor ot the orchestra: "Why does that man hit at the woman with his stick?"

"He is not hitting at her," replied his mother. "Keep quiet." "Well, then, what is she hollerin' so for?"

===== 0002601_19150213_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FLAIL OF THE HUN.

now the German has Afflicted the Farmer.

Mr John Ashton, an Englishman doing Red Cross work, gives a lurid account in the Christ- mas number of the "Breeders' gazette" (Chicago) of the doings of the Germans in the horse-breeding districts of Belgium. He says: "According to what I am told by M. Vanden Warden, it appears that the great Belgian stallion Indigene du Fosteau is no more. Indigene, although about fourteen years old, was recently purchased by a syndicate of breeders, consisting of Messrs. Firmin and Camille Losseau, Albert Hazard and M. Bury, at 10,000 dollars. When the Germans got to the farm of M. Bury, at Lobbes, where the stal- lion was being kept, the latter strenuously ob- jected to delivering the horse to the invaders. As a consequence the latter shot the Belgian farmer dead and took possession of the horse. The animal, finding himself in strange hands, began kicking at the Germans, as a result of which he met the same fate as his master.

"M. Vanden Warden, a very rich farmer and breeder, left everything he possessed in charge of his manager. His barns were full of grain, and he had also 100 stocks of newly-harvested wheat, oats, and barley. All of it has been threshed by the Germans and carried off. The chateau was broken into and all the valuable furniture, paintings, collections of china, among the finest in Belgium, have been plun- dered by the Germans. The last time he heard about his farm the Germans had taken sixteen head of horses, but he expects that they have returned for more since. This gentleman owned one of the largest beet-sugar factories in Bel- gium, and he is anxious to know what has become of his beet crop. Enough is known to warrant the assertion that Belgian breeding stock will be very scarce in the home of the breed for some time. "The Germans have also devastated the Ardennais country, both in Belgium and France. They also made great efforts to get in the Boulonnais region, but failed. Accord- ing to M. Vanden Warden, some valuable breeding stock has been sent to England for safely. The firm of Durot-Hazard got their stallions and mares away in this manner. Jules Vandenscheuren, well known to Ameri- can importers, lost sixty head out of eighty, according to report. Joseph Hardy, Thuilles, begged and prayed that the Germans would not take his finest brood mare among others, but his entreaties fell on deaf ears."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE TAWS OF DEATH.

Ring was now alone. He thought his time lia4 surely come. However, when in the very j3ws of death, he came upon a tribe of blacks. Henceforth his story reads like the books of adventure we read in boyhood. The blacks were hostile, and were debating on his fate, when he shot a couple of birds. The blacks, who had never seen a gun, were im- mensely impressed. To add to his prestige, he cured the chief of some ailment—for he had s•-nne medical skill. His fame was now estab- jibed. This white man who killed with a tire-sprouting stick, and who could make whole the sick was a decided acquisition, and he remained with the tribe as an honoured guest. But exposure and exhaustion had left their mark, and even the assiduous worship, which took expression in lavish gifts of food, could not counteract all he had suffered. Some months later a relief expedition, sent from Melbourne, found hint almost dying. He was nursed back to something like health, and led his rescuers to where Wills had died, and then to where Burke's bones still olutched a rusty -pistol. They were covered with a Union Jack and buried, while the tribe that had 'befriended King was given fifty pounds of sugar and immeasurable col- otired ribbon.

The saddest part of the whole story is that the expedition failed. It did not reach North Australia proper. Their route was out of the way, and quite unsuitable to the, overland wire which now runs hundreds of miles west- ward. Even the consolation of success was lacking. This by no means closes the list of heroic names. And what of the nameless ones who have braved the desert ? The gold prospector and the opal seeker, Whose going was unno- ticed, and whose return has too often never been: the stockman who has made his way even further out to find " feed " for his cat- tle. These are the men that have extended tho frontiers of Lsett:ement even more per- haps, and yet their names are lost. Where the explorer has passed by, these men stay— and so is a map made. Of them it is written: "Unsung, unhonoured have we gone A nation's thanks to earn; We have seen our .:iead pass one by one, And waited for our turn, With the warrigal and the crow to mourn, Our shroud the sa-ad drift wide, That our children's children might bet born lin.tere their faili?r's fatlin (Fed."

===== 0002601_19150213_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] EXPLORERS WHO DID NOT RETURN.

The history of Australian exploration re- mains to be written. Much of it indeed can never be written, because the chief actors have left nothing to tell of. They have com- pletely disappeared, and left not a trace be- hind. The crow and the dingo have buried them with grisly funeral rites and their names alone survive.

Nothing has had so much to do with the opening up of Australia as the English spirit of romance which !eads men into all manner of perils for their own sake. It is the lust for adventure as such which other races can never understand, and which drove on our pioneers. This was really the spirit in which Sturt started upon his expedition. Sturt was the first " Transcontinental " explorer. Hi- therto the pioneering had been done more or less near to the sea coast, and the Interior, the "Never Never " had been untouchl. Like the discoverers of America he took the setting sun as his goal, and steered by it. At last he got into a dry marsh. It was an awful place, a forest of enormous reeds growing in brittle earth into which their feet sank. It was almost impossible to make any headway, especially with the pack horses; while if they stood still, they were suffocated with the dust and heat, and lack of air. Also the place, like all dry marshes, was full of snakes.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
A TERRIBLE EXPERIENCE.

However, they pushed on, and at last came to a broad river, and down this they sailed light-heartedly. After their labours it was such a relief to glide smoothly with the stream, that no one apparently worried about the return. At last they reached the sea, in what is now South Australia,---and then came the journey back. This was one long nightmare. Early in the proceedings the boat struck a snag, and turned over, losing all their flour. Hence- forth they lived on bark and the yellow tree worms, which the blacks eat in extremities. On this diet they had to pull against the cur- rent day alter day under the blazing sun. It was a terrible experience. They strained to and fro at the oars as if in a dream. After a while every thought disappeared, except the everlasting necessity) of swinging up and down, up and down. Men dropped where they rowed, and lay till they became con- scious again. Then they resumed their toil. Some got sunstroke: one went mad. The journey cannot be described, because its heroes say that they remember very litt!e. But it can be imagined. And yet when they reached settled land most were alive to tell the tale.

Many men would have been satisfied with this. Not so Sturt. He still felt curious as to what lay beyond this river. So once more he set out. This time he made his way well into Central Australia, but not before he had lost some of his men from scurvy. After a while he had to confess himself beaten.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FATE OF KENNEDY

The story of Kennedy and Jacky-Jacky reads like a Sunday school tale. In 1848 Ken- ned,, with a dozen men set out to explore North Queensland. Even now this tropical district is not properly known, and the blacks are still among the most dangerous in Australia. Before long the party found that they could make no headway through the jungle. Every foot of 'their track had to be cleared, and even so spiny creepers tore their flesh and c7othes to ribbons. At last the party split up, Kennedy, Jacky-Jacky his servant, and three white men went forward. Before they had gone far, one of the whitea acci- dentally shot himself, and the other two were left with him. Kennedy, alone, save for his boy, pushed on into this frightful land. Of course he was foolish to do so. But still it is just such folly that if successful is hailed as heroism. Kennedy did not succeed, and the penalty he paid should silence all cavilling. One night he was stooping over the camp fire, when he fell, speared in the back, and in the ankle. Jacky-Jacky fired and drove the natives off for awhile, but Kennedy's wounds were mortal, and he knew it. He gave Jacky-Jacky his maps and reports, and told him to take them back. Then he died in his arms. Jacky dug a shallow grave for his master—firing a shot every few minutes to keep the blacks away—and taking off his own coat and shirt, buried Kennedy in them. Then he dived into the river, and swam un- der water, only coming up to breathe. How long he swam he does not know, because a native cannot count above .ten. However, some time later he was fouud half dead and taken to Brisbane where he told of Ken- nedy's fate. Of the other' eleven men of the party, only two ever returned, and one died immediately after.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
A TRAGIC EXPEDITION.

But even more tragic was the expedition of Burke and Wills, which cost many lives and £67,000, besides practically failing in ita object. This was the largest expedition ever sent out in Australia; the one most supported by the Government, the one which aroused the greatest interest, the one which had more than Australian significance—and the big- gest failure. During the fifties, it was pro- posed to connect Australia with England by cable, that is to say to link up North Aus- tralia with the wire that already ran to In- dia and Singapore. At the time nothing was known of Northern Australia, where such a cable would emerge from the sea, and a large prize was offered to anyone who would find a route from the north to the south. Funds were subscribed by the public and by the Government for the matter was one of international importance, and in 1860 Burke and Wills left Melbourne to try and do what no one had ever done before. They had a large and well equipped party, which in- cluded twenty-six Arabian camels and fin- duo driv.2rs. Horses had been found—bitter experience was the teacher—to be ill adapted to sandy country. So. amid the cheers of the populace, the expedition set out. Before long the leader recognised that it was too large to travel with any speed, so an advance guard Was formed. From this again, when they reached a point in Central Austra- lia, araother advance ;guard, was cut off to push on, while the rest. remained where they were for three months. After this "leave was given to presume death " in the legal phrase and they cored return home. So four men, Burke, Wiljs, King and Grey proceeded alone with six iamels. Henceforth the tale is like the nursery rhyme of the Ten little Nigger Boys. One by one they drop out and die. The camels are the first to go. They persist in getting bogged in salt marshes, and nothing became them like their death, for they provided fresh meat. Then Grey succumbs to exhaustion and bad food. But the, three press on, until after hardships, which can only be imagined, they reach salt water. Burke's diary telis of their joy at the thought that they were the first men to cross Australia. He at least and Wills were spared the pain of finding that it was not so. It was only the tidal waters of one of the huge rivers that run into the Gulf of Carpentaria, and not North/Australia proper.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ILL-STARRED RETURN.

Then came the ill-starred return. Progress was slow, and they on:y reached the rendez- vous four months after they had left it. A bitter disappointment awaited them. That yery morning; the depot had been abandoned. The commander, after waiting a month be- yond the time had come to the conclusion that to stay further was vain, and had left but three 1-4-airs before. It was a moment of despair. However, a smal: cach6 of provisions had bees and with th,s-. the trio set off on their lonz r)urny to settlei parts. When °the': food gave out, they lived on Nardoo, a small •••• bi roasted and pounded and made into a paste. But gather- ing it is slow work, and it took them all their time to get enough to eat. This could not continue, so Wills was sent off to try and find some friendly blacks. He failed in this, and returning to the place where he had left his companions, he found them gone. Now the thread of hope was snapped, and he lay down to die. The entries in his diary, hitherto religiously kept, be- come scanty and intermittent. His mind wan- ders for there are frequent irrelevancies and blunders.. A....—. a ag returned to the rendez- vous and found Wills dying. They left him what food they had, and went off on one last despairing journey to find succour. It sounds heartless, but what else could they do ? Wills gave Burke his watch, and a letter for his father, a.nd, said good-bye,. The others de-
