CONIFORTS FOR OUR SOLDIERS.

LIREiI U.XVOIIEN SOCIALS.

A Letter from a Delighted "Tommy."

HUGE HOWLEY PARTY.

Under the auspices of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association two more highly successful ward socials have this week been held in the Crosfield Memorial Hall for the purpose of raising funds for the purchase of further materials out of which the big sewing parties of members of the Association hope to make more large consignments of comforting articles of wear for our gallant soldiers serving on the Continent and at home. The first of the two socials took place on Monday evening, and was more especially set apart for Liberals from the Howley Ward, who rallied in such splendid numbers that the attendance constituted a record for the series, every table being occupied for the whist and domino games, to which the earlier part of the evening was devoted, whilst for the remainder of the night the spacious dancing floor of the beautiful hall was crowded with merry dancers, who to the music of Mr. Louis Hales waltzed and lancered to their hearts' content. During the evening the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), Alderman Tinnion, and Councillors Osborne and Wilkinson dropped in and joined the festive throng. The arrangements were superintended by the Ward Captain (Mrs. G. A. Pendlebury), who was assisted by the members of the committee of the War Sewing Fund and by the following auxiliaries of the ward: Mesdames Vose, Fox, Gatley, Ridgeway, Malkin, Wilson, Stringer, and A. Percival, whilst Mr. E. A. Sanders (Liberal agent) acted as M.C. in his usual cour- teous and efficient manner.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
MRS. CROSFIELD'S BUSY TIME.

During an interval in the dance programme Mrs. Osborne—who has played a leading part in the war sewing and knitting work under- taken by the Association, and has also had much to do with managing the highly successful series of socials in aid of the same—presented the prizes won at whist and dominoes, and, in doing so, she referred with pleasure to the fact that there was such a splendid attendance. She also read a letter from Mr. A. H. Crosfield, the prospective Liberal candidate, in which that gentleman said that he had been looking for- ward to meeting the Howley friends, but had been forced to change his plans in order to pay a visit to his brother, Captain Crosfield, then quartered with his regiment, the South Lama- shires, in the south of England. "Will you say," wrote Mr. Crosfield, "how much Mrs. Crosfield and I would have liked to have had the plea- sure of being present had circumstances per- mitted. Mrs. Crosfield is just now busier than ever with the additional work she has taken in hand with helping Mrs. Arthur Pearson, who is engaged in the big undertaking for finding employment for women who are short of it through the war." (Applause.) Mr. Crosfield concluded by offering "all good wishes for a successful evening "—wishes, by the way, that were most decidedly gratified.

Enjoying Themselves—But in a Good Cause. Many of the members of the sewing parties, said Mrs. Osborne, were working extremely hard, and if the enthusiasm shown up to the present, both with regard to the working parties and those socials, were continued—well, they would have to be on the look out for an even bigger hall. (Laughter and applause.) When the series of socials, of which that one formed part, was first mooted she met several friends and asked them to come, and they posi- tively held up their hands in horror at the idea of attending anything of the kind in the present time of war. She did not think that attitude was 'altogether justified, for, in holding socials for the purpose for which the Liberal Women's socials had been held, and would be held, she contended that far from showing any disrespect to their gallant fighting men—either those who had fallen in the glorious cause or those who were happily still spared to fight on—they were rendering them practical aid. (Hear, hear.) They were helping to provide them with things that they needed, and she felt that it was their duty to go on with their good work; it was their duty as women of England to do the best they could to provide the soldiers with comforts, and by gathering together as they had done that night that was exactly what they were doing, for they were providing the funds that in turn would provide many more of the articles so much needed. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150213_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
WENCHES TO TRENCHES.

Mrs. Osborne then read a letter that had been forwarded her by Messrs. Locker and Co., of the Church-street wireworks, first explaining that that firm had contributed very largely towards the scheme for providing "mitts," and through Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield to the Grand Duke Michael's scheme, over four hund- dred pairs of " mitts " having altogether been supplied by the employees of the firm. The letter was written by Private W. A. Nicholls, 3rd Troop, A Squadron, 3rd Dragoon Guards, attached to the British Expeditionary Force, and ran as follows:

"Just a line to thank you and. your lassies [the girl employees at the wireworks] for your very useful gift of mittens, which we received quite safely. I marsay that they arrived at a very opportune moment. We were pre- paring for the trenches, where we are going to-morrow (February 3rd). I am not allowed to say where we are. I should like also to thank, through you [Messrs. Locker], the kind-hearted,girl who put a packet of "Wood- bines" in -the pair which I received. In another pair there was a note and a packet of "Woodbines." The note was as follows: I hope the one who wears these " mitts "

Will knock the Germans into fits; And when you're lonely in the trenches Just think of Locker's wenches. It was received by Private Batchelor, 3rd Troop, A Squadron, 3rd Dragoon Guards, and he would like to write to the writer of the note.

Knocked the Prussian Guars into Fits. I am glad to be able to tell you that our regiment did knock the famous Prussian Guards into fits, and we will knock some more fits out of them before we have finished with them. . . There is very little sickness amongst our boys. They are all fit to knock spots off any Germans that may come along. I hope you will thank, on our behalf, those loyal, kind-hearted girls who have devoted so much time and labour on the mittens, which

we appreciate very much." (Applause.) Mrs. Osborne then handed over the prizes— with a gracious smile and a few words of con- gratulation for each recipient—after which, refreshments having been served, the dancing was resumed.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
sT. AUSTIN'S EXCELLENT II ILLY.

The social for St. Austin's Ward was held on Thursday night, and, like all its predecessors, was a big success, there being a—splendid at- tendance, which included the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Peacock), Alder- man and Mrs. Tinnion, Councillor and Mrs. Osborne, and Councillor Wilkinson. The prizes for the whist drive and domino tournament, which occupied the first part of the evening, were given by Alderman Tinnion and Councillor Osborne, and were presented to the winners by Mrs. Tinnion.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN TINNION'S TRIBUTE OF ADMIRATION.

Alderman TINNION, in the course of a brief speech, expressed his pleasure and satisfaction at the way in which the whist drives had been conducted and at the good results which had so far been obtained. He would like to pay his tribute of admiration for the way in which the Liberal ladies of Warrington had worked to make not only those social functions, but also the sewing and knitting parties, a success.. It inust be a great pleasure to all concerned to

see and hear of the success of their efforts. They all had their hearts in that matter of provid- ing comforts for the men • who were so nobly fighting for their country. The ladies who had plied busy fingers in the working parties and all those who had, by their attendance at these socials, helped on the good cause, could hava the satisfaction of knowing that they had been Of some service, that they had done a part in _help- ing to help the soldiers so bravely fighting the country's battles at the Front. From time to time the lady workers had got letters from the men who had received gifts from them, and the appreciation shown in those letters should prove a great encouragement .to those who were con- cerned in making the comforts. The war was not over by a long way yet; the soldiers still had a great task before them. There need be no doubt about their being able to perform that task—(hear, hear)—or about their. being able to do it thoroughly and well. (Applause.) And, whilst the men in the field were doing their part, those who had perforce to remain at home also had a duty to do. The Liberal women of Warrington were discharging that duty, but he was sure that all present would agree with him that, much as they had done, more still re- mained for them to do. Their quiet, steady work at home would, in some small degree, help to recompense, as well as to even further hearten, the brave fellows who had made such great sacrifices on behalf of their fellow coun- trymen and countrywomen. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150213_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
HELPERS AND ENTERTAINERS.

Bunch, assistec by the members of the committee of the Liberal Women's Associa- tion and the following ward auxiliaries: Mes- dames Bibby, Critchley, Hill, Pacey, Davies, Barlow; Taylor. "PRECIOUS PRESENTS."

Soldiers' Letters of Thanks to Liberal Women Workers.

Excellent progress has this week again mal•ked the work being done by the devoted hand of busy needlewomen and knitters at the Crosfield Memorial Hall in connection with the Grand Duke Michael's Gloves and Mittens Scheme and also the Women Liberals' Sewing Class for the soldiers.

Over a thousand more pairs of mittens have just been sent away to the Grand Duke, and the class—as well as all the lassies from the various works participating in the scheme—are continuing their labour of love as enthusiastic- ally as ever. GRATEFUL "MR. ATKINS."

Meanwhile the Women Liberals have been despatching still further large consignments of eagerly-awaited and much-needed garments to the "Tummies" at home and abroad, and by way of reward—all the reward they desire or expect—they have been the recipients of a number of grateful letters from our gallant lads in various parts of the continent and this country. Private J. Mahon, of the Shropshire Light Infantry, writing from a military camp in the South of England, says: "Very many thanks for your kind gift of a singlet and muffler, which came at a time when they were most needed. We had just got back to camp after waiting about for five hours in the rain and snow for Lord Kitchener to come and in- spect us. I am glad to hear that your associa- tion is interesting itself in such kind work, and I hope its efforts will be rewarded with every success. Prosperity to all!"

===== 0002601_19150213_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
CAME AT THE RIGHT TIME.

Driver W. Timmins, 198th A.S.C. attached to the 30th Lancers (Indian Expeditionary Force), addresses a letter of thanks from a camp in France. He describes the comforts which he has received as "precious presents," and says that they came in a time of special need—a time when one was naturally inclined to appre- ciate them more than ever before. Private Timmins' home address is 6, Wakefield-street, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
VETERINARY CORPS' APPEAL.

Mrs. Adelaide M. Moore, wife of General Moore and secretary for the Army Veterinary Corps Comfort Fund, has forwarded the fol- lowing letter to Mrs. Osborne: " Cartington, Alexandra-road, South Farnborough. I take this opportunity to again thank you for the kind contribution which I have received since the inauguration of the above fund. I can assure all those who have so generously sent gifts ,that their kindness has been immensely appreciated, and officers, N.C.O.'s, and men of the Veterinary Corps, now on active service, ask me to convey warm thanks to all who have provided the garments, comforts, tobacco, &c.— which were sent out, and have been gratefully received. "The conditions under which these men work are exceedingly trying, and officers have informed me that they consider it, in a marked degree, due to these comforts that their men have kept so fit and well and in such cheerful spirits. . . I know there is a loyal spirit of esprit de corps among the veterinary profes- sion and its friends, and it would be greatly appreciated if any friends would undertake to supply weekly papers, magazines, games, cards, &c., to the recreation shelters. I will with pleasure forward the address to any who may 80 desire to help."

The True Lion.

His Majesty's Inspector was testing the class in general knowledge.

"Now, lads," he said, gravely, "your teacher, I expect, has explained to you the meanings of most of the mottoes which apply to the months of the year. Thus: If February give much snow, a fine summer it doth foreshow,' and 'ln January if sun appear, March and April pay full dear.' But I wonder which of you can re- member what comes in like a lion and goes out like a lamb?" There was an awestruck silence for a few moments, and then a pale-looking boy said : "Please, sir, it's our landlord when he gets his arrears paid up !"

===== 0002601_19150213_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
Absent-Minded.

The absent-minded man didn't look up from his paper when the conductor came around. He just felt in his side pocket for his ticket and handed the ticket to the conductor. The ticket, however, was a theatre coupon. , "Here," spoke up the conductor, rather sharply, "this don't go. Come ion with the right one." The absent-minded man glanced down over the top of his paper at the coupon. "Well," lie snapped, "that's the coupon off the ticket the man gave me, and I purpose to occupy this seat right through all three acts. You can just—"

_ . Then he came out of the kg, and with a sheepish took- fished out the car ticket.

A LONELY WARRINGTON GRAVE

IN SURREY.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
The TOO-OLD-AT-FORTY TRAGEDY

A correspondent, referring to our recent refer- ence to the success of Mr. Edward Porritt, the Warrington journalist, in America, says : You recently made kindly references to a War- rington journalist in America. I was reminded of these on Saturday, when I paid my regular visit to the grave of my dear wife, of cherished memory, in the pretty little cemetery of Bandon Hill, Wallington, Surrey. For a long time I thought my beloved was the only Warring- tonian interred in this hillside burial ground, until one day I unexpectedly came across the isolated grave of a well-known . Warrington journalist of other days. It was the grave of Edward J. Burton, formerly of Latchford, and latterly of Streatham and Croydon. He was for many years London correspondent of a Lancashire paper, but was eventually displaced by the introduction of a new regime. He was born at the old Latchford Station House, which stood for some time on the banks of the Ship Canal, but I am not sure whether it still stands. He was only 55 when he died, and I venture to say that he died of a broken heart, through being unable, after diligent search, to find an- other London post. London is a heartless place for a man over 40. When I reached the late Mr. Burton's age I, too, was displaced, and I could many "a tale unfold" of a two years' attempt to place my feet once again on the rungs of the industrial ladder. I could also tell of tragedies that have resulted through men who have served one firm for 40 years being suddenly thrown amongst the great army of unemployed by the failure of the firm. or the introduction of modern methods. But this by the way. Whenever at Bandon Hill I have always made a pilgrimage to this lonely grave. To my sur- prise, on Saturday, I found it contained another tenant, as there had recently been interred the remains of the widow, who had survived her late husband several years.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====

THE PROCESS OF DEATH.

A Transition from the Imperfect to the

More Perfect:

SWEDENBORGIAN LECTURE.

"Where are the dead?" is the title of this year's series of lectures to be given in War- rington under the auspices of the New Church. The first of the series was given in the Masonic Hall, Bold-street, on Tuesday evening by the Rev. J. Ashby, of Southport, there being a good attendance.

The series is intended to cover various aspects of the question asked in the title, and that on Tuesday night dealt with "The 'Process of Death." Death and its surroundings, said the lecturer, constituted a legitimate and rational matter for inquiry, though it was one of those things which they looked forward to without any sort of anticipation, or without, in the main, any real and earnest anticipation. They frequently said to one another, apropos of the death of one they had known, "Well he (or she) is better off in. a land where things are more to one's satisfac- tion," but he was afraid that when they uttered that commonplace they did not often believe what they said. THE LAST CHANGE WHICH MAN MAKES.

They had not yet realised that the last change which man made, so far as the human eye could see, was a desirable one; indeed, was it not a fact that most of them tried to put away from their minds all thought of death? There was a natural wish to avoid the last change; a dis- inclination to talk about it and to consider it. Yet, however little they might like it, death was pressed upon their attention daily, and the doctrine of the New Church was that, far from 4hrinking before it, they should endeavour to understand it. Death was a change that could not be warded off, and therefore some people said it was a very troublesome thing, and they called it "the King of Terrors." Two views were commonly submitted on this question of death, the one being "Oh, it will come, and the best thing is to forget it, though when it does come we shall pass through it as well as most people," and the other—and this was sub- mitted on the part of the people who had good sense and wisdom—that, whilst realising its inevitability, people should get to understand it, for the mysterious darkness which sur- rounded it in the minds of most people brought with it a sense of fearful foreboding, whereas, if ignorance on the subject gave place to know- ledge. death could be faced with a certain con- tentment and repose.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
A COMMON MISTAKE.

Therefore they should try to understand what the process of death really was. It was quite a mistake to assume, as most people were apt to—how often one heard it said of a person who had passed from sight, "Now he knows all about it !"—that one understood death by the mere fact of having passed through it, and the fact that death so often came in horrible forms made simple people doubt whether, after all, there was a good Providence ruling the affairs of human life; made people say, as so many were saying, that the present war, in which there would be thousands of deaths, would make more infidels than anything else. However, he could not see that, for, to start with, war did not come from God, but was brought about entirely by the disposition of man's mind and what were called the exigencies of life. Consequently the frightful forma of death incidental to war ought to have been avoided. AFTER TEMPORAL DEATH.

No; to understand what death -really was they must discriminate between the death of the body and the death of the soul. The dead, the speaker went on to postulate, were still in existence, although the living might not be sensible of them. Though they had passed from this world they still possessed all the faculties of human beings, not in the body of material narticles—they had left that behind—but in the body which belonged to the spiritual world. There were two parts in man—the spiritual body and the natural body—and the two worked more or less harmoniously together up to a cer- tain point; that point being death. Death was a mere change—a separation of the interior man from the gross environment in which he com- menced his existence. The dead rose in the next world perfect—conscious of themselves and of the things around them. Just as the morning came after the night, so after death came the day of heaven. The passing from this world to the world beyond was like the passing from early youth into full possession of the powers of manhood:

PREPARATORY TRAINING CORPS

FAIRFIELD AND HOWLEY COMPANY.

At the Municipal range on Tuesday evening, the company firing was very interesting. In a contest for the best five shots out of 8 rounds, the following scores were made Out of the 50 possible: S. Oddy 44, A. Bailie 44, 11. Gaunt 42, T. H. Newton 42, E. Mann 42, E. Balmer 40, A. Bayley (Latchford) 38 (aggregate 55), H. Taylor (St. John's) 37 (aggregate) 53, S. Williams 37, A. Roylance 33, I. Bowen 33. J. Langhorn 33, G. Spencer 33, G. Beckett 30, L. Cross 30, W. Nicholls 30, and W. Paskin 30.

The first six had a majority of six points over the next nine in the aggregates, as follows : First Six: S. Oddy /64, A. Bailie 61, H. Gaunt 57, T. H. Newton 57, E. Balmer 56, E. Mann 54; total 349. Next Nine: S. Williams 47, A. Roy- lance 43, I. Bowen 43, J. Langhorn 37, G. Spencer 37, G. Beckett 37, L. Cross 33, W. Nicholls 33, W. Paskin 33; total 34.3. The aggregate for a team of ten, 519, is a 5 per cent improvement on previous efforts. An inter-company shoot with the Town Hall and St. Austin's Coppany is arranged for Mon- day evening at the Legh-street range, and on the following week the friendly rivalry with Whitecross and Bewsey will be resumed.

AN ARREST AT MIDNIGHT.

At Tuesday's Police Court, Councillor IT. Roberts presiding, Private Alfred Boardman was charged with having been an absentee from the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment (stationed in the south) since Saturday.— Acting-Sergeant Armstrong described how he had stopped and questioned Boardman at mid- night in Lovely-lane, the offence being admitted. —Prisoner was ordered to be handed over tit a military escort.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
FIVE ABSENTEES RETURN.

"Return to your centre with the escort." Such was the order of the Bench at Tuesday's Police Court, when William Thomas, Ernest James Kinsey, Charles Edwin Williams, John Parry, and James Naylor—all of the 4th Bat- talion South Lancashire Regiment were charged with being absentees between midnight and 1230 that morning.—All five admitted being absentees, and the Chief Constable stated that four of them had been arrested before on the same charge.—Acting.Serg,eants Armstrong and Howard spoke to the detection of tho defaulters, who returned to their centres later in the day.

"BROUGHT BACK FROM BOULOGNE," 'A King's bad bargain" was the description which Superintendent Williamson applied to Private Michael John Connor, when that indi- vidual was charged at Monday's Police Court with having been a deserter from the 4th Bat- talion South Lancashire Regiment (at present stationed near the south coast) since New Year's Day.—Prisoner pleaded guilty.—P.C. Taylor stated that he heard Connor was a de- serter and challenged him in Marsh House. lane. Connor replied that he was a sergeant "down here recruiting."—Prisoner said he had been a soldier for over twenty years. He ap- plied three times to go to the front, but they would not let him go. He was determined to go, and reached Boulogne; but he was brought back.—Superintendent Williamson stated that Connor had deserted while he was awaiting trial by court-martial. He got rid of his kit and joined the King's (Liverpool) Regiment, but deserted from Seaforth Barracks. He after- wards joined the "Pals" Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment at St. Helens, but deserted from that on Friday. The uniform be was now, wearing was that of the South Lanca- shires.—Prisoner was ordered to be handed over to an escort.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
SCALED BARRACKS WALL.

At the Police Court on Monday, Alfred Lloyd, a private in the South Lancashire Regi- ment, admitted having been a deserter from Orford Barracks since Saturday.—P.C. Richards Lid that on Saturday night he saw Lloyd in Bewsey-road, and noticed his military appear- ance. He stopped him, and then saw that he was wearing an army shirt. Lloyd said he had got over the wall of the barracks.—Superin- tendent Williamson said that prisoner was awaiting a court-martial when he deserted.— Prisoner was handed over to a ;nilitary escort.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
"ONLY A JOKE."

Private Henry Spencer Bailey, Of the 4th Bat- talion South Lancashire Regiment (Terri- torials), was charged at the Borough Court on Monday with having been an absentee since February sth.—P.C. Yates said he had written a figure "1" in front of the "5" on his pass; making the date February 15th.—Bailey ex- plained that it had only been done as a joke. They could gee that, as the figure "1 "was only written in pencil. He had meant to go back to his regiment. —Superintendent Williamson: They're always going back by the next train when they're arrested! An escort is coming for this man to-day.----Prisoner was ordered to be handed over to it.

===== 0002601_19150213_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLY MORNING CAPTURE.

At Monday's Police Court Private Jackson, of the 6th Battalion King's Own Scottish Bor- derers, pleaded not guilty to a charge of being an absentee since the previous day.—P.C. Arm- strong stated that just after five o'clock that morning he saw Jackson in Sankey-street and asked to see his pass, whereupon he admitted being an absentee.—Superintendent Williamson said Jackson was on his way to Wigan when caught.—He was remandedlo await an escort.

KITCHENER'S ARMY. ARTILLERY BRIGADES TO BE FORMED. RECRUITS WANTED HERE AND NOW. LORD DERBY has been requested by Lord Kitchener to raise in West Lancashire, two Brigades of Field Artillery, with their attendant Ammu- nition Column, and a Battery of Heavy Artillery. PAY AND ALLOWANCE AS IN REGULAR ARMY. Smart men, especially those who have previous Artillery experience—or who can ride are particularly invited to come forward. Care will be taken to, as far as possible, keep friends together. Come For ward and form 'Comrades' Artillery Brigades. The men will be billeted for the present at Lytham for training. Recruits will be taken at COOPER'S BUILDINGS, CHURCH ST., and 20, OLD HAYMARKET, LIVERPOOL, or at any Local Recruiting Office, any day, from 9 o'clocic onwards, until further notice.

TO-DAY'S BIG MATCH. Warrin g ton to Visit .Broughton. Warrington are due to play their return fix- ture with Broughton, at Broughton, to-day. The team, as selected on Wednesday night, shows three changes from that which did duty against Dewsbury on Saturday. Ffigham, who saw ser- vice with the "A" team in the early part of the season, has been selected to Play centre in place of Brookes, the Warrington captain, ancl_Dain- teVi re-appears in the half-back line, displacing Laws. In the forward line, Critchley supplants Mather. The full team as selected will be:— Jolley. Bradshaw, Tranter, Ffigham and Baker- Dainteth and Young. Com, Green; T. Cunliffe, W. Cunliffe, Critcbley and Yearnley. Warrington and Broughton last met on Sep- tember 12th, at Wilderspool, when the visiting team were victorious by 8 points to 5. INTERMEDIATE RUGBY LEAGUE. LAST SATURDAY'S RESULTS. Beamont United 0.8., 4; St. James' 0.8.. 2. Bolton Council 0.8., 7; Beamont Ath. TO-DAY'S MATCHES. At Wilderspool. Kick-off 2.30. Arpley-street v. Sacred Heart; referee, Mr. W. Bibby. St. James' Juniors v. St. Mary's; Mr. W. Cooper. At Victoria Park. Beamont Ath. v. St. Ann's; Mr. J. Bearsdell. THE CUP. Warringlon Drawn at Home. The draw for the first round of the Northern Union Challenge Cup Com Petition, to be played on February 27th, was made in Manchester on Tuesday night. . Warrington were drawn against Leeds, the match to be played at Wilderspool. The teams have met twice previously this season. The first encounter, on December 28th, ended dis- astrously for Warrington, Leeds putting on 35 points and .the Wilderspool lads being unable to make any response. A few days later, however; they managed to get some of their own back, making a draw of 3 points all with the York- shiremen, who, on that occasion, were the visit- ors.
